First Price Auction is 1 − 1 / e 2 1-1/e^{2} Efficient
Yaonan Jin, Pinyan Lu

Abstract
We prove that the PoA of First Price Auctions is 1 − 1 / e 2 ≈ 0.8647 1-1/e^{2}\approx 0.8647 , closing the gap between the best known bounds [ 0.7430 , 0.8689 ] [0.7430,\,0.8689] .



1
Introduction

In 1961, Vickrey
[
Vic61
]
, a Nobel laureate in Economics,
initiated auction theory. At the center of his work was the solution concept of Nash equilibrium
[
Nas50
]
for auctions as non-cooperative games. This game-theoretical approach has shaped modern auction theory and has a tremendous influence also on other areas in mathematical economics
[
HR04
]
. In particular, Vickrey systematically investigated the first-price auction (or its equivalent, the Dutch auction),
the most common auction format in real business.

In the first-price auction, the auctioneer (seller) sells an indivisible item to
n
potential bidders (buyers).
The rule is as simple as it can be: All bidders simultaneously submit bids to the auctioneer (each of which is unknown to the other bidders); the highest bidder wins the item, paying his/her own bid.
Simple as the rule is, the bidders’ optimal bidding strategies can be sophisticated.
From one bidder’s own perspective, a higher bid means a higher payment on winning but also a better chance of winning.
Accordingly, this bidder’s optimal bidding strategy depends on the competitive environment, which in turn is determined by the other bidders’ bidding strategies. This situation is perfectly a non-cooperative game and the standard solution concept is Bayesian Nash equilibrium.
To get a better sense, let us show a warm-up example from Vickrey’s original work.

Example 1

(
[
Vic61
]
)
.

Consider two bidders: Alice and Bob have (independent) uniform random values
v_{1},\,v_{2}\sim U[0,\,1]
and respectively bid
b_{1}=\frac{v_{1}}{2}
and
b_{2}=\frac{v_{2}}{2}
. The value distributions and bidding strategies determine the (independent) bid distributions of bidders. In this example, they are (independent) uniform random bids
b_{1},\,b_{2}\sim U[0,\,\frac{1}{2}]
, whose CDFs are
B_{1}(b)=B_{2}(b)=\min(2b,\,1)
.
By bidding
b\geq 0
, Alice wins with probability
\min(2b,\,1)
and gains a utility
(v_{1}-b)
conditioned on winning. Her expected utility is
(v_{1}-b)\cdot\min(2b,\,1)
, which is maximized when
b=\frac{v_{1}}{2}
.
Thus, her current strategy
b_{1}=\frac{v_{1}}{2}
is optimal. By symmetry, Bob’s current strategy
b_{2}=\frac{v_{2}}{2}
is also optimal.
In sum, the above strategy profile
(b_{1},\,b_{2})=(\frac{v_{1}}{2},\,\frac{v_{2}}{2})
is an equilibrium, in a sense that no bidder can gain a better utility by deviating from his/her current strategy.

For clarity, let us formalize the model. Each bidder
i\in[n]
independently draws his/her value from a distribution
v_{i}\sim V_{i}
.
Only with this knowledge and depending on his/her own strategy
s_{i}
, bidder
i
submits a possibly random bid
b_{i}=s_{i}(v_{i})
.
Then over the randomness of the other bidders’ values
\boldsymbol{v}_{-i}=(v_{k})_{k\neq i}
and strategies
\boldsymbol{s}_{-i}=\{s_{k}\}_{k\neq i}
, bidder
i
wins with probability
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b_{i})\in[0,\,1]
and gains an expected utility
u_{i}(v_{i},\,b_{i})=(v_{i}-b_{i})\cdot\mathrm{x}_{i}(b_{i})
.

Definition 1.1

(Equilibria)
.

A strategy profile
\boldsymbol{s}=\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
is a
Bayesian Nash Equilibrium
when: For each bidder
i\in[n]
and any possible value
v\in\mathrm{supp}(V_{i})
, the current strategy
s_{i}(v)
is optimal, namely
\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{s_{i}}\big[u_{i}(v,\,s_{i}(v))\big]\geq u_{i}(v,\,b)
for any deviation bid
b\geq 0
.

Example

1
is special in that bidders have identically distributed values. This
symmetric
setting is well understood: The first-price auction has a
unique
Bayesian Nash equilibrium
[
CH13
]
, which is
fully efficient
– The bidder with the highest value always wins the item.
Instead, the current trend focuses more on the
asymmetric
setting, where bidders’ values are distinguished by their distributions.
Again, let us get a better sense through two concrete examples.

Example 2
.

Consider two bidders: Alice has a fixed value
v_{1}\equiv 2
and always bids
s_{1}(v_{1})\equiv 1
. Bob has a uniform random value
v_{2}\sim U[0,\,1]
and
truthfully
bids his value
s_{2}(v_{2})=v_{2}
, namely the distribution
B_{2}(b)=\min(b,\,1)
.
By bidding
b\geq 0
, Alice gains an expected utility
(v_{1}-b)\cdot\min(b,\,1)
, for which her current strategy
s_{1}(v_{1})\equiv 1
is optimal.
Bob cannot gain a positive utility because his value
v_{2}\sim U[0,\,1]
is at most Alice’s bid
s_{1}(v_{1})\equiv 1
; thus his current strategy
s_{2}(v_{2})=v_{2}
is also optimal.
In sum, this strategy profile
(s_{1},s_{2})
is an equilibrium.

Unlike the symmetric setting, this auction game has
multiple
equilibria.
E.g., it is easy to verify that the same strategy
s_{1}(v_{1})\equiv 1
for Alice and a different strategy
\widetilde{s}_{2}(v_{2})=\max(\frac{2v_{2}-1}{v_{2}},\,0)
for Bob (namely the distribution
\widetilde{B}_{2}(b)=\frac{1}{2-b}
for
b\in[0,\,1]
) are another equilibrium.

Both equilibria
(s_{1},\,s_{2})
and
(s_{1},\,\widetilde{s}_{2})
given in
Example

2
have two features:
(i) The strategies
s_{1}(v_{1})
,
s_{2}(v_{2})
and
\widetilde{s}_{2}(v_{2})
are
pure strategies
– Each of them has no randomness, just the function of a value.
(ii) Both equilibria are
fully efficient
akin to the symmetric setting – Alice always has the highest value
\equiv 2
and always wins the item.
However, these are not always the case in the asymmetric setting, such as in the following example, which only slightly modifies
Example

2
by changing the fixed value of Alice from
2
to
1
.

Example 3
.

Consider two bidders: Alice has a fixed value
v_{1}\equiv 1
and bids
s_{1}(v_{1})\sim B_{1}
following the distribution
B_{1}(b)=\frac{1}{4b-2}\exp(\frac{4b-3}{2b-1})
for
b\in[\frac{1}{2},\,\frac{3}{4}]
. Bob has a uniform random value
v_{2}\sim U[0,\,1]
and bids
s_{2}(v_{2})=\max(\frac{4v_{2}-1}{4v_{2}},\,0)
, namely the distribution
B_{2}(b)=\frac{1}{4-4b}
for
b\in[0,\,\frac{3}{4}]
.
By bidding
b\geq 0
, Alice gains an expected utility
(v_{1}-b)\cdot B_{2}(b)
, which is maximized
=\frac{1}{4}
anywhere between
b\in[0,\,\frac{3}{4}]
, so her current strategy
s_{1}(v_{1})\sim B_{1}
is optimal. Using elementary algebra, we can check that Bob’s current strategy
s_{2}(v_{2})=\max(\frac{4v_{2}-1}{4v_{2}},\,0)
also is optimal. In sum, this strategy profile
(s_{1},s_{2})
is an equilibrium.

In
Example

3
: Alice has a
mixed strategy
– A fixed value
v_{1}\equiv 1
but a random bid
s_{1}(v_{1})\sim B_{1}
.
Furthermore, the equilibrium
(s_{1},\,s_{2})
is
not
fully efficient. E.g., with a value
v_{2}=\frac{3}{4}
, although not the highest value
<v_{1}\equiv 1
, Bob bids
s_{2}(v_{2})=\frac{2}{3}
and wins with probability
B_{1}(\frac{2}{3})=\frac{3}{2e}\approx 0.5518
. Indeed,
Example

3
has infinite equilibria, among which the given one
(s_{1},s_{2})
has the relatively “simplest” format. But none of those equilibria is a pure equilibrium or is fully efficient, despite that
Example

3
is only a minor modification of
Example

2
.

From the above examples, we observe that Bayesian Nash equilibria
can be very complicated and sensitive to the value distributions, despite the intrinsic simple rule of the first-price auction. After an extensive study for more than 60 years, the first-price auction and its equilibria remain the centerpiece of modern auction theory and have promoted a rich literature; see
[
SZ90
,
Leb06
,
Leb96
,
Leb99
,
MR00a
,
MR00b
,
JSSZ02
,
MR03
,
HKMN11
,
CH13
,
ST13
,
Syr14
,
HHT14
,
FLN16
,
HTW18
,
WSZ20
,
FGH+21
]
etc. These efforts are justifiable since the study of the first-price auction and its equilibria is both theoretically challenging and practically important.

Among various aspects of the first-price auction,
efficiency
at equilibria is of primary interest. In economics, efficiency measures to what extent a resource can be allocated to the persons who value it the most, thus maximizing the
social welfare
, particularly in a competitive environment.
As shown above (
Example

3
), the first-price auction generally is not fully efficient at an equilibrium: The winner has the highest
bid
but possibly not the highest
value
; this crucially depends on both (i) the instance
\boldsymbol{V}
itself and (ii) which Bayesian Nash equilibrium
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\boldsymbol{V})
it falls into.
Earlier works in economics focus on (generalizing) the conditions for the value distribution
\boldsymbol{V}=\{V_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}

that guarantee the full efficiency.

However, the quality of (in)efficiency should not be all-or-nothing.
E.g., when the highest value is
1
, a value-
0.99
bidder versus a value-
0.01
bidder is very different, although neither of them is fully efficient. Towards a quantitative analysis,
Koutsoupias and Papadimitriou introduced a new measure on the efficiency degradation under selfish behaviors, the Price of Anarchy
[
KP99
]
(which is an analog of the “approximation ratio” in theoretical computer science).
For the first-price auction, denote by
{\sf OPT}(\boldsymbol{V})
the expected optimal social welfare from an instance
\boldsymbol{V}
, and by
{\sf FPA}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})
the expected social welfare at an equilibrium
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\boldsymbol{V})
, then the Price of Anarchy is defined to be the minimum possible ratio, as follows.

Definition 1.2

(
Price of Anarchy
)
.

Regarding
First Price Auctions
, the
Price of Anarchy
is given by

{\sf PoA}~:=~\inf\bigg\{\,\frac{{\sf FPA}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})}{{\sf OPT}(\boldsymbol{V})}\,\,\bigg|\,\bigg.\,\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\boldsymbol{V})~\text{and}~{\sf OPT}(\boldsymbol{V})<+\infty\,\bigg\}.

The Price of Anarchy is bounded between
[0,\,1]
. Namely, a larger ratio means a higher efficiency and the
=1
ratio means the full efficiency.
For the first-price auction, Syrgkanis and Tardos first proved that the
PoA
is at least
1-1/e\approx 0.6321

[
ST13
]
.
Later, Hoy, Taggart and Wang derived an improved lower bound of
\approx 0.7430

[
HTW18
]
.
On the other hand, Hartline, Hoy and Taggart gave a concrete instance of ratio
\approx 0.8689

[
HHT14
]
, which remains the best known upper bound.

Despite the prevalence of the first-price auction and much effort in studying its efficiency, there has been a persistent gap in the state of the art. In the current paper, we will completely solve this long-standing open problem.

Theorem 1

(Tight
PoA
)
.

The
Price of Anarchy
in
First Price Auctions
is
1-1/e^{2}\approx 0.8647
.

Remarkably, neither the best known lower bound
\approx 0.7430
nor the upper bound
\approx 0.8689
is tight; we close the gap by improving both of them. Our tight bound
1-1/e^{2}\approx 0.8647
not only is a mathematically elegant result but has further implications in real business since it is fairly close to
1
. Namely, at any equilibrium, the efficiency degradation in the first-price auction is small, no worse than 13.53%, which might be acceptable given other merits of the first-price auction.

En route to the tight
PoA
, we obtain many new important perspectives, characterizations, and properties of equilibria in the first-price auction. These might be of independent interest and find applications in the future study of other aspects of the first-price auction, e.g., the complexity of computing equilibria. Beyond the first-price auction, our overall approach is general enough and might help determine the Price of Anarchy in other auctions.

1.1
Technical overview

This subsection sketches our high-level proof ideas. To ease the readability, some descriptions are roughly accurate but not perfectly, and most technical details are deferred to
Sections

2
,
3
,
4
,
5
and
6
.
Our approach is very different from the prior works
[
ST13
,
HTW18
]
, which mainly adopt the smoothness techniques or an extension.
More concretely, we employ a first principle approach that directly characterizes the
worst-case
instance and the
worst-case
Bayesian Nash equilibrium regarding the definition of the Price of Anarchy. To this end, we step by step narrow down the search space of the worst case by proving more and more necessary conditions it must satisfy.
Finally, we capture the exact worst case and thus derive the tight
PoA
bound.

Changing the viewpoints (
Section

2
)

Regarding the
PoA
definition, we need to prove that
the auction social welfare
{\sf FPA}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})
is within a certain ratio of the optimal social welfare
{\sf OPT}(\boldsymbol{V})
, given any value distribution
\boldsymbol{V}=\{V_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
and any equilibrium thereof
\boldsymbol{s}=\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

Two difficulties emerge immediately: (i) One value distribution
\boldsymbol{V}\in\mathbb{V}
generally has
multiple
or even
infinite
equilibria. (ii) One equilibrium
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\boldsymbol{V})
generally has
no
analytic
solution; even an efficient algorithm for computing (approximate) equilibria from value distributions is unknown.

To the rescue, instead of the original value-strategy representation
(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})\in\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{BNE}
of equilibria,
we use another representation, the
bid distributions

\boldsymbol{B}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})
resulted from equilibria.
These two representations and their relationship are demonstrated in
Figure

1
.
Given one equilibrium bid distribution
\boldsymbol{B}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})=\{B_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
: (i) One equilibrium
(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})\in\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{BNE}
is
uniquely
determined.
(ii) The reconstruction of the
(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})
essentially
has
an analytic
solution, through the
bid-to-value mappings

\varphi_{i}(b):=b+(\sum_{k\neq i}B^{\prime}_{k}(b)/B_{k}(b))^{-1}
that are almost the inverse functions of the strategies
\boldsymbol{s}=\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.
1
1

1

Although the mapping formulas
\varphi_{i}(b)=b+(\sum_{k\neq i}B^{\prime}_{k}(b)/B_{k}(b))^{-1}
are previously known, NO prior work takes ANY further step. Even this structure result is unknown: “One (valid) bid distribution
\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
backward uniquely determines the underlying value-strategy tuple
(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})\in\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{BNE}
”; let alone techniques towards the tight
{\sf PoA}=1-1/e^{2}
.

These mitigate the above two difficulties.

In sum, there is a bijection between the space of equilibria
\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{BNE}\ni(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})
and the space of equilibrium bid distributions
\{\boldsymbol{B}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})\}
.
Further, the new representation
\{\boldsymbol{B}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})\}
is mathematically equivalent but technically easier – this is
one
infinite set instead of the Cartesian product
\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{BNE}
of
two
infinite sets – showing an avenue towards the tight
PoA
. (Later in
Sections

2
,
3
,
4
,
5
and
6
, we will see more advantages.)

Space
\mathbb{V}
Space
\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
\boldsymbol{V}
\boldsymbol{B}
\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}
\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}}
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\boldsymbol{V})
\overline{\boldsymbol{s}}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\boldsymbol{V})
\widehat{\boldsymbol{s}}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\boldsymbol{V})
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{s}}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\boldsymbol{V})

(a)
Forward direction:
\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{BNE}\mapsto\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}

Space
\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{BNE}
Space
\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})
\boldsymbol{B}
(\boldsymbol{V},\,\overline{\boldsymbol{s}})
\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}
(\boldsymbol{V},\,\widehat{\boldsymbol{s}})
\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}
(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}},\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{s}})
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}

(b)
Inverse direction:
\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}\mapsto\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{BNE}

Figure 1:
Diagram of the two representations of equilibrium, (i) the value-strategy representation
(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})\in\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{BNE}
versus (ii) the bid distribution representation
\boldsymbol{B}=\boldsymbol{B}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
.
One value distribution
\boldsymbol{V}
has multiple or even infinite equilibria
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\boldsymbol{V})
; each equilibrium induces one valid bid distribution
\boldsymbol{B}=\boldsymbol{B}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
. Backward, the
\boldsymbol{B}
determines the underlying tuple
(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})\in\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{BNE}
via the bid-to-value mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
. Hence, there is a bijection between the two spaces
\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{BNE}
and
\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
.

Nonetheless, there is no free lunch. The new representation has two drawbacks. (i) Unlike that any value distribution
\boldsymbol{V}\in\mathbb{V}
must be feasible since the existence of an equilibrium
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\boldsymbol{V})
is promised
[
Leb96
]
,
2
2

2

This existence result requires suitable
tie-breaking
rules in the first-price auction; see
Section

2
for more details.

a generic bid distribution
\boldsymbol{B}
not necessarily induces an equilibrium.
To remedy this issue, we introduce the notion of
validity
, which almost refers to
monotonicity
of the bid-to-value mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.
In addition, (ii) a mapping
\varphi_{i}
is undefined at a singular point where the equilibrium bid distribution
B_{i}
of that bidder
i\in[n]
has a probability mass, which must be handled separately.
Luckily, such probability masses are possible only at the
left endpoint
of the distribution’s support.
So, we further consider the
conditional value distribution

P
given one’s bid being at the left endpoint.
Indeed, the concept of validity refers to a tuple
(\boldsymbol{B},\,P)
of equilibrium bid distributions and a conditional value distribution.

More rigorously, our new representation considers all the
valid
tuples/instances
(\boldsymbol{B},\,P)\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
.
Here, the
valid
distributions
\boldsymbol{B}
can be replaced with the
increasing
bid-to-value mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
: One determines the other and vice versa (
Lemma

3.4
).
More importantly, monotonicity of the mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
gives a strong geometric intuition, making the third representation
(\boldsymbol{\varphi},\,P)
of equilibrium more convenient for our later use.
In particular, in what follows, the figures of the mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}

play the role of visual demonstrations
, i.e.,
horizontal bid axes
and
vertical value axes
, where each mapping
\varphi_{i}
denotes one bidder
i\in[n]
.

The worst-case instance (
Section

6
)

Our overall approach is to reduce any given instance to the
worst-case
instance step by step. Thus, it is helpful to describe the worst case in advance, which explains why we design those reductions since our target is very specific.

Figure 2:

The worst case instance
H\otimes\{\sqrt[n]{L}\}^{\otimes n}
. Bidder
H
has a fixed high value
\varphi_{H}(b)\equiv 1
for
b\in[0,\,1-4/e^{2}]
and each bidder
L
has the (parametric) bid-to-value mapping
\varphi_{L}(1-t^{2}\cdot e^{2-2t})=1-t\cdot e^{2-2t}
for
t\in[1,\,2]
.

The worst case has
n+1
bidders, one bidder
H
with a
fixed
high value
v_{H}\equiv 1
and
sufficiently many

n\gg 1
identical low-value bidders
\{\sqrt[n]{L}\}^{\otimes n}
.
Among the low-value bidders, the highest value
v_{L}\sim V_{L}
is distributed following the parametric equation
V_{L}(1-t\cdot e^{2-2t})=4/t^{2}\cdot e^{2t-4}
for
t\in[1,\,2]
, over the value support
v_{L}\in\mathrm{supp}(V_{L})=[0,\,1-2/e^{2}]
.
See
Figure

2
for a visual aid, based on the bid-to-value mappings
\varphi_{H}\otimes\{\varphi_{L}\}^{\otimes n}
.

The equilibrium strategies
s_{H}\otimes\{s_{L}\}^{\otimes n}
and the bid distributions
H\otimes\{\sqrt[n]{L}\}^{\otimes n}
are less important for understanding our approach;
3
3

3

[
HHT14
]
“just” considered a reasonable instance
H(b)=\sqrt{b/\lambda}
and
L(b)=\frac{1-\lambda}{1-b}
for
\lambda=0.57
, but neither gave argument/evidence for “worstness” of the instance family
H\otimes\{\sqrt[n]{L}\}^{\otimes n}
, nor searched the worst case
(H^{*},\,\lambda^{*})
.
In contrast, our contributions are twofold: (primary) “worstness” of this family; (secondary) the nontrivial worst case
b=1-{4H^{*}}\cdot{e^{2-4\sqrt{H^{*}}}}
and
L^{*}(b)=\frac{1-\lambda^{*}}{1-b}
for
\lambda^{*}=1-4/e^{2}
. In this family, the
PoA
is less sensitive to different instances, hence two numerically close bounds
0.8689
versus
0.8647
.

we will show in
Section

6
how to deduce them and the tight
PoA

=1-1/e^{2}
.
The parametric equation
V_{L}
also is less important, being included just for completeness.

In contrast, the point is the underlying structure: The bidder
H
always contributes the optimal social welfare
\equiv 1
.
The low-value bidders
\{\sqrt[n]{L}\}^{\otimes n}

individually
have negligible effects (the winning probabilities etc)
but
collectively
make the auction game less efficient.

We are inspired by the instance due to Hartline, Hoy and Taggart
[
HHT14
]
, which has the same
H\otimes\{\sqrt[n]{L}\}^{\otimes n}
structure. Our construction differs from theirs in the concrete distributions, hence a slightly worse
PoA
of
1-1/e^{2}\approx 0.8647
in comparison with their ratio of
\approx 0.8689
(
Footnote

3
).

The pseudo instances (
Section

3.1
) and
(
Section

4.3
)

Rigorously, the worst case
H\otimes\{\sqrt[n]{L}\}^{\otimes n}
for
n\gg 1
described above is not a specific instance, but a sequence of instances with the
limit

n\to+\infty
being the worst case.
This incurs some notational inconvenience.
When such issues occur in real/complex analysis, the standard method is to add the infinite point(s) to the space of the real/complex numbers, making the space more complete.
In the same spirit, we introduce the notion of
pseudo bidders
and
pseudo instances
(
Definition

3.1
), thus including the above limit to our search space.

(a)

The input
non collapsed
mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}

(b)

The output
collapsed
mappings
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}

Figure 3:
The
Collapse
reduction.
When a specific bidder
i\in[n]
has a fixed high value
\varphi_{i}(b)\equiv v_{i}\geq\max\{\varphi_{k}(b):k\neq i\}
over the bid support
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
, the
Collapse
reduction transforms this instance
\boldsymbol{B}=B_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{B}_{-i}
to a two-bidder pseudo instance
B_{i}\otimes\widetilde{L}
that yields a worse-or-equal
PoA
.

In our extended language, the worst case has a succinct format: one high-value bidder
H
and one pseudo bidder
L
,
namely a two-bidder pseudo instance (
Figure

2
).
Beyond notational brevity, the notion of pseudo bidders/instances also simplifies our proof in several places.

This extension does not hurt our proof, given that pseudo instances are only considered in the
lower-bound
analysis. Precisely, we show that even the worst case in the extended search space has the
PoA

\geq 1-1/e^{2}
, implying a lower bound of
1-1/e^{2}
for the original problem.
In addition, the
upper-bound
analysis leverages the above instance sequence
H\otimes\{\sqrt[n]{L}\}^{\otimes n}
.
Precisely, we show that when the
n\gg 1
is sufficiently large, the
PoA
can be arbitrarily close to
1-1/e^{2}
. As a combination, the tight
PoA
in
Theorem

1
gets established.

Another related thing is the
reduction. As
Figures

3a
and
3b
show,
when a specific bidder
i\in[n]
has a fixed high value
\varphi_{i}(b)\equiv v_{i}\geq\max\{\varphi_{k}(b):k\neq i\}
over the bid support
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
,
the
reduction
can replace all the other bidders
\{B_{k}\}_{k\neq i}
by one pseudo bidder
\widetilde{L}
, resulting in a two-bidder pseudo instance
B_{i}\otimes\widetilde{L}
that has a worse-or-equal
PoA
.
(I.e, whether before or after the
reduction, the optimal social welfare is the bidder
i
’s fixed high value
\equiv v_{i}
. Moreover, it turns out that the auction social welfare can only decrease.)
Such a two-bidder pseudo instance
B_{i}\otimes\widetilde{L}

will
be easy to handle since it has the same shape as the worst case
H\otimes L
.

In sum, the remaining task is to transform every valid instance
\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
into a specific instance that the
Collapse
reduction can work on, i.e., an instance that has one
fixed-high-value
bidder.

(
Section

3.4
): Hierarchizing the bidders

(a)

The input
increasing
mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}

(b)

The output
layered
mappings
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}

Figure 4:
The
Layer
reduction. Each color corresponds to a bidder. After the reduction, the bidders are ordered. For example, the red bidder is the highest bidder.

To transform a given instance into the worst case, we shall identify one special high-value bidder
H\in[n]
and “derandomize” his/her value, i.e., making the bid-to-value mapping a constant function
\varphi_{H}(b)\equiv v_{H}
. (If so, upon scaling, we can normalize this fixed value
v_{H}=1
.)
But it is even unclear which bidder
i\in[n]
should be the candidate: The highest-value bidder
\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}\{\varphi_{i}(b):i\in[n]\}
at different bids
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
can be different.
More concretely, consider the example in
Figure

4a
,
the highest-value bidder is the red bidder
B_{1}
initially for small bids,
but changes to the blue bidder
B_{2}
later for large bids.

We can segment the bid-to-value mappings into pieces and rearrange them, in a sense of
Figure

4b
, making them
ordered point-wise

\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(b)\geq\dots\geq\widetilde{\varphi}_{n}(b)
.
Then, the candidate high-value bidder clearly is the index-
1
bidder
\widetilde{B}_{1}
(i.e., the red one in
Figure

4b
).
We call this reduction
.

Under the
reduction, the auction/optimal social welfares
FPA
and
OPT
each remain the same, so the
PoA
is invariant.
This proof is not that technically involved,
once we formalize the reduction.
However, without switching our viewpoint to the bid-to-value mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, even describing this reduction seems difficult.

In sum, we narrow down the search space to the “layered” instances and the remaining task to address the candidate high-value bidder
B_{1}
, particularly derandomizing his/her value
\varphi_{1}(b)\equiv v_{1}
.

and
(
Sections

3.2
and
3.3
): Two simplifications

(a)

The input
increasing
mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}

(b)

The output
discretized
/
translated
mappings
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}

Figure 5:
The
Discretize
and
Translate
reductions

Our main reduction is an
iterative
algorithm; thus it is convenient to work with
bounded discrete
instances, for which the main reduction will terminate in
finite
iterations.
Such an idea appears in many prior works
[
CGL14
,
AHN+19
,
JLTX20
,
JLQ+19
,
JJLZ21
]
, and the proof plan is twofold: (i) For any bounded discrete instance, the
PoA
is at least
1-1/e^{2}
. (ii) For any valid instance, there exists a bounded discrete instance approximating the
PoA
, up to an
arbitrary but fixed
error
\varepsilon>0
.
As a combination, the
PoA
for any valid instance is at least
1-1/e^{2}
.

However, our discretization scheme is subtle. First, we cannot discretize the valid bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}
given that they must be continuous everywhere except the left endpoint of the bid support.
Second, we cannot discretize the value distributions and the strategies
(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})
.
Although bounded discrete value distributions
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}}
that well approximate the given ones
\boldsymbol{V}
are trivial, it is unclear how to obtain desirable strategies
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{s}}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}})
:
No algorithm for computing equilibria from value distributions is available.
Also, with modified value distributions, even the existence of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{s}}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}})
that is “close enough” to the given equilibrium
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\boldsymbol{V})
is doubtful.

We circumvent these issues by discretizing the bid-to-value mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
instead (
Figure

5
), i.e., approximating them
through
piecewise constant
functions
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
.
4
4

4

In real analysis, for various purposes, piecewise constant functions (a.k.a. simple functions) are widely used as approximations to general functions.

The benefits are threefold:
(i) The valid bid distributions
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}
, the value distributions
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}}
, and the equilibrium strategies
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{s}}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}})
can be reconstructed from the new mappings
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
, through analytic expressions.
(ii) The piecewise constancy of the new mappings
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
makes the value distribution
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}}

bounded
and
discrete
.
(iii) More importantly, we are able to obtain a
sequence
of piecewise constant mappings
\{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\}
that
pointwise converge
to the given ones
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
.
The corresponding
\{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\}
,
\{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}}\}
, and
\{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{s}}\}
can be arbitrarily close to the given
\boldsymbol{B}
,
\boldsymbol{V}
, and
\boldsymbol{s}
, approximating the
PoA
up to any fixed error
\varepsilon>0
.
This exactly implements the second part of our proof plan.

Afterward, the
reduction further vanishes the lowest bid, through shifting both the value space and the bid space by the same distance of
-\gamma
(
Fig.

5
).
As a consequence, both the auction/optimal social welfares decrease by an amount of
\gamma
, giving a worse-or-equal
PoA
.

In sum, we can focus on
piecewise constant
and
increasing
mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
over a
bounded
support
b\in[0,\,\lambda]
, together with the conditional value
P
given the nil bid
b=0
(i.e., the left endpoint).

and
(
Sections

3.5
and
4.2
): Dealing with the probability masses

Now we address the probability masses and the conditional value
P
. (This is the only information that cannot be reconstructed from the bid-to-value mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
and must be handled separately.)
Our main observation is (
Lemma

2.16
) that
at most one
bidder can have a nontrivial conditional value
P
, who will be called the monopolist
H
.
(After the
reduction, this monopolist
H
will be the
high-value
bidder; cf.
Figure

4
.)
That is why we can use a single conditional value
P
, instead of
n
values akin to the distributions
\boldsymbol{V}
or
\boldsymbol{B}
. Moreover, following the monotonicity, this value
P
is supported on
[0,\varphi_{H}(0)]
.

The
reduction derandomizes the conditional value by moving all the probabilities to either
P\equiv 0
or
P\equiv\varphi_{H}(0)
.
Namely, this is a
win-win
reduction, in a sense that at least one of the two possibilities
P\equiv 0
and
P\equiv\varphi_{H}(0)
induces a worse-or-equal
PoA
than the given instance. Later, such win-win reductions also appear in several other places.

Further, the
reduction totally determines the conditional value
P\equiv\varphi_{H}(0)
by eliminating the other possibility.
This reduction modifies both the conditional value
P
and the mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
, thus far more complicated than the
reduction, which only modifies the
P
.
5
5

5

For very technical reasons, we
should not
unify
and
into a single reduction (cf.
Sections

3
and
4
).

In conclusion, we can drop the notation
P
hereafter: Just the
piecewise constant
and
increasing
mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
are enough to describe the entire instance and the reductions on it.

10
and
(
Sections

4.4
and
4.5
): The main reductions

Our main reduction aims to reduce the number of pieces of the high-value bidder
H
’s mapping
\varphi_{H}
.
(After the
reduction, this mapping has
finite
many pieces. In particular, the one in the worst-case instance has one
single
piece (
Figure

2
), namely a constant mapping.)
To this end, we look at the first
jump point
of this mapping
\varphi_{H}
.
Furthermore, we distinguish two types of jumps,
pseudo jumps
versus
real jumps
, and adopt two different reductions respectively.

For a
4.4
(
),
the after-jump parts of all mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
are
universally
higher than the before-jump parts, so the entire instance naturally divides into the
left-lower
part versus the
right-upper
part (
and
).
We prove that at least one part induces a worse-or-equal
PoA
than the given instance.
6
6

6

In particular, for the right-upper part, we reapply the
reduction to make the bid space start from
0
.

This is another win-win reduction and will be called
10
.

For a
4.5
(
), the after-jump parts (of some mappings) still interlace with the before-jump parts.
We would modify the first two pieces of the high-value mapping
\varphi_{H}
to reduce the number of pieces by one.
I.e., we either
ascend
the first piece to the value of the second piece (
) or
descend
the second piece to the value of the first piece (
).
We show that at least one modification gives a worse-or-equal
PoA
than the given instance. This is again a win-win reduction and will be called
.

To conclude, whether a
4.4
or a
4.5
, we obtain a new instance that (i) has a worse-or-equal
PoA
and (ii) the high-value mapping
\varphi_{H}
has one fewer piece.
Clearly, we can
iterate
the entire process until a constant mapping
\varphi_{H}(b)\equiv v_{H}
, i.e., one
single
piece.
Then, the
reduction can replace all the other bidders
\{B_{k}\}_{k\neq H}
by one pseudo bidder
L
, hence a two-bidder pseudo instance
H\otimes L
that has the same shape as the worst case (
Figure

3
), as desired.

We stress again that the above description is greatly simplified and thus just roughly accurate – The high-level idea is delivered, but most technical details are hidden.

Solving the functional optimization (
Section

5
)

After the above reductions, we get a nice-looking instance: (i) one bidder
H
with a fixed high value
\varphi_{H}(b)\equiv v_{H}
and (ii) infinite
n\to+\infty
identical low-value bidders
\{\sqrt[n]{L}\}^{\otimes n}
, or one pseudo bidder
L
in our extended language.
Towards the worst case, it remains to determine the pseudo mapping
\varphi_{L}(b)
, or essentially, the high-value bidder
H
’s bid distribution.

This is accomplished by standard tools from the calculus of variations.
Via the Euler-Lagrange equation
[
GS+00
]
, we formulate the
worst-case
bid distribution
H
as the solution to an ordinary differential equation (ODE).
Luckily, this ODE admits a closed-form solution, and “coincidentally” the tight bound
1-1/e^{2}
is pretty nice-looking.

Comparison with previous techniques

On the Price of Anarchy in auctions, the canonical approach is the
smoothness framework
proposed by Roughgarden
[
Rou15
]
and developed by Syrgkanis and Tardos
[
ST13
]
.
The past decade has seen an abundance of its applications and extensions (cf. the survey
[
RST17
]
).

However, the smoothness framework has an intrinsic restriction – It focuses on the structure of an auction game BUT ignores the
independence
, i.e., both the independence of value distributions
\boldsymbol{V}=\{V_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
and the independence of strategies
\boldsymbol{s}=\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.
Thus, although giving an arsenal of tools for proving
lower bounds
, this approach seems hard to access
tight bounds
for some auctions. In particular for the first-price auction, the smoothness-based bound of
1-1/e\approx 0.6321
obtained by Syrgkanis and Tardos
[
ST13
]
is tight when
correlated
distributions are allowed. Hence, this bound cannot be improved without taking the independence into account.

The work by Hoy, Taggart, and Wang
[
HTW18
]
is one of the very few follow-ups that transcend the smoothness framework.
7
7

7

To the best of our knowledge,
[
HTW18
]
is the only work for Bayesian
single-item
auctions that transcends the smoothness framework. Other such works for
multi-item
auctions are thoroughly discussed in
[
RST17
, Section 8.3]
.

For the first-price auction, they show an improved lower bound of
\approx 0.7430
, but this bound is still not tight.
Technically, the improvement stems from an extension of the smoothness framework that leverages the independence.

In sum, towards
tight bounds
for the first-price auction and the beyond, the primary consideration is:
What is the consequence of the
independence
of values
\boldsymbol{v}=(v_{i})_{i\in[n]}\sim\boldsymbol{V}
and strategies
\boldsymbol{s}
?
Our answer is, the bids
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})=(s_{i}(v_{i}))_{i\in[n]}
are also independent and follow a product distribution
\boldsymbol{B}=\{B_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
. This is the starting point of our overall approach.

In the literature, ALL prior works (within/beyond the smoothness framework) follow the proof paradigm: “searching over value distributions
\boldsymbol{V}\in\mathbb{V}
and equilibrium strategies
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\boldsymbol{V})
plus deviation strategies thereof
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}\neq\boldsymbol{s}
”
[
RST17
, Section 8.3]
. Instead, we present the FIRST different proof paradigm: “searching over (valid) bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}=\boldsymbol{B}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
that are resulted from certain value-strategy tuples
(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s})\in\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{BNE}
”.

In our main proof, we step by step narrow down the search space of the worst-case instance, by showing stronger and stronger conditions it must satisfy, and completely capture the worst-case instance eventually.
En route, an abundance of tools is developed, which may find applications in the future.
It is conceivable that our approach can be adapted to other auctions.
So, we hope that this would develop into a complement to the smoothness framework, especially in the setting of independent distributions.

2
Structural Results

This section shows a number of structural results on
First Price Auction
and
Bayesian Nash Equilibrium
. Some results or their analogs may already appear in the literature
[
SZ90
,
Leb96
,
MR00a
,
MR00b
,
JSSZ02
,
MR03
,
HKMN11
,
CH13
]
. However, we still formalize and reprove them for completeness.
Provided these structural results, we can reformulate the
Price of Anarchy
problem in terms of bid distributions rather than value distributions.

To sell a single indivisible item to one of
n\geq 1
bidders, each bidder
i\in[n]
is asked to submit a non-negative bid
b_{i}\geq 0
. A generic auction
\mathcal{A}=(\mathrm{x},\,\boldsymbol{\rho})
is given by its allocation rule
\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b}):\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto[n]
and payment rule
\boldsymbol{\rho}(\boldsymbol{b})=(\rho_{i}(\boldsymbol{b}))_{i\in[n]}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, both of which can be
randomized
.
Concretely, the item is allocated to
the
bidder
\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})\in[n]
, and
\rho_{i}(\boldsymbol{b})
’s are the payments of bidders
i\in[n]
.

Rigorously,
First Price Auction
is not a single auction but a family of auctions, each of which has a specific allocation rule that obeys the
•
‣
2.1
/
•
‣
2.1
principles.

Definition 2.1

(
First Price Auctions
)
.

An
n
-bidder single-item auction
\mathcal{A}=(\mathrm{x},\,\boldsymbol{\rho})
is called a
First Price Auction
when its allocation rule
\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})
and payment rule
\boldsymbol{\rho}(\boldsymbol{b})=(\rho_{i}(\boldsymbol{b}))_{i\in[n]}
satisfy that:

•

first price allocation:

Let
X(\boldsymbol{b}):=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}\big\{b_{i}:i\in[n]\big\}
be the set of first-order bidders. When such bidders are not unique
|X(\boldsymbol{b})|\geq 2
, allocate the item to one of them
\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})\in X(\boldsymbol{b})
, through a (randomized)
tie-breaking
rule given by the auction
\mathcal{A}
itself on this bid profile
\boldsymbol{b}
. When such a bidder is unique
|X(\boldsymbol{b})|=1
, allocate the item to him/her
\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})\equiv X(\boldsymbol{b})
.

•

first price payment:
The allocated bidder
\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})
pays his/her own bid, while non-allocated bidders
[n]\setminus\{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})\}
each pay nothing. Formally,
\rho_{i}(\boldsymbol{b})=b_{i}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\big(i=\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})\big)
for
i\in[n]
.

Without ambiguity, the allocation rule
\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})
itself will be called the considered
First Price Auction
, since it controls the payment rule
\boldsymbol{\rho}(\boldsymbol{b})
.
Denote by
\mathbb{FPA}\ni\mathrm{x}
the space of all
First Price Auctions
.

The only undetermined part of a
First Price Auction
is the tie-breaking rule, which is subtle and plays an important role in our analysis.
To clarify it, let us rigorously define the interim allocation/ utility formulas (
Definition

2.2
) and
Bayesian Nash Equilibrium
(
Definition

2.3
).

Definition 2.2

(Interim allocations/utilities)
.

Given a
First Price Auction

\mathrm{x}\in\mathbb{FPA}
, value distributions
\boldsymbol{V}=\{V_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, and a strategy profile
\boldsymbol{s}=\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
:

•

Each interim allocation formula
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b):=\operatorname{{\bf Pr}}_{\mathrm{x},\,\boldsymbol{s}_{-i}(\boldsymbol{v}_{-i})}\big[i=\mathrm{x}\big(b,\,\boldsymbol{s}_{-i}(\boldsymbol{v}_{-i})\big)\big]
for
b\geq 0
, over the randomness of the others’ bids
\boldsymbol{s}_{-i}(\boldsymbol{v}_{-i})
and the considered
First Price Auction

\mathrm{x}\in\mathbb{FPA}
itself.

•

Each interim utility formula
u_{i}(v,\,b):=(v-b)\cdot\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)
for
v\in\mathrm{supp}(V_{i})
and
b\geq 0
.

Definition 2.3

(
Bayesian Nash Equilibria
)
.

Following
Definition

2.2
, the strategy profile
\boldsymbol{s}=\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
reaches a
Bayesian Nash Equilibrium
for this distribution-auction tuple, namely
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\mathrm{x})
, when: For each bidder
i\in[n]
, any possible value
v\in\mathrm{supp}(V_{i})
, and any deviation bid
b\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{s_{i}}\big[\,u_{i}(v,\,s_{i}(v))\,\big]~\geq~u_{i}(v,\,b).

Definition

2.3
means for any value
v\in\mathrm{supp}(V_{i})
, nearly all the equilibrium bids
b\in\mathrm{supp}(s_{i}(v))
EACH maximize the interim utility formula
u_{i}(v,\,b)
, except a
zero-measure
set.
By excluding these zero-measure sets from the bid supports
\mathrm{supp}(s_{i}(v))
, for
i\in[n]
, the modified strategy profile still satisfies the definition of
Bayesian Nash Equilibrium
. Hereafter, without loss of generality, we assume that EVERY equilibrium bid
b\in\mathrm{supp}(s_{i}(v))
maximizes the formula
u_{i}(v,\,b)
.

The following existence result can be concluded from
[
Leb96
]
.

Theorem 2

(
Bayesian Nash Equilibrium

[
Leb96
]
)
.

Given any value distribution
\boldsymbol{V}=\{V_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, there exists at least one
First Price Auction

\mathrm{x}\in\mathbb{FPA}
that admits an equilibrium
\mathbb{BNE}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\mathrm{x})\neq\emptyset
.

2.1
The forward direction:
Bayesian Nash Equilibria

This subsection presents a bunch of lemmas that characterize an equilibrium
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\mathrm{x})\neq\emptyset
.
To this end, let us introduce some helpful notations, which will be adopted throughout this paper.

•

\boldsymbol{B}=\{B_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
denotes the equilibrium bid distributions
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v})=(s_{i}(v_{i}))_{i\in[n]}\sim\boldsymbol{B}
.

•

\mathcal{B}(b)=\prod_{i\in[n]}B_{i}(b)
denotes the first-order bid distribution
\max(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))\sim\mathcal{B}
.

•

\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)=\prod_{k\in[n]\setminus\{i\}}B_{k}(b)
denotes the competing bid distribution of each bidder
i\in[n]
.

•

\gamma:=\inf(\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{B}))
and
\lambda:=\sup(\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{B}))
denote the “infimum”/“supremum” first-order bids, respectively.
Without ambiguity, we call
v,\,b<\gamma
the low values/bids,
v,\,b=\gamma
the boundary values/bids, and
v,\,b>\gamma
the normal values/bids. As their names suggest:
(i) low bids
b<\gamma
each gives a zero winning probability, so these bids are less important;
(ii) normal bids
b>\gamma
are the most common ones and we will show that they behave nicely; and
(iii) boundary bids
b=\gamma
are tricky and we will deal with them separately.

•

\mathcal{U}_{i}(v):=\max\big\{u_{i}(v,\,b):b\geq 0\big\}
denotes the optimal utility formula of each bidder
i\in[n]
. This is well defined on the value support
v\in\mathrm{supp}(V_{i})
, because (
Definition

2.3
) every equilibrium bid
s_{i}(v)
does optimize the interim utility formula
u_{i}(v,\,s_{i}(v))=\mathcal{U}_{i}(v)
.

Each bidder’s interim allocation
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)
relies on his/her competing bid distribution
\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
, plus the tie-breaking rule of the considered auction
\mathrm{x}\in\mathbb{FPA}
.
When
\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
is a continuous distribution,
they are identical
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)\equiv\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
,
because the probability of being ONE of the first-order bidders
\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[X(\boldsymbol{b})\ni i]
is exactly the probability of being the ONLY one
\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[X(\boldsymbol{b})=\{i\}]
.
In general, we can at least obtain (
Lemma

2.4
) the following properties for an interim allocation
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)
.

Lemma 2.4

(Interim allocations)
.

For each interim allocation formula
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)
:

1.

\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)
is weakly increasing for
b\geq 0
.

2.

It is point-wise lower/upper bounded as
\sup\big\{\mathcal{B}_{-i}(t):t<b\big\}\leq\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)\leq\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
for
b\geq 0
.

3.

\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)=0
for any low bid
b<\gamma
, AND
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)>0
for any normal bid
b>\gamma
.

Proof.

Items

1
and
2
directly follows from the
•
‣
2.1
principle (
Definition

2.1
). Especially for
Item

2
, suppose that (with a nonzero probability) bidder
B_{i}
ties with someone else
[n]\setminus\{i\}
for this first-order bid
s_{i}(v_{i})=\max(\boldsymbol{s}_{-i}(\boldsymbol{v}_{-i}))=b
, then the auction always favors this bidder
B_{i}
when “
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)=\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
”, or always disfavor him/her when “
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)=\sup\big\{\mathcal{B}_{-i}(t):t<b\big\}
”.

Item

3
holds because
\gamma=\inf(\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{B}))
is the infimum first-order bid
\max(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))\sim\mathcal{B}
.
∎

Lemma

2.5
shows that both of the value space
v\in\mathrm{supp}(V_{i})
and the bid space
b\geq 0
of each bidder
i\in[n]
, are almost separated by the infimum first-order bid
\gamma=\inf(\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{B}))
.

Lemma 2.5

(Bidding dichotomy)
.

For each bidder
i\in[n]
:

1.

For a low/boundary value
v\in\mathrm{supp}_{\leq\gamma}(V_{i})
, the optimal utility is zero
\mathcal{U}_{i}(v)=0
and, almost surely over random strategy
s_{i}
, the equilibrium bid is low/boundary
s_{i}(v)\leq\gamma
.

2.

For a normal value
v\in\mathrm{supp}_{>\gamma}(V_{i})
, the optimal utility is nonzero
\mathcal{U}_{i}(v)>0
and, almost surely over random strategy
s_{i}
, the equilibrium bid is boundary/normal
\gamma\leq s_{i}(v)<v
.

Proof.

Recall
Definition

2.2
for the interim utility formula
u_{i}(v,\,b)=(v-b)\cdot\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)
.

Item

1
.

A low/boundary value
v\in\mathrm{supp}_{\leq\gamma}(V_{i})
induces EITHER a low (under)bid
b<v\leq\gamma
and a zero interim allocation/utility
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)=u_{i}(v,\,b)=0
, OR a truthful/over bid
b\geq v
and a negative interim utility
u_{i}(v,\,b)\leq 0
. Especially, a normal bid
b>\gamma\geq v
induces a nonzero interim allocation
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)>0
and a strictly negative interim utility
u_{i}(v,\,b)<0
. Therefore, the optimal utility is zero
\mathcal{U}_{i}(v)=0
and the equilibrium bid must be low/boundary
s_{i}(v)\leq\gamma
.

Item

1
follows then.

Item

2
.

A normal value
v\in\mathrm{supp}_{>\gamma}(V_{i})
is able to gain a positive utility
u_{i}(v,\,b)>0
; for example, underbidding
b^{*}=(\gamma+v)/2\in(\gamma,\,v)
gives a nonzero allocation/utility
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b^{*}),\,u_{i}(v,\,b^{*})>0
.
So, the equilibrium bid
s_{i}(v)
neither can be truthful/over
b\geq v
(hence a negative utility
u_{i}(v,\,b)\leq 0
), nor can be low
b<\gamma
(hence a zero allocation/utility
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)=u_{i}(v,\,b)=0
). That is, the optimal utility is nonzero
\mathcal{U}_{i}(v)>0
and the equilibrium (under)bid must be boundary/normal
\gamma\leq s_{i}(v)<v
.

Item

2
follows then.
∎

Lemma

2.6
shows, in the normal value regime
v\in\mathrm{supp}_{>\gamma}(V_{i})
, the optimal utility formula
\mathcal{U}_{i}(v)
and the equilibrium strategy
s_{i}(v)
are monotonic (cf.
[
CH13
, Lemma 3.9]
for a similar result).

Lemma 2.6

(Bidding monotonicity)
.

For any two normal values
v,\,w\in\mathrm{supp}_{>\gamma}(V_{i})
that
v>w
of a bidder
i\in[n]
:

1.

The two optimal utilities are strictly monotonic
\mathcal{U}_{i}(v)>\mathcal{U}_{i}(w)>0
.

2.

The two random bids are weakly monotonic
s_{i}(v)\geq s_{i}(w)\geq\gamma
, almost surely over the random strategy
s_{i}
.

Proof.

Lemma

2.5
already shows that the utilities are nonzero
\mathcal{U}_{i}(v),\,\mathcal{U}_{i}(w)>0
and the equilibrium bids are boundary/normal
s_{i}(v),\,s_{i}(w)\geq\gamma
. It remains to verify the utility/bidding monotonicity.

Item

1
.

The utility monotonicity
\mathcal{U}_{i}(v)>\mathcal{U}_{i}(w)
. We deduce that

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{i}(v)~\geq~u_{i}(v,\,s_{i}(w))~=~\frac{v-s_{i}(w)}{w-s_{i}(w)}\cdot u_{i}(w,\,s_{i}(w))~=~\frac{v-s_{i}(w)}{w-s_{i}(w)}\cdot\mathcal{U}_{i}(w)~>~\mathcal{U}_{i}(w),

The first step: Under the value
v
, the optimal utility
\mathcal{U}_{i}(v)
is at least the interim utility
u_{i}(v,\,s_{i}(w))
resulted from bidding
s_{i}(w)
, namely the equilibrium bid at the (lower) value
w
.

The second step: Apply the interim utility formula to
u_{i}(v,\,s_{i}(w))
and
u_{i}(w,\,s_{i}(w))
.

The third step: The random bid
s_{i}(w)
optimizes the interim utility formula
u_{i}(w,\,s_{i}(w))=\mathcal{U}_{i}(w)
at the value
w
.

The fourth step: (
Lemma

2.5
)
v>w>s_{i}(w)
and
\mathcal{U}_{i}(w)>0
.

Item

1
follows then.

Item

2
.

The bidding monotonicity
s_{i}(v)\geq s_{i}(w)
. This result also appears in
[
CH13
, Lemma 3.9]
; we would recap their proof for completeness.
Assume to the opposite that
s_{i}(v)<s_{i}(w)
.
Following the interim utility formula (
Definition

2.2
),

\displaystyle u_{i}(v,\,s_{i}(v))

\displaystyle~=~u_{i}(w,\,s_{i}(v))~+~(v-w)\cdot\mathrm{x}_{i}(s_{i}(v)),\phantom{\big.}

\displaystyle u_{i}(v,\,s_{i}(w))

\displaystyle~=~u_{i}(w,\,s_{i}(w))~+~(v-w)\cdot\mathrm{x}_{i}(s_{i}(w)).\phantom{\big.}

We have
(i)

u_{i}(v,\,s_{i}(v))\geq u_{i}(v,\,s_{i}(w))
, since the equilibrium bid
s_{i}(v)
optimizes the interim utility formula
u_{i}(v,\,b)
for this value
v
. Similarly, we have
(ii)

u_{i}(w,\,s_{i}(w))\geq u_{i}(w,\,s_{i}(v))
.
Also, under the assumption
s_{i}(v)<s_{i}(w)
, we know from
Item

1
of
Lemma

2.4
that
(iii)

\mathrm{x}_{i}(s_{i}(v))\leq\mathrm{x}_{i}(s_{i}(w))
.
Given the above two equations (note that
v>w
), each of
2.1
,
2.1
, and
2.1

must achieve the equality. However, this means
8
8

8

In particular, the equality of
2.1
gives
(v-s_{i}(v))\cdot\mathrm{x}(s_{i}(v))=(v-s_{i}(w))\cdot\mathrm{x}(s_{i}(w))
; notice that
s_{i}(v)<s_{i}(w)
. And the equality of
2.1
gives
\mathrm{x}(s_{i}(v))=\mathrm{x}(s_{i}(w))
. For these reasons, we must have
\mathrm{x}(s_{i}(v))=\mathrm{x}(s_{i}(w))=0
.
all interim allocations/utilities are zero
\mathrm{x}_{i}(s_{i}(v))=\mathrm{x}_{i}(s_{i}(w))=u_{i}(v,\,s_{i}(v))=u_{i}(w,\,s_{i}(w))=0
, which contradicts
Item

1
.
Rejecting our assumption gives
Item

2
.
∎

Remark 3

(Quantiles)
.

Due to
Lemma

2.6
, the normal value space
v_{i}>\gamma
and normal/boundary bid space
s_{i}(v_{i})\geq\gamma
each identify the other in terms of quantiles. From this perspective, the stochastic process of the auction game runs as follows: Draw a uniform random quantile
q_{i}\sim U[0,1]
, and then realize the value/bid
V_{i}^{-1}(q_{i})
and
B_{i}^{-1}(q_{i})
accordingly. The two stochastic processes are equivalent conditioned on the realized value being normal
V_{i}^{-1}(q_{i})>\gamma
.

Lemma

2.7
shows that, on the whole interval
b\in[\gamma=\inf(\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{B})),\,\lambda=\sup(\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{B}))]
, all of the equilibrium/competing/first-order bid distributions
B_{i}(b)
,
\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
, and
\mathcal{B}(b)
are well structured.
The earlier works
[
MR00a
,
MR00b
,
MR03
]
derive similar results (under additional assumptions).

Lemma 2.7

(Bid distributions)
.

Each of the following holds:

1.

The competing/first-order bid distributions
\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
for
i\in[n]
and
\mathcal{B}(b)
each have probability densities almost everywhere on
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
, therefore being strictly increasing CDF’s on the CLOSED interval
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
.

2.

The equilibrium/competing/first-order bid distributions
B_{i}(b)
for
i\in[n]
,
\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
for
i\in[n]
and
\mathcal{B}(b)
, each have no probability mass on
b\in(\gamma,\,\lambda]
, excluding the boundary
\gamma=\inf(\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{B}))
, therefore being continuous CDF’s on the CLOSED interval
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
.

Proof.

We safely assume
n\geq 2
. (Otherwise,
Lemma

2.7
is vacuously true since the unique bidder always bids zero
[\gamma,\,\lambda]=\{0\}
in any
First Price Auction
.)
The proof relies on
A
.

Fact A
.

Any two equilibrium bid distributions
B_{i}
and
B_{k}
for
i\neq k\in[n]
cannot both have probability masses at a normal bid
b>\gamma
.

Proof.

Assume the opposite. Then both bidders
B_{i}
and
B_{k}
(and possibly someone else) tie for this first-order bid
s_{i}(v_{i})=s_{k}(v_{k})=\max(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))=b>\gamma
with a nonzero probability
>0
. Conditioned on this, with a nonzero probability
>0
, at least one bidder between
B_{i}
and
B_{k}
cannot be allocated. Hence, either
B_{i}
or
B_{k}
or both can gain a nonzero extra allocation by raising his/her (equilibrium) bid
s_{i}(v_{i})=s_{k}(v_{k})=b
. More formally, for some nonzero probabilities
\delta_{i},\,\delta_{k}>0
, we have EITHER
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b^{*})\geq\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)+\delta_{i}
for any
b^{*}>b
, OR
\mathrm{x}_{k}(b^{*})\geq\mathrm{x}_{k}(b)+\delta_{k}
for any
b^{*}>b
, OR both.

Without loss of generality, let us consider the case that
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b^{*})\geq\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)+\delta_{i}
for any
b^{*}>b
. However, this means some deviation bid, such as
b^{*}=b+\frac{\delta_{i}/2}{\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)+\delta_{i}}\cdot(v_{i}-b)
, can benefit. Namely, because this bidder
B_{i}
has a normal equilibrium bid
s_{i}(v_{i})=b>\gamma
, (
Item

2
of
Lemma

2.5
) he/she must have a higher value
v_{i}>s_{i}(v_{i})=b
. This implies that (by construction)
b<b^{*}<v_{i}
and thus, that the deviated utility
u_{i}(v_{i},\,b^{*})=(v_{i}-b^{*})\cdot\mathrm{x}_{i}(b^{*})
is lower bounded as

\displaystyle u_{i}(v_{i},\,b^{*})

\displaystyle~\geq~(v_{i}-b^{*})\cdot\big(\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)+\delta_{i}\big)

the “WLOG”

\displaystyle~=~(v_{i}-b)\cdot\big(\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)+\delta_{i}/2\big)

construction of
b^{*}

\displaystyle~>~(v_{i}-b)\cdot\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)

v_{i}>b
and
\delta_{i}>0

\displaystyle~=~u_{i}(v_{i},\,b)~=~u_{i}(v_{i},\,s_{i}(v_{i})).

s_{i}(v_{i})=b

To conclude, the deviation bid
b^{*}
strictly surpasses the equilibrium bid
s_{i}(v_{i})
, which contradicts (
Definition

2.3
) the optimality of
s_{i}(v_{i})
. Rejecting our assumption gives
A
.
∎

Remark 4
.

In the proof of
A
, the concrete construction of the deviation bid
b^{*}
is less important.
Instead, the point is that
we are able to find two bids
b^{*}>b=s_{i}(v_{i})
that can be arbitrarily close
(b^{*}-b)\searrow 0
BUT
admit a
nonzero
interim allocation gap
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b^{*})-\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)\geq\delta_{i}>0
.
Suppose so, bidder
i
strictly benefits
u_{i}(v,\,b^{*})>u_{i}(v,\,b)
from the deviation bid
b^{*}
when it is close enough to the equilibrium bid
b^{*}\searrow b
, hence a contradiction to the optimality of
b=s_{i}(v_{i})
.

Also, suppose there are two bids
b^{*}<b=s_{i}(v_{i})
that yield two arbitrarily close
nonzero
interim allocations
(\mathrm{x}_{i}(b^{*})-\mathrm{x}_{i}(b))\nearrow 0
BUT themselves are bounded away
b-b^{*}\geq\delta_{i}>0
,
then bidder
i
again benefits from the deviation bid
b^{*}
, hence a contradiction to the optimality of
b=s_{i}(v_{i})
.

We will apply such arguments in many places, WITHOUT specifying the deviation bids
b^{*}
as the explicit constructions are less informative.
For clarity, such arguments will be called the
Neighborhood Deviation Arguments
.

Below let us prove
Item

1
and
Item

2
. We reason about the first-order bid distribution
\mathcal{B}(b)
and the competing bid distributions
\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
separately.

Item

1
:
\mathcal{B}(b)
.

Assume the opposite to the
\mathcal{B}(b)
’s strong monotonicity: The first-order bid distribution
\mathcal{B}(b)
has no probability density/mass on an OPEN interval
(\alpha,\,\beta)\subseteq[\gamma,\,\lambda]
.

Indeed, because
\gamma=\inf(\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{B}))
and
\lambda=\sup(\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{B}))
,
we can find a
maximal
such interval
(\alpha,\,\beta)
such that, the
\mathcal{B}(b)
has probability densities or probability masses within both of the left/right neighborhoods
(\alpha-\delta,\,\alpha]
and
[\beta,\,\beta+\delta)
, for
whatever

\delta>0
. This gives
\mathcal{B}(\beta)\geq\mathcal{B}(\alpha)>0
.
Let us do case analysis:

•

Case 1:
\mathcal{B}(\beta)>\mathcal{B}(\alpha)>0
.
Then the
\mathcal{B}(b)
must have ONE probability mass at the
\beta
.

(
A
) Exactly one equilibrium bid distribution,
B_{k}(b)
for a specific
k\in[n]
, has a probability mass at the
\beta
; his/her competing bid distribution
\mathcal{B}_{-k}(b)
has no probability mass there, thus being continuous at the
\beta
. This competing bid distribution
\mathcal{B}_{-k}(b)
has no probability density/mass on the open interval
(\alpha,\,\beta)
, à la the first-order bid distribution
\mathcal{B}(b)
.

Hence, (
Item

1
of
Lemma

2.4
) bidder
B_{k}(b)
has a
constant
interim allocation
\mathrm{x}_{k}(b)=\mathrm{x}_{k}(\beta)=\mathcal{B}_{-k}(\beta)>0
on the left-open
right-closed
interval
b\in(\alpha,\,\beta]
.
But this means, conditioned on this bidder’s equilibrium bid being at the probability mass
\big\{s_{k}(v_{k})=\beta\big\}
, any lower deviation bid
b^{*}\in(\alpha,\,\beta)
yields a better deviated utility
u_{k}(v_{k},\,b^{*})>u_{k}(v_{k},\,s_{k}(v_{k}))
. This contradicts (
Definition

2.3
) the optimality of this equilibrium bid
s_{k}(v_{k})=\beta
.

•

Case 2:
\mathcal{B}(\beta)=\mathcal{B}(\alpha)>0
.
Then the
\mathcal{B}(b)
must have NO probability mass at the
\beta
.

À la the
\mathcal{B}(b)
, at least one bidder
B_{k}(b)
for some
k\in[n]
, has probability densities within the right neighborhood
[\beta,\,\beta+\delta)
, for whatever
\delta>0
. No matter how close
b\searrow\beta
, we can find an equilibrium bid
b=s_{k}(v)
, for some
v\in\mathrm{supp}(V_{k})
, that gives a positive utility
u_{k}(v,\,b)>0
.

À la
Case 1
, this bidder has a
constant
interim allocation
\mathrm{x}_{k}(b^{*})=\mathrm{x}_{k}(\beta)=\mathcal{B}_{-k}(\beta)>0
on the left-open
right-closed
interval
b^{*}\in(\alpha,\,\beta]
; let us consider a particular bid
b^{*}=\frac{1}{2}(\alpha+\beta)
.
This deviation bid
b^{*}
is bounded away from the above equilibrium bid
b-b^{*}\geq\frac{1}{2}(\beta-\alpha)>0
BUT, because there is no probability mass at
\beta
, gives an arbitrarily close interim allocation
\big(\mathrm{x}_{k}(b)-\mathrm{x}_{k}(b^{*})\big)\searrow 0
.
Therefore, we can apply the
4
to get a contradiction.

To conclude, we get a contradiction in either case. Reject our assumption: The first-order bid distribution
\mathcal{B}(b)
is strictly increasing on
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
.

Item

1
:
\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
.

Assume the opposite to the
\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
’s strong monotonicity: At least one competing bid distribution
\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
has no probability density/mass on an OPEN interval
(\alpha,\,\beta)\subseteq[\gamma,\,\lambda]
.

In contrast, the remaining bidder
B_{i}(b)
has probability densities/masses almost everywhere on
(\alpha,\,\beta)
, since the first-order bid distribution
\mathcal{B}(b)=B_{i}(b)\cdot\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
is strongly increasing. Consider such an equilibrium bid
s_{i}(v_{i})\in(\alpha,\,\beta)
. However, any lower deviation bid
b^{*}\in\big(\alpha,\,s_{i}(v_{i})\big)
results in the same nonzero allocation
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b^{*})=\mathrm{x}_{i}(s_{i}(v_{i}))>0
and thus a better deviated utility
u_{i}(v_{i},\,b^{*})=(v_{i}-b^{*})\cdot\mathrm{x}_{i}(b^{*})>\big(v_{i}-s_{i}(v_{i})\big)\cdot\mathrm{x}_{i}(b^{*})=u_{i}(v_{i},\,s_{i}(v_{i}))
.
This contradicts (
Definition

2.3
) the optimality of the equilibrium bid
s_{i}(v_{i})\in(\alpha,\,\beta)
.
Reject our assumption: Each competing bid distribution
\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
is strictly increasing on
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
.

Item

1
follows then.

Item

2
.

We only need to verify continuity of the first-order bid distribution
\mathcal{B}(b)=B_{i}(b)\cdot\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
, which implies continuity of the equilibrium/competing bid distributions
\mathcal{B}_{i}(b)
and
\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
.

Assume the opposite:
\mathcal{B}(b)
has a probability mass at some normal bid
\beta\in(\gamma,\,\lambda]
.
According to
A
, exactly one bidder
B_{i}(b)
, for a specific
i\in[n]
, has a probability mass at this
\beta
. Further, following
Item

1
, his/her competing bid distribution
\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
has probability densities within the OPEN left neighborhood
b\in(\beta-\delta,\,\beta)
, for
whatever

\delta>0
; therefore at least one OTHER bidder
k\in[n]\setminus\{i\}
has probability densities there.

This other bidder
B_{k}(b)
’s interim allocation formula
\mathrm{x}_{k}(b)
is
discontinuous
at the
\beta\in(\gamma,\,\lambda]
,
because of
B_{i}(b)
’s probability mass at the
\beta
. Formally (
Lemma

2.4
), there exists a nonzero interim allocation gap
\xi^{*}:=\inf\big\{\,\mathrm{x}_{k}(b^{*})-\mathrm{x}_{k}(b):b^{*}>\beta>b\,\big\}>0
around the
\beta\in(\gamma,\,\lambda]
.
Hence, for some bids
b\in(\beta-\delta,\,\beta)
and
b^{*}>\beta
that are arbitrary close
(b^{*}-b)\searrow 0
, we can use the
4
to get a contradiction.
Rejecting our assumption gives
Item

2
.

This finishes the proof of
Lemma

2.7
.
∎

An important and direct implication of the continuity is that
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)=\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
for any
b\in(\gamma,\lambda]
. This is formalized as
Corollary

2.8
and will be used in many places in the paper.

Corollary 2.8

(Interim allocations)
.

The interim allocation formula
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)
of each bidder
i\in[n]
is identical to his/her competing bid distribution
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)=\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
, for any normal bid
b\in(\gamma,\lambda]
.

According to the Lebesgue differentiation theorem
[
Leb04
]
, a monotonic function
f:(\gamma,\,\lambda)\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is differentiable almost everywhere, except a set
\mathbb{D}_{f}\subseteq(0,\,\lambda)
of a zero measure.
9
9

9

Rigorously, the set
\mathbb{D}_{f}
has a zero
Lebesgue
measure. Yet since the bid distributions
B_{i}(b)
,
\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
, and
\mathcal{B}(b)
are continuous on
b\in(\gamma,\,\lambda)
, we need not to distinguish the Lebesgue/probabilistic measures.

We thus conclude
Corollary

2.9
directly from
Lemma

2.7
.

Corollary 2.9

(Bid distributions)
.

Each of the equilibrium/competing/first-order bid distributions,
\{B_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
,
\{\mathcal{B}_{-i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, and
\mathcal{B}

is differentiable almost everywhere on the OPEN interval
b\in(\gamma,\,\lambda)
, except a set
\mathbb{D}\subseteq(\gamma,\,\lambda)
on which this bid distribution,
B_{i}(b)
,
\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
, or
\mathcal{B}(b)
, has a zero measure.

Remark 5

(Bid distributions)
.

The continuity/monotonicity/differentiability shown in
Lemmas

2.7
and
2.9
are the basics of our later discussions; all subsequent bid distributions will satisfy them. So for brevity, we often omit a formal clarification. Also, we often simply write the derivatives
B^{\prime}_{i}(b)
etc by omitting (
Corollary

2.9
) the zero-measure indifferentiable points
\mathbb{D}\subseteq(\gamma,\,\lambda)
;
we can use standard tools from real analysis to deal with those points
\mathbb{D}\subseteq(\gamma,\,\lambda)
separately.

2.2
The inverse direction: Bid-to-value mappings

In this subsection, we study the
inverse
mappings of strategies
\boldsymbol{s}^{-1}=\{s_{i}^{-1}\}_{i\in[n]}
and try to reconstruct the value distributions
\boldsymbol{V}=\{V_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
from (equilibrium) bid distributions.

Definition 2.10

(Inverses of strategies)
.

Consider bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}=\{B_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
given by value distributions
\boldsymbol{v}=(v_{i})_{i\in[n]}\sim\boldsymbol{V}
and a strategy profile
\boldsymbol{s}=\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
.
Each
random
inverse
s_{i}^{-1}(b)
for
b\in\mathrm{supp}(B_{i})
is defined as the
conditional
distribution
\{v_{i}\sim V_{i}\,\,\big|\,\big.\,s_{i}(v_{i})=b\}
.
So, each random inverse
s_{i}^{-1}(b_{i})
for
b_{i}\sim B_{i}
is identically distributed as the random value
v_{i}\sim V_{i}
.

Lemma

2.11
shows two basic properties of the random inverses
\boldsymbol{s}^{-1}=\{s_{i}^{-1}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

Lemma 2.11

(Inverses of strategies)
.

Almost surely over each random inverse
s_{i}^{-1}
:

1.

dichotomy:

s_{i}^{-1}(b)\leq\gamma
for any low bid
b\in\mathrm{supp}_{<\gamma}(B_{i})
.

dichotomy:

s_{i}^{-1}(b)>b>\gamma
for any normal bid
b\in\mathrm{supp}_{>\gamma}(B_{i})
.

2.

monotonicity:

s_{i}^{-1}(b)\geq s_{i}^{-1}(t)
for two boundary/normal bids
b,\,t\in\mathrm{supp}_{\geq\gamma}(B_{i})
that
b>t
.

Proof.

Under our definition of the
s_{i}^{-1}(b)
,
Item

1
follows directly from
Lemma

2.5
, while
Item

2
follows from a combination of
Lemma

2.5
and
Lemma

2.6
(
Item

2
).
∎

Below, we characterize (
Lemma

2.13
) the inverses of
equilibrium
strategies
s_{i}^{-1}(b)
for
normal
bids
b\in(\gamma,\,\lambda]
, using (
Definition

2.12
) the concept of
bid-to-value mappings

\varphi_{i}(b)
. We prove that the inverse
s_{i}^{-1}(b)
at a normal bid
b\in(\gamma,\,\lambda]
is essentially a
deterministic
value. (The inverses at the boundary bid
s_{i}^{-1}(\gamma)
are tricky and will be studied later.)

Definition 2.12

(Bid-to-value mappings)
.

Given equilibrium bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}=\{B_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
(cf.
Lemmas

2.7
and
2.9
), define each
bid-to-value mapping

\varphi_{i}(b)
for
b\in(\gamma,\,\lambda)
as follows:

\varphi_{i}(b)~:=~b+\frac{\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)}{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{-i}(b)}~=~b+\Big(\sum_{k\in[n]\setminus\{i\}}\frac{B^{\prime}_{k}(b)}{B_{k}(b)}\Big)^{-1}~=~b+\Big(\frac{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(b)}{\mathcal{B}(b)}-\frac{B^{\prime}_{i}(b)}{B_{i}(b)}\Big)^{-1}

It turns out that (
Item

2
of
Lemma

2.13
) each
\varphi_{i}(b)
is an increasing function, so the domain can be extended to include the both endpoints
\varphi_{i}(\gamma):=\lim_{b\searrow\gamma}\varphi_{i}(b)
and
\varphi_{i}(\lambda):=\lim_{b\nearrow\lambda}\varphi_{i}(b)
.

The above bid-to-value mapping is known in the literature (
[
HHT14
]
etc). However, it is usually defined only on the support of
B_{i}
. We define it and study its properties on the entire interval
[\gamma,\,\lambda]
, which is very important to our argument.

Lemma 2.13

(Normal bids)
.

For each bid-to-value mapping
\varphi_{i}(b)
, the following hold:

1.

invertibility:

s_{i}^{-1}(b)=\varphi_{i}(b)
almost surely over the random inverse
s_{i}^{-1}
, for any normal bid
b\in\mathrm{supp}_{>\gamma}(B_{i})
.
10
10

10

More rigorously, (
Corollaries

2.9
and
5
) we shall exclude a zero-measure set
\mathbb{D}\subseteq(\gamma,\,\lambda]
, namely the indifferentiable points of the
B_{i}(b)
’s

2.

monotonicity:

\varphi_{i}(b)
is weakly increasing on the closed interval
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
.

3.

rationality:

\varphi_{i}(b)>b
on the left-open right-closed interval
b\in(\gamma,\,\lambda]
.
Further,
\varphi_{i}(\gamma)\geq\gamma
.

Proof.

We safely assume
n\geq 2
. (Otherwise,
Lemma

2.13
is vacuously true since the unique bidder always bids zero
[\gamma,\,\lambda]=\{0\}
in any
First Price Auction
.)

Item

1
.

Consider a specific inverse/value
s_{i}^{-1}(b)=v
resulted from a normal bid
b\in\mathrm{supp}_{>\gamma}(B_{i})
. Regarding a normal bid
b^{*}>\gamma
, we can rewrite (
Lemma

2.4
(
Item

2
) and
Corollary

2.8
) the interim utility formula
u_{i}(v,\,b^{*})=(v-b^{*})\cdot\mathrm{x}_{i}(b^{*})=(v-b^{*})\cdot\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b^{*})
and deduce the partial derivative

\tfrac{\partial u_{i}}{\partial b^{*}}~=~-\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b^{*})+(v-b^{*})\cdot\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{-i}(b^{*})~=~\big(v-\varphi_{i}(b^{*})\big)\cdot\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{-i}(b^{*}).

Hence, to make (
Definition

2.3
) the equilibrium bid
b\in\mathrm{supp}_{>\gamma}(B_{i})
optimize the formula
u_{i}(v,\,b^{*})
, we must have
\varphi_{i}(b)=v=s_{i}^{-1}(b)
. Here we use the fact that
B^{\prime}_{-i}(b^{*})>0
since
B_{-i}
is strictly increasing (
Lemma

2.7
).
Thus,
Item

1
follows then.

Item

2
.

For brevity, we consider a
twice differentiable
11
11

11

In general, following the Lebesgue differentiation theorem
[
Leb04
]
, the
\mathcal{B}(b)
is twice differentiable almost everywhere on
(\gamma,\,\lambda)
, except a zero-measure set
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq(\gamma,\,\lambda)
that can be handled by standard tools from real analysis.
first-order bid CDF
\mathcal{B}(b)=B_{i}(b)\cdot\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
.
Thus for
i\in[n]
, each competing bid CDF
\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
is also twice differentiable and each bid-to-value mapping
\varphi_{i}(b)=b+\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)\big/\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{-i}(b)
is continuous on
b\in(\gamma,\,\lambda)
.

Following a combination of
Item

1
and
Lemma

2.11
(
Item

2
), each mapping
\varphi_{i}(b)
is increasing on this equilibrium bid distribution
B_{i}
’s normal bid support
\mathrm{supp}_{>\gamma}(B_{i})
, so the task is to extend this monotonicity to the whole interval
b\in(\gamma,\,\lambda)
.
The proof relies on
A
and
B
.

Fact A
.

A mapping
\varphi(b)=b+B(b)\big/B^{\prime}(b)
is increasing iff the reciprocal function
1\big/B(b)
is convex.

Proof.

Once again, we assume that the underlying CDF
B(b)
is twice differentiable.
The reciprocal function
1\big/B(b)
has the second derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}b^{2}}\big(\frac{1}{B}\big)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}b}\big(-\frac{B^{\prime}}{B^{2}}\big)=-\frac{B^{\prime\prime}\cdot B-2\cdot(B^{\prime})^{2}}{B^{2}}
.
So we can rewrite the first derivative of the mapping
\varphi^{\prime}(b)=1+\frac{(B^{\prime})^{2}-B\cdot B^{\prime\prime}}{(B^{\prime})^{2}}=\frac{B^{2}}{(B^{\prime})^{2}}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}b^{2}}\big(\frac{1}{B}\big)
.
Precisely, the mapping is increasing
\varphi^{\prime}(b)\geq 0
iff the reciprocal function is convex
\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}b^{2}}(\frac{1}{B})\geq 0
.
A
follows then.
∎

Fact B
.

The first-order mapping
\varphi_{\mathcal{B}}(b):=b+\mathcal{B}(b)\big/\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(b)
is increasing on
b\in(\gamma,\,\lambda)
.

Proof.

Based on
A
, it suffices to show that the first-order reciprocal function
\mathcal{R}(b):=1\big/\mathcal{B}(b)
is convex.
For brevity, we denote the equilibrium/competing reciprocal functions
R_{i}(b):=1\big/B_{i}(b)
and
\mathcal{R}_{-i}(b):=1\big/\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
for
i\in[n]
. Also, we may simply write
R_{i}=R_{i}(b)
etc.

By elementary algebra, each competing reciprocal function
\mathcal{R}_{-i}(b)=\prod_{k\in[n]\setminus\{i\}}R_{k}(b)
has the second derivative

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}_{-i}

\displaystyle~=~\sum_{k_{1}\neq k_{2}\neq i}\Big(R^{\prime}_{k_{1}}R^{\prime}_{k_{2}}\prod_{k_{3}\notin\{i,\,k_{1},\,k_{2}\}}R_{k_{3}}\Big)+\sum_{k\neq i}\Big(R^{\prime\prime}_{k}\prod_{k_{3}\notin\{i,\,k\}}R_{k_{3}}\Big)

\displaystyle~=~\mathcal{R}_{-i}\cdot\Big(\sum_{k_{1}\neq k_{2}\neq i}\tfrac{R^{\prime}_{k_{1}}R^{\prime}_{k_{2}}}{R_{k_{1}}R_{k_{2}}}+\sum_{k\neq i}\tfrac{R^{\prime\prime}_{k}}{R_{k}}\Big).\qquad\mbox{factor out $\mathcal{R}_{-i}=\prod_{k\neq i}R_{k}$}

We thus deduce that

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i\in[n]}R_{i}\cdot\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}_{-i}

\displaystyle~=~\tfrac{\mathcal{R}}{n-1}\sum_{i\in[n]}\Big(\sum_{k_{1}\neq k_{2}\neq i}\tfrac{R^{\prime}_{k_{1}}R^{\prime}_{k_{2}}}{R_{k_{1}}R_{k_{2}}}+\sum_{k\neq i}\tfrac{R^{\prime\prime}_{k}}{R_{k}}\Big)

R_{i}\cdot\mathcal{R}_{-i}=\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle~=~\tfrac{n-2}{n-1}\cdot\mathcal{R}\cdot\Big(\sum_{k_{1}\neq k_{2}}\tfrac{R^{\prime}_{k_{1}}R^{\prime}_{k_{2}}}{R_{k_{1}}R_{k_{2}}}\Big)~+~\mathcal{R}\cdot\Big(\sum_{k\in[n]}\tfrac{R^{\prime\prime}_{k}}{R_{k}}\Big).

combine the like terms

Moreover, the first-order reciprocal function
\mathcal{R}(b)=\prod_{k\in[n]}R_{k}(b)
has the second derivative

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle~=~\mathcal{R}\cdot\Big(\sum_{k_{1}\neq k_{2}}\tfrac{R^{\prime}_{k_{1}}R^{\prime}_{k_{2}}}{R_{k_{1}}R_{k_{2}}}+\sum_{k\in[n]}\tfrac{R^{\prime\prime}_{k}}{R_{k}}\Big)

à la the
\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}_{-i}
formulas

\displaystyle~=~\tfrac{\mathcal{R}}{n-1}\sum_{k_{1}\neq k_{2}}\tfrac{R^{\prime}_{k_{1}}R^{\prime}_{k_{2}}}{R_{k_{1}}R_{k_{2}}}+\tfrac{1}{n-1}\sum_{k\in[n]}R_{k}\cdot\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}_{-k}~\geq~0.

substitute
\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}_{-i}\cdot R_{i}

The first summation:
R^{\prime}_{k_{1}},\,R^{\prime}_{k_{2}}\leq 0
for
k_{1}\neq k_{2}\in[n]
, so the product
R^{\prime}_{k_{1}}R^{\prime}_{k_{2}}\geq 0
.

The second summation:
\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}_{-k}\geq 0
for
k\in[n]
. At the considered bid
b\in\mathrm{supp}_{>\gamma}(B_{i})
, each mapping is increasing
\varphi^{\prime}_{i}(b)\geq 0
(
Item

1
) and thus each reciprocal is convex
\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}_{-i}(b)\geq 0
(
A
).

Hence, the first-order reciprocal function
\mathcal{R}(b)
is convex on
b\in\mathrm{supp}_{>\gamma}(B_{i})
.

B
follows.
∎

Item

2
follows directly: On the normal bid support
b\in\mathrm{supp}_{>\gamma}(B_{i})
, the mapping
\varphi_{i}(b)=s_{i}^{-1}(b)
must be increasing (
Item

2
of
Lemma

2.11
).
Outside the support
b\in(\gamma,\,\lambda)\setminus\mathrm{supp}_{>\gamma}(B_{i})
, we have
\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)\big/\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{-i}(b)=\mathcal{B}(b)\big/\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(b)
, so the mapping
\varphi_{i}(b)=\varphi_{\mathcal{B}}(b)
is still increasing (
B
).

Item

3
.

Following
Lemma

2.7
, each competing bid distribution
\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
is a continuous and strictly increasing function on
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
.
Hence, by definition
\varphi_{i}(b)=b+\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)\big/\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{-i}(b)\geq b
; the equality is possible only at the boundary bid
b=\gamma
.
Item

3
follows then. This finishes the proof.
∎

As an implication of
Lemma

2.13
, the value distributions
\boldsymbol{V}=\{V_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
can partially be reconstructed from the equilibrium bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}=\{B_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
. (See
Remark

3
.)

Corollary 2.14

(Reconstructions)
.

For each
i\in[n]
, the
v\geq\varphi_{i}(\gamma)
part of the value distribution
V_{i}(v)=\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{v_{i}\sim V_{i}}\big[v_{i}\leq v\big]
can be reconstructed from the equilibrium bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}
as follows.

V_{i}(v)~=~\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{b_{i}\sim B_{i}}\big[(b_{i}\leq\gamma)\vee(\varphi_{i}(b_{i})\leq v)\big],\qquad\qquad\forall v\geq\varphi_{i}(\gamma).

In the statement of
Corollary

2.14
, a mapping
\varphi_{i}(b_{i})
is undefined when
b_{i}<\gamma
, but since the first condition
(b_{i}\leq\gamma)
already holds, we think of the disjunction
(b_{i}\leq\gamma)\vee(\varphi_{i}(b_{i})\leq v)
as being satisfied.

Figure 6:
Diagram of the reconstruction of a monopolist
h
’s value distributions
V_{h}
(
Definitions

2.15
and
2.18
).
Notice that
V_{i}(\gamma)\leq B_{i}(\gamma)\leq V_{i}(\varphi_{i}(\gamma))
.
By
Lemmas

2.5
,
2.6
and
2.13
:

(i) A normal value
v_{h}>\varphi_{h}(\gamma)
induces a normal bid
s_{h}(v_{h})>\gamma
.

(ii) The normal value
v_{h}=\varphi_{h}(\gamma)
induces a normal bid
s_{h}(v_{h})>\gamma
or the boundary bid
s_{h}(v_{h})=\gamma
, both of which are possible especially when there is a probability mass
\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{v_{h}\sim V_{h}}[v_{h}=\varphi_{h}(\gamma)]>0
.

(iii) A normal value
v_{h}\in(\gamma,\,\varphi_{h}(\gamma))
induces the boundary bid
s_{h}(v_{h})=\gamma
.

(iv) The boundary value
v_{h}=\gamma
induces a low bid
s_{h}(v_{h})<\gamma
or the boundary bid
s_{h}(v_{h})=\gamma
, both of which are possible especially when there is a probability mass
\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}[v_{h}=\gamma]>0
.

(v) A low value
v_{h}<\gamma
induces a bid
s_{h}(v_{h})
that yields the optimal zero utility
u_{h}(v_{h},\,s_{h}(v_{h}))=0
but is otherwise arbitrary; even an overbid
s_{h}(v_{h})>v_{h}
is fine.

Corollary

2.14
enables us to reconstruct the
v\geq\varphi_{i}(\gamma)
part of each value distribution
V_{i}
.
Then how about the
v<\varphi_{i}(\gamma)
part?
We shall consider two subparts separately.
(i) The low value
v<\gamma
subpart of a value distribution
V_{i}
cannot be reconstructed from the bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}
. That is, an equilibrium strategy
b_{i}=s_{i}(v)
for a low value
v<\gamma
is arbitrary as long as it yields the optimal zero utility
u_{i}(v,\,b_{i})=0
. Luckily, these low value
v<\gamma
subparts (
Lemmas

2.20
and
2.21
) turn out to have no contribution to neither of the auction/optimal
Social Welfares
, so we can ignore them.
(ii) The
\gamma\leq v<\varphi_{i}(\gamma)
subpart of a value distribution
V_{i}

has
contributions to the auction/optimal
Social Welfares
but also cannot be reconstructed.
This subpart always induces to the boundary bid
b_{i}=s_{i}(v)=\gamma
, following
2
(
Lemmas

2.5
and
2.13
).

As a remedy, we introduce the concept of
monopolists
(
Definition

2.15
); cf.
Figure

6
for a visual aid.
Here we notice that
V_{i}(\gamma)\leq B_{i}(\gamma)=\operatorname{{\bf Pr}}_{b_{i}\sim B_{i}}[b_{i}\leq\gamma]\leq V_{i}(\varphi_{i}(\gamma))
.
I.e., the first inequality holds since (
Lemma

2.5
) a normal bid
b_{i}>\gamma
induces a higher normal value
v_{i}=s_{i}^{-1}(b_{i})=\varphi_{i}(b_{i})>b_{i}>\gamma
.
The second inequality directly follows from
Corollary

2.14
.

Definition 2.15

(Monopolists)
.

A bidder
h\in[n]
is called a
monopolist
when the probabilities of taking normal bids/values are unequal
1-B_{h}(\gamma)<1-V_{h}(\gamma)
or equivalently, when the probability of taking the boundary bid yet a normal value is nonzero
\operatorname{{\bf Pr}}_{b_{h},\,s_{h}^{-1}}\big[(b_{h}=\gamma)\wedge(s_{h}^{-1}(b_{h})>\gamma)\big]>0
.

It is easier to understand this definition from the perspective of quantiles (
Remark

3
): A bidder
h\in[n]
is a monopolist when there are some quantiles
q
such that
V_{h}^{-1}(q)>\gamma
but
B_{h}^{-1}(q)=\gamma
.

Lemma

2.16
presents the properties about the monopolists.
(From the statement of
Lemma

2.16
, we can infer that there is NO monopolist when the first-order bid
\max(\boldsymbol{b})\sim\mathcal{B}
has no probability mass at the boundary bid
\mathcal{B}(\gamma)=0
.)

Lemma 2.16

(Monopolists)
.

There exists at most one monopolist
h
. If existential:

•

monopoly:

The probability of a boundary first-order bid
\big\{\max(\boldsymbol{b})=\gamma\big\}
is nonzero
\mathcal{B}(\gamma)>0
.
Conditioned on the tiebreaker
\big\{b_{h}=\max(\boldsymbol{b})=\gamma\big\}
, the monopolist wins
\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})=h
almost surely over the random allocation
\mathrm{x}
.

•

boundedness:

A boundary bid
b_{h}=\gamma
induces a bounded random value
s_{h}^{-1}(\gamma)\in[\gamma,\,\varphi_{h}(\gamma)]
.

Proof.

Suppose there is a monopolist
h\in[n]
.
We first verify
•
‣
2.16
and
•
‣
2.16
, and then prove that there is no other monopolist.

For some normal value
v_{h}=s_{h}^{-1}(b_{h})>\gamma
, (
Definitions

2.15
and
2.5
) the boundary bid
b_{h}=\gamma
yields the optimal utility
u_{h}(v_{h},\,\gamma)=(v_{h}-\gamma)\cdot\mathrm{x}_{h}(\gamma)=\mathcal{U}_{h}(v_{h})>0
, so the interim allocation is nonzero
\mathrm{x}_{h}(\gamma)>0
.
