The boundary first-order bid
\big\{\max(\boldsymbol{b})=\gamma\big\}
occurs with probability
\mathcal{B}(\gamma)=B_{h}(\gamma)\cdot\mathcal{B}_{-h}(\gamma)\geq\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{b_{h}}[b_{h}=\gamma]\cdot\mathrm{x}_{h}(\gamma)>0
.
Assume for contradiction that this monopolist
h\in[n]
loses the tiebreaker
\big\{b_{h}=\max(\boldsymbol{b})=\gamma\big\}
with a nonzero probability
>0
.
Based on the
4
, some (infinitesimally) higher deviation bid
b_{h}^{*}>b_{h}=\gamma
gives a strictly better utility
u_{h}(v_{h},\,b_{h}^{*})>u_{h}(v_{h},\,\gamma)>0
, which contradicts (
Definition

2.3
) the optimality of this equilibrium bid
b_{h}=\gamma
.
Reject our assumption: This monopolist
h\in[n]
always wins the tiebreaker
\big\{b_{h}=\max(\boldsymbol{b})=\gamma\big\}
(
•
‣
2.16
).

A boundary bid
b_{h}=\gamma
induces a bounded random value
\gamma\leq s_{h}^{-1}(\gamma)\leq\varphi_{h}(\gamma)
(
•
‣
2.16
). (i) This monopolist
h\in[n]
can never take a low value
s_{h}^{-1}(\gamma)<\gamma
. The tiebreaker
\big\{b_{h}=\max(\boldsymbol{b})=\gamma\big\}
occurs with a nonzero probability
>0
and, suppose so, the monopolist wins
\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})=h
almost surely over the random allocation
\mathrm{x}
.
Hence, a low value
s_{h}^{-1}(\gamma)<\gamma
together with a boundary bid
b_{h}=\gamma
yields a negative utility
<0
, which is impossible.
(ii) The value is also upper bounded
s_{h}^{-1}(\gamma)\leq\lim_{b\searrow\gamma}s_{h}^{-1}(b)=\lim_{b\searrow\gamma}\varphi_{h}(b)=\varphi_{h}(\gamma)
, given bidding monotonicity (
Item

2
of
Lemma

2.6
) and
1
(
Item

1
of
Lemma

2.13
).

This monopolist
h\in[n]
is the unique one. Otherwise, (
Definition

2.15
and
•
‣
2.16
) at least two monopolists
h\neq k\in[n]
tie for the boundary first-order bid
\big\{b_{h}=b_{k}=\max(\boldsymbol{b})=\gamma\big\}
with a nonzero probability
>0
and, suppose so, BOTH win
\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})=\{h,\,k\}
almost surely over the random allocation
\mathrm{x}
.
However, this is impossible because we are auctioning ONE item.

Lemma

2.16
follows then.
∎

Conceivably, we shall reconstruct the
\gamma\leq v<\varphi_{h}(\gamma)
subpart just for the value distribution
V_{h}
of the unique monopolist
h
(if existential) – We will show this later in
Lemma

2.18
. To reconstruct the
V_{h}
, we introduce (
Definition

2.17
) the concept of
conditional value distributions
.

Definition 2.17

(Conditional value distributions)
.

Regarding the monopolist
h
’s truncated random value
\max(s_{h}^{-1}(b_{h}),\,\gamma)
for
b_{h}\sim B_{h}
, define the
conditional value distribution

P(v)
as follows.

P(v)~:=~\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{b_{h}\sim B_{h},\,s_{h}^{-1}}\big[\,\max(s_{h}^{-1}(b_{h}),\,\gamma)\leq v\,\,\big|\,\big.\,b_{h}\leq\gamma\,\big],\qquad\qquad\forall v\geq 0.

In the case of no monopolist
h=\emptyset
, the
P(v):=\mathbbm{1}(v\geq\gamma)
always takes the boundary value
\gamma
.

Remark 6

(Conditional value distributions)
.

The random value
s_{h}^{-1}(b_{h})
for
b_{h}\sim B_{h}
exactly follows the distribution
V_{h}
.
With the help of
Figure

6
, we can see that the CDF
P(v)
is given by

\displaystyle P(v)~=~\begin{cases}0&\forall v\in[0,\,\gamma)\\
V_{h}(v)/B_{h}(\gamma)&\forall v\in[\gamma,\,\varphi_{h}(\gamma))\\
1&\forall v\in[\varphi_{h}(\gamma),\,+\infty]\end{cases}.

In general, the
P(v)
can be any
[\gamma,\,\varphi_{h}(\gamma)]
-supported distribution.
Given this extra information, we can reconstruct the value distributions
\boldsymbol{V}=\{V_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
(
Lemma

2.18
) except the unimportant low value
v<\gamma
parts.

Lemma 2.18

(Reconstructions)
.

For each
i\in[n]
, the
v\geq\gamma
part of the value distribution
V_{i}(v)=\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{v_{i}\sim V_{i}}\big[v_{i}\leq v\big]
can be reconstructed from the equilibrium bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}
plus the conditional value distribution
P
as follows.

•

V_{i}(v)=\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{b_{i}\sim B_{i}}\big[(b_{i}\leq\gamma)\vee(\varphi_{i}(b_{i})\leq v)\big]
in the case of a non-monopoly bidder
i\in[n]\setminus\{h\}
.

•

V_{h}(v)=P(v)\cdot\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{b_{h}\sim B_{h}}\big[(b_{h}\leq\gamma)\vee(\varphi_{h}(b_{h})\leq v)\big]
in the case of the monopolist
h
.

(a)

The monopolist
h

(b)

A non-monopoly bidder
i\in[n]\setminus\{h\}

Figure 7:
Diagram of (
Lemma

2.18
) the reconstruction of the value distributions
\boldsymbol{V}
.

Proof.

See
Figure

7
for a visual aid.
Regarding a non-monopoly bidder
i\in[n]\setminus\{h\}
, (
Lemmas

2.5
and
2.15
; cf.
Figure

7b
) a normal bid
>\gamma
induces a normal value
>\gamma
and vice versa.
By
2
, such a normal value
v_{i}=\varphi_{i}(b_{i})
for
b_{i}>\gamma
is at least
v_{i}\geq\varphi_{i}(\gamma)
. Hence, on the OPEN interval
v\in(\gamma,\,\varphi_{i}(\gamma))
, the value distribution
V_{i}(v)
has no density and keeps a constant function
V_{i}(v)=B_{i}(\gamma)
. Given these, the reconstruction in
Corollary

2.14
can be generalized naively: For
v\in[\gamma,\,\varphi_{i}(\gamma))
, we have
V_{i}(v)=B_{i}(\gamma)=\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{b_{i}\sim B_{i}}\big[b_{i}\leq\gamma\big]=\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{b_{i}\sim B_{i}}\big[(b_{i}\leq\gamma)\vee(\varphi_{i}(b_{i})\leq v\big]
.

Further, we can check the claimed “monopolist” formula
V_{h}(v)
for
v\in[\gamma,\,\varphi_{i}(\gamma))
(cf.
Figure

7a
):

P(v)\cdot\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{b_{h}\sim B_{h}}\big[(b_{h}\leq\gamma)\vee(\varphi_{h}(b_{h})\leq v)\big]=P(v)\cdot\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{b_{h}\sim B_{h}}\big[b_{h}\leq\gamma\big]=P(v)\cdot B_{h}(\gamma)=V_{h}(v)
, where the first step again leverages
2
.
This finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma

2.19
will be useful for formulating the expected auction/optimal
Social Welfares
.

Lemma 2.19

(
Social Welfares
)
.

Conditioned on the boundary first-order bid
\big\{\max(\boldsymbol{b})=\gamma\big\}
, each of the following exactly follows the conditional value distribution
P
:

•

The conditional auction
Social Welfare

\big\{s_{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})}^{-1}(\gamma)\,\big|\,\big.\max(\boldsymbol{b})=\gamma\big\}
.

•

The conditional optimal
Social Welfare

\big\{\max(\boldsymbol{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{b}))\,\big|\,\big.\max(\boldsymbol{b})=\gamma\big\}
.

Proof.

The random events
\big\{\max(\boldsymbol{b})=\gamma\big\}
and
\big\{\max(\boldsymbol{b})\leq\gamma\big\}
are identical, regarding the infimum first-order bid
\gamma=\inf(\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{B}))
.
Conditioned on this, (i) a non-monopoly bidder
i\in[n]\setminus\{h\}
has a low/boundary bid
b_{i}\leq\gamma
and (
Lemmas

2.5
and
2.15
) a low/boundary value
s_{i}^{-1}(b_{i})\leq\gamma
; while
(ii) the monopolist
h
has EITHER a low bid
b_{h}<\gamma
and a low/boundary value
s_{h}^{-1}(b_{h})\leq\gamma
, OR the boundary bid
b_{h}=\gamma
and (
Lemma

2.16
) a boundary/normal value
s_{h}^{-1}(b_{h})\in[\gamma,\,\varphi_{h}(\gamma)]
.

The allocated bidder
\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})\in[n]
always take the boundary bid
b_{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})}=\gamma
and, to make the utility nonnegative, a boundary/normal value
s_{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})}^{-1}(b_{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})})=s_{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})}^{-1}(\gamma)\geq\gamma
; the case of a normal value
>\gamma
occurs only if the monopolist is allocated
\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})=h
.
Hence, the conditional optimal
Social Welfare
is identically distributed as each of the following:

\displaystyle\big\{\max(\boldsymbol{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{b}))\,\big|\,\big.\max(\boldsymbol{b})=\gamma\big\}

\displaystyle~\overset{\tt d}{=}~\big\{\max(\boldsymbol{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{b}))\,\big|\,\big.\max(\boldsymbol{b})\leq\gamma\big\}

\displaystyle\gamma=\inf(\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{B}))

\displaystyle~\overset{\tt d}{=}~\big\{\max(\boldsymbol{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{b}),\,\gamma)\,\big|\,\big.\max(\boldsymbol{b})\leq\gamma\big\}

\displaystyle s_{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})}^{-1}(b_{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})})\geq\gamma

\displaystyle~\overset{\tt d}{=}~\big\{\max(s_{h}^{-1}(b_{h}),\,\gamma)\,\big|\,\big.\max(\boldsymbol{b})\leq\gamma\big\}

s_{i}^{-1}(b_{i})\leq\gamma
for
i\neq h

\displaystyle~\overset{\tt d}{=}~\big\{\max(s_{h}^{-1}(b_{h}),\,\gamma)\,\big|\,\big.b_{h}\leq\gamma\big\}.

independence

That is, the conditional optimal
Social Welfare
follows (
Definition

2.17
) the distribution
P
.
Further, since the monopolist
h
(as the only possible bidder that has a normal value
>\gamma
) always wins the tiebreaker
\big\{b_{h}=\max(\boldsymbol{b})=\gamma\big\}
(
Lemma

2.16
), the conditional auction/optimal
Social Welfares
are identically distributed
\big\{s_{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})}^{-1}(\gamma)\,\big|\,\big.\max(\boldsymbol{b})=\gamma\big\}\overset{\tt d}{=}\big\{\max(\boldsymbol{s}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{b}))\,\big|\,\big.\max(\boldsymbol{b})=\gamma\big\}
, which again follow the distribution
P
.
This finishes the proof.
∎

2.3
Reformulation for the
Price of Anarchy
problem

To address the
Price of Anarchy
problem, we shall formulate the expected auction/optimal
Social Welfares

{\sf FPA}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s},\,\mathrm{x})
and
{\sf OPT}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s},\,\mathrm{x})
.
Indeed, these can be written in terms of the equilibrium bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}
plus the conditional value distribution
P
(
Definition

2.17
).

First,
Lemma

2.20
formulates the expected auction
Social Welfare

{\sf FPA}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s},\,\mathrm{x})
.

Lemma 2.20

(Auction
Social Welfare
)
.

The expected auction
Social Welfare

{\sf FPA}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s},\,\mathrm{x})
can be formulated based on the conditional value distribution and the equilibrium bid distributions
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})
:

\displaystyle{\sf FPA}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s},\,\mathrm{x})~=~{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})~=~\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[P]\cdot\mathcal{B}(\gamma)+\sum_{i\in[n]}\bigg(\int_{\gamma}^{\lambda}\varphi_{i}(b)\cdot\frac{B^{\prime}_{i}(b)}{B_{i}(b)}\cdot\mathcal{B}(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b\bigg).

where the first-order bid distribution
\mathcal{B}(b)=\prod_{i\in[n]}B_{i}(b)
and the bid-to-value mappings
\varphi_{i}(b)
can be computed from the bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}=\{B_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
(
Definition

2.12
).

Proof.

In any realization, the outcome auction
Social Welfare
is the allocated bidder’s value
v_{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))}
. Over the randomness of the values
\boldsymbol{v}=(v_{i})_{i\in[n]}\sim\boldsymbol{V}
, the equilibrium strategies
\boldsymbol{s}=\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, and the allocation rule
\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))
, the expected auction
Social Welfare

{\sf FPA}
is given by

\displaystyle{\sf FPA}~=~\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{v},\,\boldsymbol{s},\,\mathrm{x}}\big[\,v_{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))}\,\big]

\displaystyle~=~\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{v},\,\boldsymbol{s},\,\mathrm{x}}\big[\,v_{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(\max(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))=\gamma)\,\big]

(B)

\displaystyle\phantom{~=~}+\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{v},\,\boldsymbol{s},\,\mathrm{x}}\big[\,v_{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(\max(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))>\gamma)\,\big].\hskip 13.37282pt

(N)

Here we used the fact that the first-order bid
\max(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))\sim\mathcal{B}
is at least
\gamma=\inf(\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{B}))
.

For Term (
B
), we deduce that

Term (
B
)

\displaystyle~=~\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{v},\,\boldsymbol{s},\,\mathrm{x}}\big[\,v_{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(\max(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))=\gamma)\,\big]

\displaystyle~=~\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{v},\,\boldsymbol{s},\,\mathrm{x}}\big[\,v_{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))}\,\,\big|\,\big.\,\max(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))=\gamma\,\big]\cdot\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{\boldsymbol{v},\,\boldsymbol{s}}\big[\,\max(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))=\gamma\,\big]

\displaystyle~=~\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[P]\cdot\mathcal{B}(\gamma).

The last equality uses the fact that conditioned on the boundary first-order bid
\big\{\max(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))=\gamma\big\}
, the allocated bidder
\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))
’s value follows the distribution
P
(
Lemma

2.19
).

For Term (
N
), we deduce that

Term (
N
)

\displaystyle~=~\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{v},\,\boldsymbol{s},\,\mathrm{x}}\big[\,v_{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(\max(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{v}))>\gamma)\,\big]\phantom{\bigg.}

\displaystyle~=~\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{b},\,\boldsymbol{s}^{-1},\,\mathrm{x}}\big[\,s_{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})}^{-1}(b_{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})})\cdot\mathbbm{1}(\max(\boldsymbol{b})>\gamma)\,\big]\phantom{\bigg.}

(N1)

\displaystyle~=~\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{b},\,s_{i}^{-1},\,\mathrm{x}}\big[\,s_{i}^{-1}(b_{i})\cdot\mathbbm{1}(\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})=i)\cdot\mathbbm{1}(b_{i}>\gamma)\,\big]\phantom{\bigg.}\hskip 46.37813pt

(N2)

\displaystyle~=~\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{b_{i}}\big[\,\varphi_{i}(b_{i})\cdot\mathrm{x}_{i}(b_{i})\cdot\mathbbm{1}(b_{i}>\gamma)\,\big]\phantom{\bigg.}

(N3)

\displaystyle~=~\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{b_{i}}\big[\,\varphi_{i}(b_{i})\cdot\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b_{i})\cdot\mathbbm{1}(b_{i}>\gamma)\,\big]\phantom{\bigg.}

(N4)

\displaystyle~=~\sum_{i\in[n]}\bigg(\int_{\gamma}^{\lambda}\varphi_{i}(b)\cdot\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)\cdot B^{\prime}_{i}(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b\bigg)

\displaystyle~=~\sum_{i\in[n]}\bigg(\int_{\gamma}^{\lambda}\varphi_{i}(b)\cdot\frac{B^{\prime}_{i}(b)}{B_{i}(b)}\cdot\mathcal{B}(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b\bigg).

(N5)

(
N1
): Redenote
b_{i}=s_{i}(v_{i})\sim B_{i}
for
i\in[n]
. Recall
Definition

2.10
that each random inverse
s_{i}^{-1}(b_{i})
for
b_{i}\sim B_{i}
is identically distributed as the random value
v_{i}\sim V_{i}
.

(
N2
): Divide the event
\big\{\max(\boldsymbol{b})>\gamma\big\}
into subevents
\big\{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})=i~\text{and}~b_{i}=\max(\boldsymbol{b})>\gamma\big\}
for
i\in[n]
.

(
N3
):
Lemma

2.13
(
Item

1
) that
s_{i}^{-1}(b)=\varphi_{i}(b)
for any normal bid
b\in\mathrm{supp}_{>\gamma}(B_{i})

and
Definition

2.2
that
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)=\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{\boldsymbol{b}_{-i},\,\mathrm{x}}\big[\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b}_{-i},\,b)=i\big]=\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{b}_{-i},\,\mathrm{x}}\big[\mathbbm{1}(\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b}_{-i},\,b)=i)\big]
.

(
N4
):
Corollary

2.8
that
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)=\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
for any normal bid
b>\gamma
.

(
N5
): The first-order bid distribution
\mathcal{B}(b)=B_{i}(b)\cdot\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
.

Combining Terms (
B
) and (
N
) together finishes the proof of
Lemma

2.20
.
∎

Moreover,
Lemma

2.21
formulates the expected optimal
Social Welfare

{\sf OPT}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s},\,\mathrm{x})
.

Lemma 2.21

(Optimal
Social Welfare
)
.

The expected optimal
Social Welfare

{\sf OPT}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s},\,\mathrm{x})
can be formulated based on the conditional value distribution and the equilibrium bid distributions
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})
:

\displaystyle{\sf OPT}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s},\,\mathrm{x})~=~{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})~=~\gamma+\int_{\gamma}^{+\infty}\Big(1-P(v)\cdot\prod_{i\in[n]}\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{b_{i}}\big[b_{i}\leq\gamma\vee\varphi_{i}(b_{i})\leq v\big]\Big)\cdot\mathrm{d}v.

Proof.

This proof simply follows from
Lemma

2.18
, i.e., we essentially reconstruct the value distributions
\boldsymbol{V}=\{V_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
from the tuple
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})
.

In any realization, the outcome optimal
Social Welfare
is the first-order value
\max(\boldsymbol{v})
. Over the randomness of the values
\boldsymbol{v}=(v_{i})_{i\in[n]}\sim\boldsymbol{V}
, the expected optimal
Social Welfare

{\sf OPT}
is given by

\displaystyle{\sf OPT}

\displaystyle~=~\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{v}}\big[\,\max(\boldsymbol{v})\,\big]~=~\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{v}}\big[\,\max(\boldsymbol{v},\,\gamma)\,\big]\phantom{\bigg.}

(O1)

\displaystyle~=~\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big(1-\prod_{i\in[n]}V_{i}(v)\cdot\mathbbm{1}(v\geq\gamma)\Big)\cdot\mathrm{d}v

(O2)

\displaystyle~=~\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big(1-\Big(1-P(v)\cdot\prod_{i\in[n]}\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{b_{i}}\big[b_{i}\leq\gamma\vee\varphi_{i}(b_{i})\leq v\big]\cdot\mathbbm{1}(v\geq\gamma)\Big)\cdot\mathrm{d}v

(O3)

\displaystyle~=~\gamma+\int_{\gamma}^{+\infty}\Big(1-P(v)\cdot\prod_{i\in[n]}\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{b_{i}}\big[b_{i}\leq\gamma\vee\varphi_{i}(b_{i})\leq v\big]\Big)\cdot\mathrm{d}v.

(
O1
): The first-order value
\max(\boldsymbol{v})
for
\boldsymbol{v}\sim\boldsymbol{V}
is always boundary/normal
\geq\gamma
.

(
O2
): The expectation of a
nonnegative
distribution
F
is given by
\operatorname{{\bf E}}[F]=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\big(1-F(v)\big)\cdot\mathrm{d}v
.

(
O3
): Apply
Lemma

2.18
. It also holds in case that no monopolist exists since, suppose so, we have
P(v)=1
for
v\geq\gamma
.
This finishes the proof of
Lemma

2.21
.
∎

The above
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})
-based
Social Welfare
formulas
{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})
and
{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})
, together with the characterizations of
P
and
\boldsymbol{B}
, are the foundation of the whole paper. From all these discussions, we notice that the below-
\gamma
parts of bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}
and value distributions
\boldsymbol{V}
are less important. To stress this observation, we have the key definition of
valid instances

(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
.

Definition 2.22

(Valid instances)
.

A
valid
instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
, where
\boldsymbol{B}=\{B_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
and
n\geq 2
, is given by
n+1
independent distributions and satisfies the following.

•

The bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}=\{B_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
have a common
infimum bid

\inf(\mathrm{supp}(B_{i}))=\gamma\in[0,\,+\infty)
and a universal
supremum bid

\max_{i\in[n]}\sup(\mathrm{supp}(B_{i}))=\lambda\in[\gamma,\,+\infty]
.

•

The bid CDF’s
B_{i}(b)
for
i\in[n]
are
continuous
functions on
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
, while the competing bid CDF’s
\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)=\prod_{k\in[n]\setminus\{i\}}B_{k}(b)
for
i\in[n]
and the first-order bid CDF
\mathcal{B}(b)=\prod_{i\in[n]}B_{i}(b)
are
continuous
and
strictly increasing
functions on
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
.

•

monotonicity:

The bid-to-value mappings
\varphi_{i}(b):=b+\frac{\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)}{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{-i}(b)}=b+\big(\sum_{k\in[n]\setminus\{i\}}\frac{B^{\prime}_{k}(b)}{B_{k}(b)}\big)^{-1}
for
i\in[n]
are
weakly increasing
functions on
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
.

•

monopoly:

The index-
1
bidder
B_{1}
without loss of generality is the unique
monopolist
.
12
12

12

This accommodates the “no monopolist” case of
Lemma

2.16
, by taking
P
as a deterministic value of
\gamma
.
I.e., this bidder always wins
\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})\equiv 1
in the all-infimum tiebreaker
\{\boldsymbol{b}=\gamma^{\otimes n}\}
.

•

boundedness:

Under the infimum bid
\gamma
, the monopolist
B_{1}
’s conditional value
s_{1}^{-1}(\gamma)\sim P
is bounded as
\mathrm{supp}(P)\subseteq[\gamma,\,\varphi_{1}(\gamma)]
, while the other bidders’ conditional values are truthful,
s_{i}^{-1}(\gamma)\equiv\gamma
for
i\in[2:n]
.

The valid instances
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
capture nearly all
Bayesian Nash Equilibria

\big\{\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\mathrm{x})\big\}
. Essentially, there is a bijection between these two spaces (without considering some less important details about
Bayesian Nash Equilibria
), especially in a sense of the
Social Welfare
invariant.
This is formalized into
Theorem

7
.

Theorem 7

(Valid instances)
.

(I) For any valid instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
, there is some equilibrium
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\mathrm{x})\neq\emptyset
, for some value distribution
\boldsymbol{V}=\{V_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, whose conditional value distribution and bid distribution are exactly the
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})
.
(II) For any value distribution
\boldsymbol{V}=\{V_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
and any equilibrium
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\mathrm{x})\neq\emptyset
,
there is some valid instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
that yields the same auction/optimal
Social Welfares

{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})={\sf FPA}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s},\,\mathrm{x})
and
{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})={\sf OPT}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\boldsymbol{s},\,\mathrm{x})
.

(a)

The monopolist
h=B_{1}

(b)

A non-monopoly bidder
B_{i}
for
i\in[2:n]

Figure 8:
Diagram of valid instances
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
and
Theorem

7
.

Proof.

See
Figure

8
for a visual aid. We prove Items (I) and (II) separately.

(I) Given a valid instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
, we can construct
the boundary/normal value
v\geq\gamma
part of some value distribution
\boldsymbol{V}=\{V_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, following
Lemma

2.18
.
For the low value
v<\gamma
part, we simply let
V_{i}(v)\equiv 0
on
v<\gamma
, namely putting all the undecided probabilities to the boundary value
\gamma
. Then the value distribution
\boldsymbol{V}=\{V_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
is well defined.

Since we zero-out everything below
\gamma
, the equivalence in
Remark

3
can be extend to the entire quantile space
q\in[0,1]
. Hence, we can construct the equilibrium by identifying the quantiles for value distributions and bid distributions.
Consider the quantile bid/value functions
\{B_{i}^{-1}\}_{i\in[n]}
and
\{V_{i}^{-1}\}_{i\in[n]}
.
As
Figure

8
shows, we construct the strategy profile
\boldsymbol{s}=\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
as follows: For
i\in[n]
and
v\in\mathrm{supp}(V_{i})
, let
q\sim U[0,\,1]
be a uniform random quantile, then

\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{s_{i}}\big[\,s_{i}(v)=b\,\big]~=~\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{q\,\sim\,U[0,\,1]}\big[\,B_{i}^{-1}(q)=b\,\,\big|\,\big.\,V_{i}^{-1}(q)=v\,\big],\qquad\qquad\forall b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda].

We conclude Item (I) with verifying the equilibrium conditions (
Definition

2.3
).

Fact A
.

u_{i}(v,\,s_{i}(v))\geq u_{i}(v,\,b^{*})
almost surely over the random strategy
s_{i}
, for each bidder
i\in[n]
, any value
v\in\mathrm{supp}(V_{i})
, and any deviation bid
b^{*}\geq 0
.

Proof.

First, the monopolist
B_{1}
(
Corollaries

2.8
,
2.15
and
2.16
) has the allocation
\mathrm{x}_{1}(b)=\mathcal{B}_{-1}(b)
for a boundary/normal bid
b\geq\gamma
and
\mathrm{x}_{1}(b)=0
for a low
b\in[0,\,\gamma)
.

(i)
A Normal Bid
s_{1}(v)\in(\gamma,\,\lambda]
.

The underlying value
v=\varphi_{1}(s_{1}(v))>s_{1}(v)>\gamma
, by construction and
Lemmas

2.18
and
2.13
.
Accordingly, the current allocation/utility are positive
u_{1}(v,\,s_{1}(v))=(v-s_{1}(v))\cdot\mathcal{B}_{-1}(s_{1}(v))>0
.
Then a low deviation bid
b^{*}<\gamma
is suboptimal, because the deviated allocation/utility are zero
u_{1}(v,\,b^{*})=(v-b^{*})\cdot\mathrm{x}_{1}(b^{*})=0
.
Moreover, a boundary/normal deviation bid
b^{*}\geq\gamma
is suboptimal – The current bid
s_{1}(v)>\gamma
maximizes the utility formula
u_{1}(v,\,b^{*})=(v-b^{*})\cdot\mathcal{B}_{-1}(b^{*})
, because the partial derivative
\frac{\partial u_{1}}{\partial b^{*}}=\big(v-\varphi_{1}(b^{*})\big)\cdot\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{-1}(b^{*})
, the underlying value
v=\varphi_{1}(s_{1}(v))
, and the mapping
\varphi_{1}(b^{*})
is increasing (
Item

2
of
Lemma

2.13
).

(ii)
A Boundary Bid
s_{1}(v)=\gamma
.

The underlying value
v=s_{1}^{-1}(\gamma)
exactly follows the conditional value distribution
P
and ranges within
\mathrm{supp}(P)\subseteq[\gamma,\,\varphi_{1}(\gamma)]
, by construction and
Definitions

2.17
and
2.22
.
Reusing the above arguments (i.e., the utility formula
u_{1}(v,\,b^{*})
is decreasing for
b^{*}\geq\gamma
),
we can easily see that the current bid
s_{1}(v)=\gamma
is optimal.
In sum, the monopolist
B_{1}
meets the equilibrium conditions.

Each non-monopoly bidder
i\in[2:n]
(
Corollaries

2.8
,
2.15
and
2.16
) has the allocation
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)=\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)
for a normal bid
b>\gamma
and
\mathrm{x}_{i}(b)=0
for a low/boundary bid
b\in[0,\,\gamma]
.

(i)
A Normal Bid
s_{i}(v)\in(\gamma,\,\lambda]
.

Reusing the above arguments for the monopolist
B_{1}
,
we can easily see that the current normal bid
s_{i}(v)\in(\gamma,\,\lambda]
is optimal.

(ii)
A Boundary Bid
s_{i}(v)=\gamma
.

The underlying value must be the boundary value
v=s_{i}^{-1}(\gamma)\equiv\gamma
, by construction and
Definitions

2.17
and
2.22
.
Obviously, the current
zero
utility
u_{i}(v,\,s_{i}(v))=0
and the current
boundary
bid
s_{i}(v)=\gamma
are optimal.
In sum, each non-monopoly bidder
i\in[2:n]
meets the equilibrium conditions.

A
follows then.
∎

(II) Given an equilibrium
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{BNE}(\boldsymbol{V},\,\mathrm{x})\neq\emptyset
, as before, consider the bid distribution
\boldsymbol{B}^{(\boldsymbol{s})}
, the conditional value distribution
P^{(\boldsymbol{s})}
, and the infimum/supremum first-order bids
\gamma=\inf(\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{B}^{(\boldsymbol{s})}))
and
\lambda=\sup(\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{B}^{(\boldsymbol{s})}))
.
Following
Lemmas

2.7
,
2.13
,
2.16
and
2.17
, this instance
(P^{(\boldsymbol{s})},\,\boldsymbol{B}^{(\boldsymbol{s})})
is almost valid, except that
the distribution
\boldsymbol{B}^{(\boldsymbol{s})}=\{B_{i}^{(\boldsymbol{s})}\}_{i\in[n]}
can be supported on low bids
b<\gamma
. (Recall that the distribution
P^{(\boldsymbol{s})}
is just supported on boundary/high values
v\geq\gamma
.)
By truncating the bid distribution
B_{i}(b)\equiv B_{i}^{(\boldsymbol{s})}(b)\cdot\mathbbm{1}(b\geq\gamma)
for
i\in[n]
and reusing the conditional value distribution
P\equiv P^{(\boldsymbol{s})}
, we obtain a valid instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
.
In particular, the truncation
B_{i}(b)\equiv B_{i}^{(\boldsymbol{s})}(b)\cdot\mathbbm{1}(b\geq\gamma)
does not modify the auction/optimal
Social Welfares
(cf.
Lemmas

2.20
and
2.21
).
This finishes the proof of Item (II).
∎

We conclude this section with (
Corollary

2.23
) an equivalent definition for
Price of Anarchy
in
First Price Auctions
, as an implication of
Theorem

7
.

Corollary 2.23

(
Price of Anarchy
)
.

Regarding
First Price Auctions
, the
Price of Anarchy
is given by

\displaystyle{\sf PoA}~=~\inf\bigg\{\,\frac{{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})}{{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})}\,\,\bigg|\,\bigg.\,(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}~\text{and}~{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})<+\infty\,\bigg\}.

3
Preprocessing Valid Instances

This section presents several preparatory reductions towards the potential worst-case instances.
In
Section

3.1
, we introduce the concept of valid
pseudo
instances, which generalizes valid instances and eases the presentation.
In
Section

3.2
, we
discretize
an instance up to any
PoA
-error
\varepsilon>0
, simplifying the bid-to-value mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
to a (finite-size) bid-to-value table
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
.
In
Section

3.3
, we
translate
the instances, making the infimum bids zero
\gamma=0
.
In
Section

3.4
, we

layer
the bid-to-value mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
, making the table
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
decreasing bidder-by-bidder.
In
Section

3.5
, we

derandomize
the conditional value
P
to either the
floor
value
P^{\downarrow}\equiv 0
or the
ceiling
value
P^{\uparrow}\equiv\varphi_{1}(0)
.

To conclude (
Section

3.6
),
we restrict the space of the worst cases to the
floor
/
ceiling
pseudo instances, which can be represented just by the table
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
.
These materials serve as the basis for the more complicated reductions later in
Section

4
.

3.1
The concept of valid pseudo instances

This subsection introduces the concept of valid
pseudo
instances
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
, a natural extension of valid
real
instances
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})
from
Definition

2.22
.
Given a valid real instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})
, we can always (
Lemma

3.5
) construct a valid pseudo instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
that yields the same auction/optimal
Social Welfares
. Thus towards a lower bound on the
Price of Anarchy
, we can consider valid pseudo instances instead (
Corollary

3.6
).

Roughly speaking, a pseudo instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
includes one more
pseudo bidder

L
to a real instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})
. To clarify this difference, we often use the letter
i\in[n]
and its variants for real bidders, and the Greek letter
\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}
and its variants for real or pseudo bidders. Without ambiguity, we redenote by
\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
the space of valid pseudo instances. The formal definition of
valid
pseudo instances is given below; cf.
Definition

2.22
for comparison.

Definition 3.1

(Valid pseudo instances)
.

A
valid
instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
, where
\boldsymbol{B}=\{B_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
and
n\geq 1
, is given by
n+2
independent distributions and satisfies the following.

•

The real bidders
B_{i}
for
i\in[n]
each compete with other bidders
\boldsymbol{B}_{-i}\otimes L
, while the pseudo bidder
L
competes with all bidders
\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L
, including him/herself
L
.

•

The bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}=\{B_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
have a common
infimum bid

\inf(\mathrm{supp}(B_{\sigma}))=\gamma\in[0,\,+\infty)
and a universal
supremum bid

\max_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}\sup(\mathrm{supp}(B_{\sigma}))=\lambda\in[\gamma,\,+\infty]
.

•

The bid CDF’s
B_{\sigma}(b)
for
\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}
are
continuous
functions on
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
, while the competing bid CDF’s
\mathcal{B}_{-i}(b)=\prod_{k\in[n]\setminus\{i\}}B_{k}(b)\cdot L(b)
for
i\in[n]
and the first-order bid CDF
\mathcal{B}(b)=\prod_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}B_{\sigma}(b)
are
continuous
and
strictly increasing
functions on
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
.

(The
\mathcal{B}(b)
is exactly the pseudo bidder
L
’s competing bid CDF.)

•

monotonicity:

The bid-to-value mappings
\varphi_{\sigma}(b):=b+\big(\frac{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(b)}{\mathcal{B}(b)}-\frac{B^{\prime}_{\sigma}(b)}{B_{\sigma}(b)}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(\sigma\neq L)\big)^{-1}=\\
b+\big(\sum_{k\in[n]\setminus\{\sigma\}}\frac{B^{\prime}_{k}(b)}{B_{k}(b)}+\frac{L^{\prime}(b)}{L(b)}\big)^{-1}
for
\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}
are
weakly increasing
functions on
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
.

•

monopoly:

The index-
1
bidder
B_{1}
without loss of generality is the unique
monopolist
. I.e., this bidder always wins
\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b})\equiv 1
in the all-infimum tiebreaker
\{\boldsymbol{b}=\gamma^{\otimes n+1}\}
.

•

boundedness:

Under the infimum bid
\gamma
, the monopolist
B_{1}
’s conditional value
s_{1}^{-1}(\gamma)\sim P
is bounded as
\mathrm{supp}(P)\subseteq[\gamma,\,\varphi_{1}(\gamma)]
, while the other bidders’ conditional values are truthful,
s_{\sigma}^{-1}(\gamma)\equiv\gamma
for
\sigma\in[2:n]\cup\{L\}
.

Remark 8

(Pseudo instances)
.

The concept of pseudo instances greatly eases the presentation and similar ideas also appear in the previous works
[
AHN+19
,
JLTX20
,
JLQ+19
,
JJLZ21
]
.

Essentially, a pseudo instance
\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L
can be viewed as the limit of a sequence of real instances. I.e., the pseudo bidder
L
can be viewed as, in the limit
k\to+\infty
, a collection of
low-impact
bidders
L_{1}(b)=\dots=L_{k}(b):=(L(b))^{1/k}
.
All these low-impact bidders have the
common
competing bid distribution
\mathfrak{B}(b)=\prod_{i\in[n]}B_{i}(b)\cdot(L(b))^{1-1/k}
, so the highest bid among them leads to the highest value and we just need to keep track of the collective information
\prod_{j\in[k]}L_{j}=L(b)
.

On the other hand, in the limit
k\to+\infty
, the common competing bid distribution
\mathfrak{B}(b)
pointwise converges to the first-order bid distribution
\mathcal{B}(b)=\prod_{i\in[n]}B_{i}(b)\cdot L(b)
.
This accounts for the pseudo mapping
\varphi_{L}(b)=b+\mathcal{B}(b)/\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(b)
.

Because valid pseudo instances
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
differ from the valid real instances ONLY in the definition of the pseudo mapping
\varphi_{L}(b)
, most results in
Section

2
still hold. In particular,

Lemma

3.2
formulates their
Social Welfares
(which restates
Lemmas

2.20
and
2.21
basically).

Lemma 3.2

(
Social Welfares
)
.

For a valid pseudo instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
, the expected auction/ optimal
Social Welfares

{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
and
{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
are given by

\displaystyle{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)\;

\displaystyle~=~\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[P]\cdot\mathcal{B}(\gamma)~+~\sum_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}\Big(\int_{\gamma}^{\lambda}\varphi_{\sigma}(b)\cdot\frac{B^{\prime}_{\sigma}(b)}{B_{\sigma}(b)}\cdot\mathcal{B}(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b\Big),

\displaystyle{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)

\displaystyle~=~\gamma~+~\int_{\gamma}^{+\infty}\Big(1-P(v)\cdot\mathcal{V}(v)\Big)\cdot\mathrm{d}v,

where the first-order bid distribution
\mathcal{B}(b):=\prod_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}B_{\sigma}(b)
and the quasi first-order value distribution
\mathcal{V}(v):=\prod_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{b_{\sigma}\sim B_{\sigma}}\big[\,(b_{\sigma}\leq\gamma)\vee(\varphi_{\sigma}(b_{\sigma})\leq v)\,\big]
can be reconstructed from
\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L
.

Lemma

3.3
shows that given a pseudo instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
, the pseudo bidder
L
always has a
dominated
bid-to-value mapping
\varphi_{L}(b)\leq\varphi_{i}(b)
, compared with other real bidders
i\in[n]
.

Lemma 3.3

(Bid-to-value mappings)
.

For a valid pseudo instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
, the bid-to-value mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\{\varphi_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
satisfy the following:

1.

\min(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(b^{\otimes n+1}))=\varphi_{L}(b)>b
for
b\in(\gamma,\,\lambda]
. Further,
\min(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\gamma^{\otimes n+1}))=\varphi_{L}(\gamma)\geq\gamma
.

2.

\sum_{i\in[n]}\frac{\varphi_{L}(b)-b}{\varphi_{i}(b)-b}\geq n-1
for
b\in(\gamma,\,\lambda]
.

Proof.

Item

1
is an immediate consequence of (
Definition

3.1
) the bid-to-value mappings

\varphi_{\sigma}(b)=b+\big(\frac{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(b)}{\mathcal{B}(b)}-\frac{B^{\prime}_{\sigma}(b)}{B_{\sigma}(b)}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(\sigma\neq L)\big)^{-1}
for
\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}
.
In particular, in the proof of
Lemma

2.13
(Fact
3
), we already show that the pseudo mapping
\varphi_{L}(b)>b
for
b\in(\gamma,\,\lambda]
and
\varphi_{L}(\gamma)\geq\gamma
. Moreover, we have

\sum_{i\in[n]}\big(\varphi_{i}(b)-b\big)^{-1}=n\cdot\frac{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(b)}{\mathcal{B}(b)}-\sum_{i\in[n]}\frac{B^{\prime}_{i}(b)}{B_{i}(b)}=(n-1)\cdot\frac{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(b)}{\mathcal{B}(b)}+\frac{L^{\prime}(b)}{L(b)}\geq(n-1)\cdot\big(\varphi_{L}(b)-b\big)^{-1}
.
Rearranging this equation gives
Item

2
.
∎

Lemma

3.4
shows that the bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L=\{B_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\otimes L
can be reconstructed from the bid-to-value mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\{\varphi_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
.
This lemma is important in that many subsequent reductions will construct new bid distributions
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L}
in terms of the mappings
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
.
Therefore, the correctness of such construction reduces to checking
Items

1
and
2
of
Lemma

3.3
and monotonicity of the mappings
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
.

Lemma 3.4

(Bid distributions)
.

For
(n+1)
functions
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\{\varphi_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
that are increasing on
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
and satisfy
Items

1
and
2
of
Lemma

3.3
,
the functions
\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L
given below are well-defined
[\gamma,\,\lambda]
-supported bid distributions and their bid-to-value mappings are exactly
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\{\varphi_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
.

\displaystyle B_{i}(b)

\displaystyle~\equiv~\exp\Big(-\int_{b}^{\lambda}\Big(\big(\varphi_{L}(b)-b\big)^{-1}-\big(\varphi_{i}(b)-b\big)^{-1}\Big)\cdot\mathrm{d}b\Big)\cdot\mathbbm{1}(b\geq\gamma),\qquad\qquad\forall i\in[n].

\displaystyle L(b)

\displaystyle~\equiv~\exp\Big(-\int_{b}^{\lambda}\Big(\sum_{i\in[n]}\big(\varphi_{i}(b)-b\big)^{-1}-(n-1)\cdot\big(\varphi_{L}(b)-b\big)^{-1}\Big)\cdot\mathrm{d}b\Big)\cdot\mathbbm{1}(b\geq\gamma).

Proof.

The construction of
\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L
intrinsically ensures that
B_{\sigma}(b)=0
below the infimum bid
b<\gamma
and
B_{\sigma}(\lambda)=1
at the supremum bid
\lambda
, for
\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}
.
Moreover,
Items

1
and
2
of
Lemma

3.3
together make each
B_{\sigma}(b)
an increasing function on
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
, hence a well-defined
[\gamma,\,\lambda]
-supported bid distribution. It remains to verify
\varphi_{\sigma}(b)=b+\big(\sum_{k\in[n]\setminus\{\sigma\}}\frac{B^{\prime}_{k}(b)}{B_{k}(b)}+\frac{L^{\prime}(b)}{L(b)}\big)^{-1}
over the bid support
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
, for
\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}
, namely the functions
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\{\varphi_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
are exactly (
Definition

3.1
) the bid-to-value mappings stemmed from the
\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L
.
These identities for
\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}
can be easily seen via elementary algebra; we omit the details for brevity.

Lemma

3.4
follows then.
∎

Lemma

3.5
suggests that any valid
real
instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})
can be reinterpreted as a valid
pseudo
instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L_{\gamma})
, by employing a specific pseudo bidder
L_{\gamma}
.
As a consequence (
Corollary

3.6
): Towards a lower bound on the
Price of Anarchy
in
First Price Auctions
, we can consider valid
pseudo
instances instead; cf.
Corollary

2.23
for comparison.

Lemma 3.5

(Pseudo instances)
.

For a valid instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
, the following hold:

1.

The pseudo bidder
L_{\gamma}(b)\equiv\mathbbm{1}(b\geq\gamma)
induces a valid pseudo instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L_{\gamma})\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
.

2.

{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L_{\gamma})={\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})
and
{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L_{\gamma})={\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})
.

Proof.

As mentioned in
Remark

8
, to see the validity
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L_{\gamma})\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
, we only need to verify
•
‣
3.1
of mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\{\varphi_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
and
•
‣
3.1
of the conditional value
P
.

Item

1
.

We have
L_{\gamma}(b)=1
and
L^{\prime}_{\gamma}(b)\big/L_{\gamma}(b)=0
for
b\geq\gamma
. Thus, each real bidder
i\in[n]
keeps the
same
mapping
\varphi_{i}(b)
after including the pseudo bidder
L_{\gamma}
(
Definitions

2.22
and
3.1
), preserving
•
‣
3.1
.
Also, the pseudo mapping can be written as
\varphi_{L_{\gamma}}(b)=b+\mathcal{B}(b)\big/\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(b)
for
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
, based on the first-order bid distribution
\mathcal{B}(b)=\prod_{i\in[n]}B_{i}(b)\cdot L_{\gamma}(b)=\prod_{i\in[n]}B_{i}(b)
.
In the proof of
Lemma

2.13
(Fact
B
), we already verify
•
‣
3.1
of this mapping.
Moreover, the
unmodified
conditional value
P
must preserve
•
‣
3.1
.

Item

2
.

This can be easily inferred from (
Lemmas

2.20
and
2.21
versus
Lemma

3.2
) the
Social Welfare
formulas for valid
real
versus
pseudo
instances; thus we omit details for brevity. Roughly speaking, the point is that the pseudo bidder
L_{\gamma}(b)=1
for
b\geq\gamma
has no effect on
Social Welfares
.
∎

Due to the above lemma, we know that the
PoA
of pseudo instances is a lower bound of the real
PoA
. (On the other hand, we know that a pseudo instance can be viewed as the limit of a sequence of real instances. As a result, any
PoA
ratio obtained by a pseudo instance can be approximated by real instances arbitrarily well.) Thus, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 3.6

(Lower bound)
.

Regarding
First Price Auctions
, the
Price of Anarchy
is at least

\displaystyle{\sf PoA}~\geq~\inf\bigg\{\,\frac{{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)}{{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)}\,\,\bigg|\,\bigg.\,(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}~\text{and}~{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)<+\infty\,\bigg\}.

3.2
Discretize
: Towards (approximate) discrete pseudo instances

In this subsection, we introduce the concept of
discretized
pseudo instances
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
; see
Definitions

3.7
and
for its formal definition and a visual aid.

Definition 3.7

(Discretized pseudo instances)
.

A
valid
pseudo instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
from
Definition

3.1
is further called
discretized
when it satisfies
•
‣
3.7
and
•
‣
3.7
.

•

finiteness:

The supremum bid/value are
bounded away
from infinite
\lambda\leq\max(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))<+\infty
.

•

piecewise constancy:

Given some
bounded
integer
0\leq m<+\infty
and some
(m+1)
-piece partition of the bid support
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}:=[\gamma=\lambda_{0},\,\lambda_{1})\cup[\lambda_{1},\,\lambda_{2})\cup\dots\cup[\lambda_{m},\,\lambda_{m+1}=\lambda]
, each bid-to-value mapping
\varphi_{\sigma}(b)
for
\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}
is a
piecewise constant
function under this partition.

Notice that the partition
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
relies on the bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L
but is irrelevant to the monopolist
B_{1}
’s conditional value
P
.

Remark 9

(Discretized pseudo instances)
.

The value distributions
V_{\sigma}(v)
for
\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}
reconstructed from a
discretized
pseudo instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
are almost discrete, except the conditional value
P
of the monopolist
B_{1}
(which ranges between
\mathrm{supp}(P)\subseteq[\gamma,\,\varphi_{1}(\gamma)]
but otherwise is arbitrary).
Namely, each index
j\in[0:m]
piece of a bid-to-value mapping
\varphi_{\sigma}(b)
induces a probability mass
\big(B_{\sigma}(\lambda_{j+1})-B_{\sigma}(\lambda_{j})\big)
of the value distribution
V_{\sigma}(v)
.

Lemma

3.8
shows that the
PoA
bound of a (generic) valid pseudo instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
can be approximated
arbitrarily close
by a
discretized
pseudo instance
(\widetilde{P},\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L})
.

Intuition
.

For the
Price of Anarchy
problem, we only consider (
Corollary

3.6
) the pseudo instances that have
bounded

Social Welfares

{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)\leq{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)<+\infty
.
In the sense of Lebesgue integrability, we can carefully truncate
13
13

13

We will use a special truncation scheme. The usual truncation scheme often induces probability masses at the truncation threshold, but we require that the bid CDF’s
\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L
are
continuous
over the bid support
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
.
the bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L
to meet the
•
‣
3.7
condition, by which the auction/optimal
Social Welfares
change by negligible amounts.

Then, we replace the bid-to-value mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\{\varphi_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
by their
interpolation functions

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}=\{\widetilde{\varphi}_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
(
•
‣
3.7
).
The mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
determine the bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L
(
Lemma

3.4
) and the auction/optimal
Social Welfares
(
Lemma

3.2
) in terms of
integral formulas
, which are insensitive to the (local) changes of the interpolation functions
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
. Thus as usual, when the interpolation scheme is accurate enough, everything (including the
PoA
-bound) can be approximated arbitrarily well.
To make the arguments rigorous, we use the continuous mapping theorem
[
Bil13
]
in measure theory.

Theorem 10

(The continuous mapping theorem
[
Bil13
]
)
.

Let
X
and
\{X_{m}\}_{m\geq 0}
be random elements defined on a metric space
\mathbb{S}
. Regarding a function
g:\mathbb{S}\mapsto\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
(where
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
is another metric space), suppose its set of discontinuity points
\mathbb{D}_{g}
has a zero measure
\operatorname{{\bf Pr}}[X\in\mathbb{D}_{g}]=0
, then this function
g
perseveres the property
of convergence in distribution. Formally,

\big\{X_{m}\big\}~\overset{{\tt dist}}{\longrightarrow}~X\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\big\{g(X_{m})\big\}~\overset{{\tt dist}}{\longrightarrow}~g(X).

Lemma 3.8

(Discretization)
.

Given a valid pseudo instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
that has bounded expected auction/optimal
Social Welfares

{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)\leq{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)<+\infty
, for any error
\varepsilon\in(0,\,1)
, there is a discretized pseudo instance
(\widetilde{P},\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L})
such that
|{\sf PoA}(\widetilde{P},\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L})-{\sf PoA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)|\leq\varepsilon
.

Proof.

For ease of presentation, we first assume that the given pseudo instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
itself satisfies
•
‣
3.7

\lambda\leq\max(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))<+\infty
; this assumption will be removed later.

Given an integer
m\geq 0
, we would divide the
bounded
bid support into
(m+1)
uniform pieces
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=[\gamma=\lambda_{0},\,\lambda_{1})\cup[\lambda_{1},\,\lambda_{2})\cup\dots\cup[\lambda_{m},\,\lambda_{m+1}=\lambda]
, via the partition points

\lambda_{j}:=\gamma+\frac{j}{m+1}\cdot(\lambda-\gamma)
for
j\in[0:\,m+1]
.
Regarding this partition
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
, the
reduction (see
and
for its description and a visual aid) transforms the given pseudo instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
into another
discretized
pseudo instance
(P,\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L})
. This is formalized into
A
.

Fact A
.

Under reduction
(P,\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L})\leftarrow\text{\tt Discretize}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L,\,\boldsymbol{\Lambda})
, the output
(P,\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L})
is a discretized pseudo instance; the conditional value
P
is unmodified.

Proof.

The
Discretize
reduction (Line
) reconstructs the output bid distributions
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L}
from the output mappings
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}=\{\widetilde{\varphi}_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
; (Line
) each output mapping
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\sigma}(b)
is the
(m+1)
-piece
interpolation
of the input mapping
\varphi_{\sigma}(b)
under the partition
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
.
In particular (see
for a visual aid), the output mappings
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\sigma}(b)=\varphi_{\sigma}(\lambda_{j+1})\geq\varphi_{\sigma}(b)
interpolate the right endpoint of each index-
j
piece
b\in[\lambda_{j},\,\lambda_{j+1})
of the partition
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
.

Obviously, each interpolation
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\sigma}(b)
is a piecewise constant function on the same bounded domain
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
(
•
‣
3.7
and
•
‣
3.7
) and is an increasing function à la the input mapping
\varphi_{\sigma}(b)
(
•
‣
3.1
).
Also, the extended range
[\gamma,\,\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(\gamma)]=[\gamma,\,\varphi_{1}(\lambda_{1})]\supseteq[\gamma,\varphi_{1}(\gamma)]\supseteq\mathrm{supp}(P)
restricts the unmodified conditional value
P
(
•
‣
3.1
).

It remains to show that the reconstructed bid distributions
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L}
are well defined, namely that
(
Lemma

3.4
) the interpolations
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}=\{\widetilde{\varphi}_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
satisfy the conditions in
Lemma

3.3
.
The input mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\{\varphi_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
themselves must satisfy those conditions:
(
Item

1
of
Lemma

3.3
) that
\min(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(b^{\otimes n+1}))=\varphi_{L}(b)>b
for
b\in(\gamma,\,\lambda]
and
\min(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\gamma^{\otimes n+1}))=\varphi_{L}(\gamma)\geq\gamma
; (
Item

2
of
Lemma

3.3
) that
\sum_{i\in[n]}\frac{\varphi_{L}(b)-b}{\varphi_{i}(b)-b}\geq n-1
for
b\in(\gamma,\,\lambda]
.

By interpolating the right endpoint of each index-
j
piece
b\in[\lambda_{j},\,\lambda_{j+1})
of the partition
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
,
the output mappings satisfy
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\sigma}(b)=\varphi_{\sigma}(\lambda_{j+1})\geq\widetilde{\varphi}_{L}(b)=\varphi_{L}(\lambda_{j+1})>\lambda_{j+1}>b
(
Item

1
of
Lemma

3.3
)
and thus
\sum_{i\in[n]}\frac{\widetilde{\varphi}_{L}(b)-b}{\widetilde{\varphi}_{i}(b)-b}=\sum_{i\in[n]}\frac{\varphi_{L}(\lambda_{j+1})-b}{\varphi_{i}(\lambda_{j+1})-b}\geq\sum_{i\in[n]}\frac{\varphi_{L}(\lambda_{j+1})-\lambda_{j+1}}{\varphi_{i}(\lambda_{j+1})-\lambda_{j+1}}\geq n-1
(
Item

2
of
Lemma

3.3
).
This finishes the proof of
A
.
∎

We thus obtain a sequence of
discretized
pseudo instances
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}^{(m)}\otimes L^{(m)})
, for
m\geq 0
.
Below we study the bound
{\sf PoA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}^{(m)}\otimes L^{(m)})
for each of them.
To this end, consider a
specific
profile
(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)\in[\gamma,\,\varphi_{1}(\gamma)]\times[\gamma,\,\lambda]^{n+1}
.
Following
Definition

3.1
, depending on whether the bids
(\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)=(b_{\sigma})_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
each take the infimum
=\gamma
or not
>\gamma
:

•

The monopolist
B_{1}
takes a value
v_{1}^{(m)}=p\cdot\mathbbm{1}(b_{1}=\gamma)+\varphi_{1}^{(m)}(b_{1})\cdot\mathbbm{1}(b_{1}>\gamma)
.

•

Each other bidder
\sigma\in[2:\,n]\cup\{L\}
takes a value
v_{\sigma}^{(m)}=\gamma\cdot\mathbbm{1}(b_{1}=\gamma)+\varphi_{\sigma}^{(m)}(b_{\sigma})\cdot\mathbbm{1}(b_{\sigma}>\gamma)
.

Over the randomness of the allocation rule
\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)
, this index-
m
pseudo instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}^{(m)}\otimes L^{(m)})
yields an interim auction
Social Welfare

\varphi_{{\sf FPA}}^{(m)}(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell):=\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\mathrm{x}}\big[v_{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)}^{(m)}\big]
.

Likewise, under the
same
profile
(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)
, the input pseudo instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
yields a counterpart formula
\varphi_{{\sf FPA}}(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell):=\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\mathrm{x}}\big[v_{\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)}\big]
.

B
and
C
show certain properties of the input/index-
m
mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
and
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{(m)}
and the input/index-
m
formulas
\varphi_{{\sf FPA}}(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)
and
\varphi_{{\sf FPA}}^{(m)}(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)
.

Fact B
.

On the domain
b\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]
, the mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\{\varphi_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
and
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{(m)}=\{\varphi_{\sigma}^{(m)}\}_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
are bounded everywhere, and continuous almost everywhere except a zero-measure set
\mathbb{D}\subseteq[\gamma,\,\lambda]
.
Also, on the domain
(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)\in[\gamma,\,\varphi_{1}(\gamma)]\times[\gamma,\,\lambda]^{n+1}
, the formulas
\varphi_{{\sf FPA}}(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)
and
\varphi_{{\sf FPA}}^{(m)}(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)
are bounded everywhere, and continuous almost everywhere except a zero-measure set
\mathbb{D}_{{\sf FPA}}
.

Proof.

Each input mapping
\varphi_{\sigma}(b)
as an increasing function (
•
‣
3.1
) is continuous almost everywhere, except countably many discontinuities that in total have a zero measure. Also, each input mapping
\varphi_{\sigma}(b)
is bounded
\varphi_{\sigma}(b)\leq\varphi_{\sigma}(\lambda)\leq\max(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))<+\infty
(
•
‣
3.7
).
Such continuity and boundedness extend to the input formula
\varphi_{{\sf FPA}}(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)
, since tiebreakers
\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}(\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)
occur with a zero probability.
14
14

14

That is, conditioned on the
all-infimum
bid profile
\big\{(\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)=\boldsymbol{\gamma}\big\}
, the monopolist
always
gets allocated
\mathrm{x}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})=B_{1}
(
•
‣
3.1
). Otherwise
\big\{(\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)\neq\boldsymbol{\gamma}\big\}
, tiebreakers occur with a zero probability because the underlying bid CDF’s
\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L
are continuous functions (
Definition

).

For the same reasons, the index-
m

\varphi_{\sigma}^{(m)}(b)
and the index-
m
formula
\varphi_{{\sf FPA}}^{(m)}(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)
are also bounded and continuous almost everywhere.

B
follows then.
∎

Fact C
.

The sequence
\big\{\varphi_{{\sf FPA}}^{(m)}(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)\big\}_{m\geq 0}
pointwise converges to
\varphi_{{\sf FPA}}(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)
almost everywhere.

Proof.

By construction, each index-
m

FPA
formula
\varphi_{{\sf FPA}}^{(m)}(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)
for
m\geq 0
is the
(m+1)^{n+1}
-piece uniform interpolation of the input
FPA
formula
\varphi_{{\sf FPA}}(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)
. Because the
\varphi_{{\sf FPA}}(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)
itself is bounded and continuous almost everywhere (
B
), letting

makes the sequence
\big\{\varphi_{{\sf FPA}}^{(m)}(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)\big\}_{m\geq 0}
converge to
\varphi_{{\sf FPA}}(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)
almost everywhere.

C
follows then.
∎

Now we would consider a
random

and

Fact D
.

The measure
\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell}\big[\,(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)\in\mathbb{D}_{{\sf FPA}}\,\big]=0
, where
\mathbb{D}_{{\sf FPA}}
is the set of discontinuity points of the input
FPA
formula
\varphi_{{\sf FPA}}(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)

Proof.

We claim that, with the
(\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)\in[\gamma,\,\lambda]^{n+1}
held constant, the formula
\varphi_{{\sf FPA}}(p,\,\boldsymbol{b},\,\ell)
is continuous with respect to
p\in[\gamma,\,\varphi_{1}(\gamma)]
, the monopolist
B_{1}
’s conditional value.

IF

Combining both cases

As a consequence, the formula

The measure

Fact F

The sequence of random profiles

The General Case.

When the input
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
violates
•
‣

, we instead consider the

\boldsymbol{B}^{(t)}\otimes L^{(t)}=\{B_{\sigma}^{(t)}\}_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
given by
B_{\sigma}^{(t)}(b)\equiv\min(\frac{B_{\sigma}(b)}{B_{\sigma}(t)},\,1)
, where the parameter
t\in(\gamma,\,\lambda)
; see
Figure

for a visual aid.
18
18

18

The proof works for any supremum bid
\lambda\leq+\infty
, including the infinite one
\lambda=+\infty
. However, we demonstrate a bounded one

in

for convenience.

By construction, (

) the mappings keep the same
\varphi_{\sigma}^{(t)}(b)=\varphi_{\sigma}(b)
on the

, which means
\sup(\mathrm{supp}(\boldsymbol{B}^{(t)}\otimes L^{(t)}))=t\leq\max(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{(t)}(\boldsymbol{t}))=\max(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\boldsymbol{t}))<+\infty
.
Precisely, together with the unmodified conditional value

, this

(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}^{(t)}\otimes L^{(t)})\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}
and satisfies
•
‣
3.7
.

We claim that when the parameter

is close enough to the supremum bid
\lambda\leq+\infty
, the truncation has an arbitrarily small effect on the
Social Welfares

{\sf FPA}(\text{input})\leq{\sf OPT}(\text{input})<+\infty
.

Following
Lemma

3.2
, the input auction
Social Welfare

{\sf FPA}(\text{input})=\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[P]\cdot\mathcal{B}(\gamma)+\int_{\gamma}^{\lambda}f(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b
, for the integrand
f(b):=\sum_{\sigma}\varphi_{\sigma}(b)\cdot\frac{B^{\prime}_{\sigma}(b)}{B_{\sigma}(b)}\cdot\mathcal{B}(b)
.
This formula
<+\infty
is Lebesgue integrable, hence
\lim_{t\nearrow\lambda}\int_{t}^{\lambda}f(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b=0
.
Likewise, the
truncated
auction
Social Welfare

\displaystyle{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}^{(t)}\otimes L^{(t)})

\displaystyle~=~\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[P]\cdot\mathcal{B}^{(t)}(\gamma)+\int_{\gamma}^{t}\Big(\sum_{\sigma}\varphi_{\sigma}^{(t)}(b)\cdot\tfrac{{B_{\sigma}^{(t)}}^{\prime}(b)}{B_{\sigma}^{(t)}(b)}\cdot\mathcal{B}^{(t)}(b)\Big)\cdot\mathrm{d}b~~~~

\displaystyle~=~\frac{1}{\mathcal{B}(t)}\cdot\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[P]\cdot\mathcal{B}(\gamma)+\frac{1}{\mathcal{B}(t)}\cdot\int_{\gamma}^{t}f(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b

\displaystyle~=~\frac{1}{\mathcal{B}(t)}\cdot\Big({\sf FPA}(\text{input})-\int_{t}^{\lambda}f(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b\Big).

We have
\lim_{t\nearrow\lambda}{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}^{(t)}\otimes L^{(t)})={\sf FPA}(\text{input})
, given continuity
\lim_{t\nearrow\lambda}\mathcal{B}(t)=1
(
Definition

3.1
).
Accordingly, for some threshold
T_{{\sf FPA}}=T_{{\sf FPA}}(\varepsilon)\in(\gamma,\,\lambda)
, any parameter
t\in[T_{{\sf FPA}},\,\lambda)
yields a close enough auction
Social Welfare

{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}^{(t)}\otimes L^{(t)})={\sf FPA}(\text{input})\cdot e^{\pm\varepsilon/3}
.

Figure 9:
Diagram of the transformation from
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B})
into
(P,\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}})
, making a valid pseudo instance further satisfy
finiteness
(

). For convenience, the demonstrated supremum bid is bounded
\lambda<+\infty
. But the transformation works for an arbitrary one
\lambda\leq+\infty
.

Due to
Lemma

3.2
, the input optimal
Social Welfare

{\sf OPT}(\text{input})=\gamma+\int_{\gamma}^{+\infty}(1-\prod_{\sigma}V_{\sigma}(v))\cdot\mathrm{d}v
.
This formula
<+\infty
is Lebesgue integrable, hence
\int_{\gamma}^{+\infty}(1-V_{\sigma}(v))\cdot\mathrm{d}v<+\infty
.
Since the bid-to-value mappings are invariant
\varphi_{\sigma}^{(t)}(b)=\varphi_{\sigma}(b)
over the
truncated
support
b\in[\gamma,\,t]
,
(
Lemma

2.18
) the bid distributions
B_{\sigma}^{(t)}(b)\equiv\min(\frac{B_{\sigma}(b)}{B_{\sigma}(t)},\,1)
induce the value distributions
V_{\sigma}^{(t)}(v)\equiv\min(\frac{V_{\sigma}(v)}{B_{\sigma}(t)},\,1)
.
Given continuity
\lim_{t\nearrow\lambda}B_{\sigma}(t)=1
(
Definition

3.1
), we deduce that
\lim_{t\nearrow\lambda}\int_{\gamma}^{+\infty}\big|V_{\sigma}^{(t)}(v)-V_{\sigma}(v)\big|\cdot\mathrm{d}v=0
.
Based on
Lemma

3.2
, we can bound the
truncated
optimal
Social Welfare
:

\displaystyle{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}^{(t)}\otimes L^{(t)})

\displaystyle~=~{\sf OPT}(\text{input})-\int_{\gamma}^{+\infty}P(v)\cdot\Big(\prod_{\sigma}V_{\sigma}^{(t)}(v)-\prod_{\sigma}V_{\sigma}(v)\Big)\cdot\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle~=~{\sf OPT}(\text{input})-\sum_{\sigma}\int_{\gamma}^{+\infty}\Big(P(v)\cdot\big(V_{\sigma}^{(t)}(v)-V_{\sigma}(v)\big)\cdot

\displaystyle\phantom{~=~{\sf OPT}(\text{input})-\int_{\gamma}^{+\infty}\sum_{\sigma}\Big(}\prod_{k<\sigma}V_{k}^{(t)}(v)\cdot\prod_{k>\sigma}V_{k}(v)\Big)\cdot\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle~=~{\sf OPT}(\text{input})\pm\sum_{\sigma}\int_{\gamma}^{+\infty}\big|V_{\sigma}^{(t)}(v)-V_{\sigma}(v)\big|\cdot\mathrm{d}v.

Here the last step uses the relaxation
|P(v)|,\,|V_{\sigma}^{(t)}(v)|,\,|V_{\sigma}(v)|\leq 1
.

Now it is clear that
\lim_{t\nearrow\lambda}{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}^{(t)}\otimes L^{(t)})={\sf OPT}(\text{input})
.
Accordingly, for some threshold
T_{{\sf OPT}}=T_{{\sf OPT}}(\varepsilon)\in(\gamma,\,\lambda)
, any parameter
t\in[T_{{\sf OPT}},\,\lambda)
yields a close enough optimal
Social Welfare

{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}^{(t)}\otimes L^{(t)})={\sf OPT}(\text{input})\cdot e^{\pm\varepsilon/3}
.

Combining everything together, the parameter

pseudo instance ensures a close enough bound
{\sf PoA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}^{(T)}\otimes L^{(T)})={\sf PoA}(\text{input})\cdot e^{\pm 2\cdot(\varepsilon/3)}={\sf PoA}(\text{input})\pm\varepsilon
and satisfies
•
‣
3.7
.
Through the
reduction, we can transform this

In particular, the resulting bound
{\sf PoA}(P,\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L})={\sf PoA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}^{(T)}\otimes L^{(T)})\pm\varepsilon={\sf PoA}(\text{input})\pm 2\varepsilon
.
Reducing the error

by a factor of
2
finishes the proof of

.

3.3

This subsection presents the

Definition 3.9

(Translated pseudo instances)
.

A

(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
from

is further called

when (
nil infimum bid
) the infimum bid is nil
\gamma=0
.

Lemma

presents performance guarantees of the

Lemma 3.10

(
Translate
;
)
.

Under reduction
(\widetilde{P},\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L})\leftarrow\text{\tt Translate}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
:

1.

The output
(\widetilde{P},\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L})
is a translated pseudo instance.

2.

A (weakly) worse bound is yielded
{\sf PoA}(\widetilde{P},\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L})\leq{\sf PoA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
.

Proof.

See
for a visual aid.
Let us verify
Items

1
and
2
one by one.

Item

1
.

The
reduction shifts the bid/value spaces each by a distance of
-\gamma
, inducing a nil infimum bid
\gamma-\gamma=0
(
3.9
). Clearly, each shifted mapping
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\sigma}(b)=\varphi_{\sigma}(b+\gamma)-\gamma
for
\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}
(akin to the input mapping
\varphi_{\sigma}
) is increasing on the shifted support
b\in[0,\,\lambda-\gamma]
(
•
‣
3.1
), and the shifted conditional value
\widetilde{P}=P-\gamma
(akin to the input one
\mathrm{supp}(P)\subseteq[\gamma,\,\varphi_{1}(\gamma)]
) ranges between
\mathrm{supp}(\widetilde{P})\subseteq[\gamma-\gamma,\,\varphi_{1}(\gamma)-\gamma]=[0,\,\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(\gamma)]
(
•
‣
3.1
).
The shifted pseudo instance
(\widetilde{P},\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L})
must preserve (
Definition

3.7
)
•
‣
3.7
of the mappings
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}=\{\widetilde{\varphi}_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
and
•
‣
3.7
, namely
\lambda-\gamma\leq\max(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}))=\max(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))-\gamma<+\infty
. Thus,
Item

1
follows.

Item

2
.

Because the bid/value spaces each are shifted by a distance of
-\gamma
, the auction/optimal
Social Welfares
(
Lemma

3.2
) each drop by an amount of
\gamma
. We thus deduce that

{\sf PoA}(\widetilde{P},\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L})~=~\frac{{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)\;-\gamma}{{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)-\gamma}~\leq~\frac{{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)\;}{{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)}~=~{\sf PoA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L).

Here the inequality holds, because the
Social Welfares

{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)\geq{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
are at least the infimum bid
\gamma
(cf.
Lemma

3.2
).
Item

2
and
Lemma

3.10
follow then.
∎

Lemma

3.11
presents alternative
Social Welfare
formulas for
translated
pseudo instances, which supplement
Lemma

3.2
and will be more convenient in several places.

Lemma 3.11

(
Social Welfares
)
.

For a translated pseudo instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
, the expected auction
Social Welfare

{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
is given by

\displaystyle{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)\;

\displaystyle~=~\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[P]\cdot\mathcal{B}(0)~+~\int_{0}^{\lambda}\sum_{i\in[n]}\Big(\frac{\varphi_{i}(b)}{\varphi_{L}(b)-b}-\frac{\varphi_{i}(b)-\varphi_{L}(b)}{\varphi_{i}(b)-b}\Big)\cdot\mathcal{B}(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b~~~~~\;\;

\displaystyle\phantom{~=~\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[P]\cdot\mathcal{B}(0)}~-~\int_{0}^{\lambda}(n-1)\cdot\frac{\varphi_{L}(b)}{\varphi_{L}(b)-b}\cdot\mathcal{B}(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b.

Regarding the underlying partition
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=[0=\lambda_{0},\,\lambda_{1})\cup[\lambda_{1},\,\lambda_{2})\cup\dots\cup[\lambda_{m},\,\lambda_{m+1}=\lambda]
,
represent the piecewise constant bid-to-value mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\{\varphi_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
as a bid-to-value table
\boldsymbol{\Phi}=\big[\phi_{\sigma,\,j}\big]
for
(\sigma,\,j)\in([n]\cup\{L\})\times[0:m]
, i.e.,
\varphi_{\sigma}(b)\equiv\phi_{\sigma,\,j}
on each index-
j
piece
b\in[\lambda_{j},\,\lambda_{j+1})
.
Then the expected optimal
Social Welfare

{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
is given by

\displaystyle{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)~=~\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big(1-P(v)\cdot\prod_{(\sigma,\,j)\,\in\,\boldsymbol{\Phi}}\big(1-\omega_{\sigma,\,j}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(v<\phi_{\sigma,\,j})\big)\Big)\cdot\mathrm{d}v.\hskip 64.58788pt

where the probabilities
\omega_{\sigma,\,j}:=1-\frac{B_{\sigma}(\lambda_{j})}{B_{\sigma}(\lambda_{j+1})}
can be reconstructed from the partition-table tuple
(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\,\boldsymbol{\Phi})
following
Lemma

3.4
.

Proof.

We deduce from
Lemma

3.4
that
\frac{B^{\prime}_{i}(b)}{B_{i}(b)}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}b}\big(\ln B_{i}(b)\big)=\big(\varphi_{L}(b)-b\big)^{-1}-\big(\varphi_{i}(b)-b\big)^{-1}
for
i\in[n]
and

\frac{L^{\prime}(b)}{L(b)}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}b}\big(\ln L(b)\big)=\sum_{i\in[n]}\big(\varphi_{i}(b)-b\big)^{-1}-(n-1)\cdot\big(\varphi_{L}(b)-b\big)^{-1}
.
Thus we can rewrite the auction
Social Welfare
formula from
Lemma

3.2
(with
3.9

\gamma=0
) as follows:

\displaystyle{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)

\displaystyle~=~\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[P]\cdot\mathcal{B}(0)~+~\sum_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}\Big(\int_{0}^{\lambda}\varphi_{\sigma}(b)\cdot\frac{B^{\prime}_{\sigma}(b)}{B_{\sigma}(b)}\cdot\mathcal{B}(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b\Big)

\displaystyle~=~\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[P]\cdot\mathcal{B}(0)~+~\sum_{i\in[n]}\Big(\int_{0}^{\lambda}\Big(\frac{\varphi_{i}(b)}{\varphi_{L}(b)-b}-\frac{\varphi_{i}(b)}{\varphi_{i}(b)-b}\Big)\cdot\mathcal{B}(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b\Big)

\displaystyle\phantom{~=~\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[P]\cdot\mathcal{B}(0)}~+~\int_{0}^{\lambda}\Big(\sum_{i\in[n]}\frac{\varphi_{L}(b)}{\varphi_{i}(b)-b}-(n-1)\cdot\frac{\varphi_{L}(b)}{\varphi_{L}(b)-b}\Big)\cdot\mathcal{B}(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b,

which after being rearranged gives the formula in the statement of
Lemma

3.11
.

Regarding the partition
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=[0\equiv\lambda_{0},\,\lambda_{1})\cup[\lambda_{1},\,\lambda_{2})\cup\dots\cup[\lambda_{m},\,\lambda_{m+1}\equiv\lambda]
and the bid-to-value table
\boldsymbol{\Phi}=\big[\phi_{\sigma,\,j}\big]
, namely
\varphi_{\sigma}(b)\equiv\phi_{\sigma,\,j}
on each piece
b\in[\lambda_{j},\,\lambda_{j+1})
, for
(\sigma,\,j)\in([n]\cup\{L\})\times[0:m]
,
we can formulate the conditional probabilities
\omega_{\sigma,\,j}=1-\frac{B_{\sigma}(\lambda_{j})}{B_{\sigma}(\lambda_{j+1})}
through
Lemma

3.4
as follows:

\displaystyle\omega_{i,\,j}~=~1-\exp\Big(-\int_{\lambda_{j}}^{\lambda_{j+1}}\Big(\big(\phi_{L,\,j}-b\big)^{-1}-\big(\phi_{i,\,j}-b\big)^{-1}\Big)\cdot\mathrm{d}b\Big),\qquad\forall i\in[n].

\displaystyle\omega_{L,\,j}~=~1-\exp\Big(-\int_{\lambda_{j}}^{\lambda_{j+1}}\Big(\sum_{i\in[n]}\big(\phi_{i,\,j}-b\big)^{-1}-(n-1)\cdot\big(\phi_{L,\,j}-b\big)^{-1}\Big)\cdot\mathrm{d}b\Big).\hskip 54.62923pt

Following
Lemma

3.2
(with
3.9

\gamma=\lambda_{0}\equiv 0
), the optimal
Social Welfare
is given by

{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\big(1-P(v)\cdot\prod_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{b_{\sigma}\sim B_{\sigma}}\big[\,(b_{\sigma}\leq\lambda_{0})\vee(\varphi_{\sigma}(b_{\sigma})\leq v)\,\big]\big)\cdot\mathrm{d}v
.
Because we are considering piecewise constant mappings, namely
\varphi_{\sigma}(b)\equiv\phi_{\sigma,\,j}
on each piece
b\in[\lambda_{j},\,\lambda_{j+1})
, we can deduce that for
\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}
and any nonnegative value
v\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{b_{\sigma}\sim B_{\sigma}}\big[\,(b_{\sigma}\leq\lambda_{0})\vee(\varphi_{\sigma}(b_{\sigma})\leq v)\,\big]

\displaystyle~=~B_{\sigma}(\lambda_{0})+\sum_{j\in[0:m]}(B_{\sigma}(\lambda_{j+1})-B_{\sigma}(\lambda_{j}))\cdot\mathbbm{1}(\phi_{\sigma,\,j}\leq v)~\,

\displaystyle~=~1-\sum_{j\in[0:m]}(B_{\sigma}(\lambda_{j+1})-B_{\sigma}(\lambda_{j}))\cdot\mathbbm{1}(v<\phi_{\sigma,\,j})

\displaystyle~=~\prod_{j\in[0:m]}\Big(1-\big(1-\tfrac{B_{\sigma}(\lambda_{j})}{B_{\sigma}(\lambda_{j+1})}\big)\cdot\mathbbm{1}(v<\phi_{\sigma,\,j})\Big)

Here the first/third steps holds because (
•
‣
3.1
) the mappings
\varphi_{\sigma}(b)
are increasing over the bid support
b\in[0,\,\lambda]
, namely
\phi_{\sigma,\,0}\leq\dots\leq\phi_{\sigma,\,j}\leq\dots\leq\phi_{\sigma,\,m}
. And the second step uses the boundary conditions
B_{\sigma}(\lambda_{m+1})=1
at the supremum bid
\lambda_{m+1}\equiv\lambda
.

Applying the above identities (together with
\omega_{\sigma,\,j}=1-\frac{B_{\sigma}(\lambda_{j})}{B_{\sigma}(\lambda_{j+1})}
) to the optimal
Social Welfare
formula gives the alternative formula claim in
Lemma

3.11
.
This finishes the proof.
∎

3.4
Layer
: Rearranging the bid-to-value mappings

This subsection shows the
reduction (see
and
for its description and for a visual aid), which transforms a
translated
pseudo instance (
Definition

3.9
) into a more special
layered
pseudo instance (
Definition

3.12
). Recall
Lemma

3.3
that over the bid support
b\in[0,\,\lambda]
, the pseudo mapping is dominated
\varphi_{L}(b)\leq\varphi_{i}(b)
by the real mappings
i\in[n]
.

Definition 3.12

(Layered pseudo instances)
.

A
translated
pseudo instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
from
Definition

3.9
is further called
layered
when (
layeredness
) the
real
bid-to-value mappings
\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
are ordered
\varphi_{1}(b)\geq\dots\geq\varphi_{i}(b)\geq\dots\geq\varphi_{n}(b)\geq\varphi_{L}(b)
over the bid support
b\in[0,\,\lambda]
.

Lemma

3.13
shows performance guarantees of the
reduction.

Lemma 3.13

(
Layer
;
)
.

Under reduction
(P,\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L})\leftarrow\text{\tt Layer}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
:

1.

The output
(P,\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L})
is a layered pseudo instance; the conditional value
P
is unmodified.

2.

The bound keeps the same
{\sf PoA}(P,\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L})={\sf PoA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
.

Proof.

Let us verify
Items

1
and
2
one by one; see
for a visual aid.

Item

1
.

The
reduction (Line
) pointwise/piecewise reorders
\{\widetilde{\varphi}_{i}\equiv\varphi_{(i)}\}_{i\in[n]}
all of the
real
mappings
\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
over the bid support
b\in[0,\,\lambda]
, namely
\varphi_{(1)}(b)\geq\dots\geq\varphi_{(i)}(b)\geq\dots\geq\varphi_{(n)}(b)
,
and (Line
) preserves the
pseudo
mapping
\widetilde{\varphi}_{L}\equiv\varphi_{L}
, which keeps being the
dominated
mapping
\min(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})\equiv\min(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{-L},\,\widetilde{\varphi}_{L})\equiv\min(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-L},\,\varphi_{L})\equiv\varphi_{L}\equiv\widetilde{\varphi}_{L}
provided Line
and
Lemma

3.3
.

Clearly, such reordered mappings
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}=\{\widetilde{\varphi}_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
satisfy
3.12
,
3.9
,
•
‣
3.7
, and
•
‣
3.7
(
Definitions

3.12
,
3.9
and
3.7
)
and are increasing over the bid support
b\in[0,\,\lambda]
(
•
‣
3.1
;
Definition

3.1
)
à la the input mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
, which can be easily seen with the help of
.
The extended range
[0,\,\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(0)]=[0,\,\varphi_{(1)}(0)]=[0,\,\max(\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathbf{0}))]\supseteq[0,\varphi_{1}(0)]\supseteq\mathrm{supp}(P)
must restrict the unmodified conditional value
P
(
•
‣
3.1
).

It remains to show that (Line
) the reconstructed bid distributions
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L}
are well defined, i.e,
(

3.4
) the reordered mappings
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}=\{\widetilde{\varphi}_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}
satisfy the conditions in

.
This is obvious in that (i) those conditions are symmetric about the real mappings
\{\widetilde{\varphi}_{i}\equiv\varphi_{(i)}\}_{i\in[n]}
and (ii) the pseudo mapping is unmodified
\widetilde{\varphi}_{L}\equiv\varphi_{L}
.
This finishes the proof of
Item

1
.

Item

2
.

Consider the auction/optimal
Social Welfare
formulas from
Lemma

3.11
:

\displaystyle{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)\;~=~\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[P]\cdot\mathcal{B}(0)~+~\int_{0}^{\lambda}\sum_{i\in[n]}\Big(\frac{\varphi_{i}(b)}{\varphi_{L}(b)-b}-\frac{\varphi_{i}(b)-\varphi_{L}(b)}{\varphi_{i}(b)-b}\Big)\cdot\mathcal{B}(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b

\displaystyle\phantom{{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)\;~=~\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[P]\cdot\mathcal{B}(0)}~-~\int_{0}^{\lambda}(n-1)\cdot\frac{\varphi_{L}(b)}{\varphi_{L}(b)-b}\cdot\mathcal{B}(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b,

\displaystyle{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)~=~\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big(1-P(v)\cdot\prod_{(\sigma,\,j)\,\in\,\boldsymbol{\Phi}}\big(1-\omega_{\sigma,\,j}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(v<\phi_{\sigma,\,j})\big)\Big)\cdot\mathrm{d}v.

The probabilities
\omega_{\sigma,\,j}
are given by the partition
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=[0=\lambda_{0},\,\lambda_{1})\cup[\lambda_{1},\,\lambda_{2})\cup\dots\cup[\lambda_{m},\,\lambda_{m+1}=\lambda]
and the bid-to-value table
\boldsymbol{\Phi}=\big[\phi_{\sigma,\,j}\big]
for
(\sigma,\,j)\in([n]\cup\{L\})\times[0:m]
.

The auction
Social Welfare
keeps the same
{\sf FPA}(P,\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L})={\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
. Concretely, (i) the pseudo mapping
\varphi_{L}
, the first-order bid distribution (
Lemma

3.4
)
\mathcal{B}(b)=\exp\big(-\int_{b}^{\lambda}(\varphi_{L}(b)-b)^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}b\big)
, and the conditional value
P
are invariant, while (ii) the auction
Social Welfare
formula is
symmetric
about the reordered
real
mappings
\{\widetilde{\varphi}_{i}\equiv\varphi_{(i)}\}_{i\in[n]}
.

The optimal
Social Welfare
again keeps the same
{\sf OPT}(P,\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{B}}\otimes\widetilde{L})={\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
, following the same arguments (see the proof of
Lemma

3.11
for more details). Basically, (i) the
magnitude
of the values
\phi_{\sigma,\,j}
and the probabilities
\omega_{\sigma,\,j}
for
(\sigma,\,j)\in([n]\cup\{L\})\times[0:m]
keeps the same, just
column-wise
(
j\in[0:m]
) reordering the
real
rows such that
\widetilde{\phi}_{1,\,j}\geq\dots\widetilde{\phi}_{i,\,j}\geq\dots\geq\widetilde{\phi}_{n,\,j}
, while
(ii) the optimal
Social Welfare
formula considers the
overall
effect of all entries
(\sigma,\,j)\in([n]\cup\{L\})\times[0:m]
and is irrelevant to the reordering.

To conclude, the
PoA
-bound keeps the same and
Item

2
follows. This finishes the proof.
∎

3.5
Polarize
: Derandomizing the conditional values under zero bids

This subsection shows the
reduction (see
for its description), which transforms a

3.12

3.14

3.9
and

‣
3.1
) the monopolist
B_{1}

ranges between
\mathrm{supp}(P)\subseteq[0,\,\varphi_{1}(0)]
.

Definition 3.14

(Floor/Ceiling pseudo instances)
.

A
layer
pseudo instance
(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)
from

3.12

/

•
‣
3.14
or
•
‣
3.14
. Namely, in either case, the conditional value

is determined by the bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L
.

•

The monopolist
B_{1}

nil value

P\equiv 0
.

Therefore, this pseudo instance can be redenoted as
H^{\downarrow}\otimes\boldsymbol{B}_{-1}\otimes L=(P^{\downarrow},\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)

H^{\downarrow}=(P^{\downarrow}\equiv 0,\,B_{1})
. Denote by
\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}^{\downarrow}
the space of such pseudo instances.

•

The monopolist
B_{1}
’s conditional value always takes the
ceiling value

P\equiv\varphi_{1}(0)
.

Therefore, this pseudo instance can be redenoted as
H^{\uparrow}\otimes\boldsymbol{B}_{-1}\otimes L=(P^{\uparrow},\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)

\mathbb{B}_{\sf valid}^{\uparrow}
the space of such pseudo instances.

3.15
presents performance guarantees of the
reduction.

Lemma 3.15

(
Polarize
;
)
.

Under reduction

The output

is EITHER a floor pseudo instance OR a ceiling pseudo instance; the bid distributions

A (weakly) worse bound is yielded

Proof.

Let us verify

By construction (Lines
to
): The output

\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L
and the mappings
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
, thus preserving

,
3.9
,

,

) and the

(

Item

We claim that either or both of the two candidates

and

(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)

Notice that each candidate only modifies the conditional value distribution

, to EITHER the

OR the

.

Auction
Social Welfares
.

Following
Lemma

3.2
(with
3.9

\gamma=0
), the input auction
Social Welfare

{\sf FPA}(\text{input})\equiv{\sf FPA}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)=\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[P]\cdot\mathcal{B}(0)+\sum_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}\big(\int_{0}^{\lambda}\varphi_{\sigma}(b)\cdot\frac{B^{\prime}_{\sigma}(b)}{B_{\sigma}(b)}\cdot\mathcal{B}(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b\big)
,
where the
\mathcal{B}(b)=\prod_{\sigma\in[n]\cup\{L\}}B_{\sigma}(b)
.
The summation term is
invariant
, since the bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L
and the mappings

are unmodified.
Therefore, we can formulate the
counterpart as
{\sf FPA}({\ref{floor}})={\sf FPA}(\text{input})-\Delta_{{\sf FPA}}^{\downarrow}
, using
19
19

19

Recall that the expectation of a
nonnegative
distribution
F
can be written as
\operatorname{{\bf E}}[F]=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\big(1-F(v)\big)\cdot\mathrm{d}v
.

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\sf FPA}}^{\downarrow}=\big(\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[P]-\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[P^{\downarrow}]\big)\cdot\mathcal{B}(0)=\mathcal{B}(0)\cdot\int_{0}^{\varphi_{1}(0)}\big(1-P(v)\big)\cdot\mathrm{d}v,\hskip 48.93877pt

•
‣
3.1

0\leq P\leq\varphi_{1}(0)

and formulate the
counterpart as
{\sf FPA}({\ref{ceiling}})={\sf FPA}(\text{input})+\Delta_{{\sf FPA}}^{\uparrow}
, using

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\sf FPA}}^{\uparrow}=\big(\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[P^{\uparrow}]-\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[P]\big)\cdot\mathcal{B}(0)=\mathcal{B}(0)\cdot\int_{0}^{\varphi_{1}(0)}P(v)\cdot\mathrm{d}v.\hskip 77.39153pt

•
‣
3.1

0\leq P\leq\varphi_{1}(0)

Optimal
Social Welfares
.

Following
Lemma

3.11
, the input optimal
Social Welfare

{\sf OPT}(\text{input})\equiv{\sf OPT}(P,\,\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L)=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\big(1-P(v)\cdot\mathcal{V}(v)\big)\cdot\mathrm{d}v
,
where the
\mathcal{V}(v)
is determined by the unmodified bid distributions
\boldsymbol{B}\otimes L
(

) and thus is
