Figure 12:
H\otimes L\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf twin}^{\uparrow}

Put
Lemmas

4.14
and
3.16
together: Towards a lower bound on the
Price of Anarchy
, we can focus on
twin ceiling
pseudo instances
H\otimes L\in\mathbb{B}_{\sf twin}^{\uparrow}
(see
Figure

12
for a visual aid).
For such a pseudo instance, the monopolist
H
has a constant bid-to-value mapping
\varphi_{H}(b)=\phi_{H,\,0}
over the bid support
b\in[0,\,\lambda]
and the optimal
Social Welfare
is exactly the ceiling value
{\sf OPT}(H\otimes L)=\phi_{H,\,0}
(
Lemma

4.10
).
Obviously, scaling this pseudo instance to normalize the ceiling value
\phi_{H,\,0}=1
does not change the
PoA
bound.

In addition, the
•
‣
4.1
condition becomes vacuously true (cf.
Lemma

3.3
), while removing the
•
‣
4.1
condition will expand the search space, which is fine for our purpose towards a lower bound on the
Price of Anarchy
.

Taking these into account, we would redefine the (normalized)
twin ceiling
pseudo instances and consider a modified functional optimization (
Corollary

4.16
).

Definition 4.15

(Twin ceiling pseudo instances)
.

For a
twin ceiling
pseudo instance
H\otimes L
:

•

The (real)
monopolist

H
competes with the pseudo bidder
L
, having a
constant
conditional value
P^{\uparrow}\equiv 1
and a
constant
bid-to-value mapping
\varphi_{H}(b)=b+\frac{L(b)}{L^{\prime}(b)}=1
on
b\in[0,\,\lambda]
. Hence, the supremum bid is bounded between
\lambda\in(0,\,1)
and the pseudo bidder
L
’s bid distribution is given by
L(b)=L_{\lambda}(b):=\frac{1-\lambda}{1-b}
.

•

The pseudo bidder
L
competes with both bidders
H\otimes L
, having an
increasing
bid-to-value mapping
\varphi_{L}(b)=b+\big(\frac{H^{\prime}(b)}{H(b)}+\frac{L^{\prime}(b)}{L(b)}\big)^{-1}=b+\big(\frac{H^{\prime}(b)}{H(b)}+\frac{1}{1-b}\big)^{-1}=1-\frac{(1-b)^{2}}{H(b)/H^{\prime}(b)+1-b}
on
b\in[0,\,\lambda]
.

Given a supremum bid
\lambda\in(0,\,1)
, the pseudo bidder
L=L_{\lambda}
is determined, so the search space is simply
\mathbb{H}_{\lambda}:=\big\{H\,\big|\,\big.\text{the pseudo mapping $\varphi_{L}(b)$ is increasing on $b\in[0,\,\lambda]$}\big\}
.

Corollary 4.16

(Lower bound)
.

Regarding
First Price Auctions
, the
Price of Anarchy
is at least

\displaystyle{\sf PoA}~\geq~\inf\Big\{\,{\sf FPA}(H\otimes L_{\lambda})\,\,\Big|\,\Big.\,\lambda\in(0,\,1)~\text{and}~H\in\mathbb{H}_{\lambda}\,\Big\}.

(
\clubsuit
)

5
Lower Bound Analysis

Following
Corollary

4.16
and Optimization (

♣

‣
4.16
), we study the space of twin ceiling pseudo instances
H\otimes L_{\lambda}
under all possible supremum bids
\lambda\in(0,\,1)
and aim to find the worst case
H^{*}\otimes L^{*}
. This task is accomplished in three steps:

•

Section

5.1
presents an explicit formula for the expected auction
Social Welfare

{\sf FPA}(H\otimes L_{\lambda})
. Also, we prove that imposing certain properties (such as
differentiability
) on the monopolist’s bid distribution
H
does not change the solution to Optimization (

♣

‣
4.16
).

•

Section

5.2
resolves Optimization (

♣

‣
4.16
) by leveraging tools from Calculus of Variations (i.e., the imposed properties enable these tools). Roughly speaking, the worst case
H^{*}\otimes L^{*}
turns out to be specified by the supremum bid
\lambda\in(0,\,1)
and one more parameter
\mu>0
, thus taking the form
H^{*}\otimes L^{*}=H_{\mu^{*}}\otimes L_{\lambda^{*}}
.

•

Section

5.3
captures the worst case
H_{\mu^{*}}\otimes L_{\lambda^{*}}
by optimizing the parameters
\lambda
and
\mu
, which immediately implies the lower-bound part of
Theorem

1
that the
{\sf PoA}\geq 1-1/e^{2}
.

5.1
The expected auction
Social Welfare

We introduce the space of
twice continuously differentiable
CDF’s
\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}^{2}
right below (
Definition

5.1
). Afterward, we (i) get an explicit reformulation for Optimization (

♣

‣
4.16
), then (ii) prove that the same solution can be achieved from a smaller search space
\mathbb{H}_{\lambda}\cap\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}^{2}\subsetneq\mathbb{H}_{\lambda}
, and then (iii) relax/expand the search space to
\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}^{2}
.

Definition 5.1

(Differentiability)
.

Denote by
\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}^{0}
the space of all
continuous
CDF’s that are supported on
[0,\,\lambda]
. The smaller space
\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}^{1}
(resp. the even smaller space
\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}^{2}
) further requires
continuously differentiable
CDF’s (resp.
twice continuously differentiable
CDF’s), i.e., the derivatives (resp. the second derivatives) of those CDF’s are continuous functions.

Lemma 5.2

(
Price of Anarchy
)
.

Regarding
First Price Auctions
, the
Price of Anarchy
satisfies that

\displaystyle{\sf PoA}

\displaystyle~\geq~1-\sup\Bigg\{\,(1-\lambda)\cdot\int_{0}^{\lambda}\frac{H(x)\cdot H^{\prime}(x)\cdot\mathrm{d}x}{H(x)+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime}(x)}\,\,\Bigg|\,\Bigg.\,\text{$\lambda\in(0,\,1)$ and $H\in\mathbb{H}_{\lambda}$}\,\Bigg\}

(I)

\displaystyle~=~1-\sup\Bigg\{\,(1-\lambda)\cdot\int_{0}^{\lambda}\frac{H(x)\cdot H^{\prime}(x)\cdot\mathrm{d}x}{H(x)+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime}(x)}\,\,\Bigg|\,\Bigg.\,\text{$\lambda\in(0,\,1)$ and $H\in(\mathbb{H}_{\lambda}\cap\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}^{2})$}\,\Bigg\}

(II)

\displaystyle~\geq~1-\sup\Bigg\{\,(1-\lambda)\cdot\int_{0}^{\lambda}\frac{H(x)\cdot H^{\prime}(x)\cdot\mathrm{d}x}{H(x)+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime}(x)}\,\,\Bigg|\,\Bigg.\,\text{$\lambda\in(0,\,1)$ and $H\in\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}^{2}$}\,\Bigg\}.

(III)

Let us prove the three steps of
Lemma

5.2
one by one.

Proof of Optimization (
I
).

This is a reformulation of Optimization (

♣

‣
4.16
). The monopolist
H
has a constant bid-to-value mapping
\varphi_{H}(b)=v_{H}=1
for
b\in[0,\,\lambda]
(
Definition

4.15
). Following the formula in
Lemma

3.2
, the expected auction
Social Welfare

{\sf FPA}(H\otimes L_{\lambda})
is given by

\displaystyle{\sf FPA}(H\otimes L_{\lambda})

\displaystyle~=~v_{H}\cdot H(0)\cdot L_{\lambda}(0)+\int_{0}^{\lambda}\bigg(v_{H}\cdot H^{\prime}(b)\cdot L_{\lambda}(b)+\varphi_{L}(b)\cdot L^{\prime}_{\lambda}(b)\cdot H(b)\bigg)\cdot\mathrm{d}b

\displaystyle~=~v_{H}\cdot H(\lambda)\cdot L_{\lambda}(\lambda)-\int_{0}^{\lambda}\big(v_{H}-\varphi_{L}(b)\big)\cdot L^{\prime}_{\lambda}(b)\cdot H(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b

\displaystyle~=~1-\int_{0}^{\lambda}\frac{(1-b)^{2}}{H(b)/H^{\prime}(b)+1-b}\cdot L^{\prime}_{\lambda}(b)\cdot H(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b

\displaystyle~=~1-(1-\lambda)\cdot\int_{0}^{\lambda}\frac{H(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b}{H(b)/H^{\prime}(b)+1-b}

Here the second step uses integration by parts
\int_{0}^{\lambda}\big(H^{\prime}(b)\cdot L_{\lambda}(b)+L^{\prime}_{\lambda}(b)\cdot H(b)\big)\cdot\mathrm{d}b=H(b)\cdot L_{\lambda}(b)\,\big|\,\big._{b=0}^{\lambda}
. The third step uses
H(\lambda)=L_{\lambda}(\lambda)=1
(because
\lambda
is the supremum bid) and the
\varphi_{L}(b)
formula in
Definition

4.15
. And the last step applies
L^{\prime}_{\lambda}(b)=\frac{1-\lambda}{(1-b)^{2}}
(
Definition

4.15
).

The above formula
{\sf FPA}(H\otimes L_{\lambda})
together with
Corollary

4.16
implies Optimization (
I
).
∎

Proof of Optimization (
II
).

Given a supremum bid
\lambda\in(0,\,1)
, we consider a specific CDF
H\in\mathbb{H}_{\lambda}
. The integral
\int_{0}^{\lambda}\frac{H(b)\cdot H^{\prime}(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b}{H(b)+(1-b)\cdot H^{\prime}(b)}
must be
Lebesgue integrable
, since the integrand
\frac{H(b)\cdot H^{\prime}(b)}{H(b)+(1-b)\cdot H^{\prime}(b)}
is nonnegative and the integral is upper bounded by
\int_{0}^{\lambda}\frac{H(b)\cdot H^{\prime}(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b}{H(b)}=H(\lambda)-H(0)\leq 1
.
Further, the integrand, if being regarded as a function of three variables
b\in(0,\,\lambda)
,
H\in[0,\,1]
and
H^{\prime}\geq 0
, is
twice continuously differentiable
. For these reasons, the CDF
H\in\mathbb{H}_{\lambda}
can be replaced
with another
twice continuously differentiable
CDF
\widetilde{H}\in(\mathbb{H}_{\lambda}\cap\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}^{2})
such that the integral
keeps the same
, namely the replacement incurs an infinitesimal error whose magnitude is irrelevant to the
\lambda\in(0,\,1)
.

To conclude, Optimization (
II
) has the same solution as Optimization (
I
).
∎

Proof of Optimization (
III
).

This step trivially holds, since it relaxes/enlarges the search space.
∎

5.2
The optima are solutions to an ODE

Following Optimization (
III
), we consider a
constant
supremum bid
\lambda\in(0,\,1)
for the moment, and study the next optimization (i.e., a functional of all twice differentiable CDF’s
H\in\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}^{2}
):

\displaystyle\sup\Bigg\{\,\int_{0}^{\lambda}\frac{H(x)\cdot H^{\prime}(x)\cdot\mathrm{d}x}{H(x)+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime}(x)}\,\,\Bigg|\,\Bigg.\,\text{$H\in\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}^{2}$}\,\Bigg\}.

(IV)

Below we first (
Lemma

5.3
) leverage tools from Calculus of Variations to get a
necessary condition
, i.e., an
ordinary differential equation
(ODE), for any worst-case CDF
H\in\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}^{2}
of Optimization (
IV
), and then (
Lemma

5.4
) explicitly resolve this ODE. In this way, any candidate worst-case CDF
H\in\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}^{2}
will be controlled by just one parameter
\mu>0
, namely
H=H_{\mu}
(see
Figure

13
for a visual aid). The following two lemmas formalize our proof plan.

Figure 13:
Diagram of the CDF
H_{\mu}
given by Implicit Equation (
3
), where a
full curve
is a well-defined CDF restricted to
\{(x,\,H_{\mu})\,|\,0\leq x\leq\lambda~\text{and}~(1+1/\mu)^{-2}\leq H_{\mu}\leq 1\}
.

In contrast, a
dotted curve
is just an
analytic continuation
, through the same implicit equation, to all
H_{\mu}\in[0,\,1]
. Such an analytic continuation violates the above restriction and/or Condition (
2
), so it is
not
a well-defined CDF supported on
[0,\,\lambda]
.

All the three CDF’s in the figure choose the same parameter
\mu=1
, so Condition (
2
) is equivalent to
\lambda\leq 1-(1+1/\mu)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/\mu}=1-4/e^{2}\approx 0.4587
.
(i) The green
full/dotted
curves choose a supremum bid
\lambda_{1}=0.35
that lies
within
the feasible space, by which the
y
-axis crosses the implicit equation twice, hence an
intersecting line
.
(ii) The black
full/dotted
curves choose the
boundary-case
supremum bid
\lambda_{2}=1-(1+1/\mu)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/\mu}
, by which the
y
-axis just touches the implicit equation, hence a
tangent line
.
(iii) The red
dotted
curve chooses an
infeasible
supremum bid
\lambda_{3}=0.6
, by which the
y
-axis
never
crosses the implicit equation, hence a
disjoint line
.

Lemma 5.3

(The ODE; a necessary condition)
.

Given a supremum bid
\lambda\in(0,\,1)
, any extremum CDF
H\in\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}^{2}
of Optimization (
IV
) satisfies the following ODE for any
x\in(0,\,\lambda)
:

\displaystyle\frac{(1-x)\cdot(H^{\prime}(x))^{2}}{(H(x)+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime}(x))^{2}}~=~\frac{2\cdot H(x)\cdot H^{\prime}(x)}{(H(x)+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime}(x))^{2}}~-~\frac{2\cdot(1-x)\cdot(H(x))^{2}\cdot H^{\prime\prime}(x)}{(H(x)+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime}(x))^{3}}.

(1)

Lemma 5.4

(The ODE solutions)
.

Given a supremum bid
\lambda\in(0,\,1)
, any extremum CDF
H\in\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}^{2}
of Optimization (
IV
) is determined by one parameter
\mu>0
such that

\displaystyle 1-(1+1/\mu)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/\mu}~\geq~\lambda,

(2)

namely
H=H_{\mu}
. Moreover, this extremum CDF
H_{\mu}
is defined by the following implicit equation:

\displaystyle\left\{\,(x,\,H_{\mu})\,\,\middle|\,\,\begin{aligned} &x~=~1-(1-\lambda)\cdot H_{\mu}\cdot\exp\Big((1+1/\mu)\cdot\big(2-2\sqrt{H_{\mu}}\big)\Big)\\
&\text{such that $0\leq x\leq\lambda$ and $(1+1/\mu)^{-2}\leq H_{\mu}\leq 1$}\phantom{\bigg.}\end{aligned}\,\right\}.

(3)

From subsequent proof details, it will be clear that whenever the parameter
\mu>0
satisfies Condition (
2
), Implicit Equation (
3
) does specify a well-defined CDF
H_{\mu}
.

We first establish
Lemma

5.3
. The proof exploits the
Euler-Lagrange equation
(stated below as
Theorem

12
), which is a standard tool from Calculus of Variations.

Theorem 12

(The Euler-Lagrange equation; Calculus of Variations
[
GS+00
]
)
.

Assume the following four premises for the functional
\mathcal{J}[f]:=\int_{x_{1}}^{x_{2}}\mathcal{L}\big(x,f(x),f^{\prime}(x)\big)\cdot\mathrm{d}x
.

•

x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are constants.

•

f(x)
is twice continuously differentiable.

•

f^{\prime}(x)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}f(x)
.

•

\mathcal{L}\big(x,f(x),f^{\prime}(x)\big)
is twice continuously differentiable with respect to variables
x
,
f
and
f^{\prime}
.

Then any extremum
\mathcal{J}[f]
satisfies (a necessary condition) the Euler-Lagrange equation

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial f}~=~\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\bigg(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial f^{\prime}}\bigg)\quad\text{for any}\quad x\in(x_{1},\,x_{2}).

Remark 13

(The Euler-Lagrange equation)
.

We emphasize that (i) the LHS of this equation takes the partial derivative of
\mathcal{L}(x,f,f^{\prime})
with respect to
f
; and (ii) the RHS first regards the partial derivative
\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial f^{\prime}}
as a function of
one
variable
x
, and then takes the derivative (with respect to
x
).

Now we use the Euler-Lagrange equation to obtain the desired necessary condition. Regarding Optimization (
IV
), we will consider the following functional.

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}[H]~:=~\int_{0}^{\lambda}\mathcal{L}\big(x,H(x),H^{\prime}(x)\big)\cdot\mathrm{d}x,\quad\text{where}\quad\mathcal{L}\big(x,H(x),H^{\prime}(x)\big)~:=~\frac{H(x)\cdot H^{\prime}(x)}{H(x)+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime}(x)}.

Notice that all the four premises of the Euler-Lagrange equation are satisfied:

•

x_{1}=0
and
x_{2}=\lambda
are constants.

•

We only consider those twice continuously differentiable CDF’s
H\in\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}^{2}
.

•

Obviously
H^{\prime}(x)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}H(x)
.

•

It is easy to verify that, with respect to variables
x\in(0,\,\lambda)\subseteq(0,\,1)
,
H\in[0,\,1]
and
H^{\prime}\geq 0
, the integrand
\mathcal{L}\big(x,H(x),H^{\prime}(x)\big)
is twice continuously differentiable.

Proof of
Lemma

5.3
.

For ease of notation, we would use the shorthands
H=H(x)
,
h=H^{\prime}(x)
and
h^{\prime}=H^{\prime\prime}(x)
, thus writing the integrand as
\mathcal{L}(x,H,h)=\frac{H\cdot h}{H+(1-x)\cdot h}
. Then regarding our functional, the LHS of the Euler-Lagrange equation is given by

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial H}~=~\frac{h\cdot(H+(1-x)\cdot h)-H\cdot h}{(H+(1-x)\cdot h)^{2}}~=~\frac{(1-x)\cdot(H^{\prime}(x))^{2}}{(H(x)+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime}(x))^{2}}~=~{\rm{LHS}}\text{ of ODE~\eqref{eq:ODE}}

where the second step combines the like terms and then substitutes back
H=H(x)
and
h=H^{\prime}(x)
.

Moreover, the partial derivative of
\mathcal{L}(x,H,h)
with respect to variable
h
is given by

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial h}~=~\frac{H\cdot(H+(1-x)\cdot h)-(H\cdot h)\cdot(1-x)}{(H+(1-x)\cdot h)^{2}}~=~\frac{H^{2}}{(H+(1-x)\cdot h)^{2}},

where the last step combines the like terms. When being regarded as a function of
one
variable
x
,
the derivative of function
\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial h}
(i.e., the RHS of the Euler-Lagrange equation) is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\bigg(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial h}\bigg)

\displaystyle~=~\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\big(H^{2}\big)~\cdot~\frac{1}{(H+(1-x)\cdot h)^{2}}~+~H^{2}~\cdot~\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\bigg(\frac{1}{(H+(1-x)\cdot h)^{2}}\bigg)

\displaystyle~=~\frac{2\cdot H\cdot h}{(H+(1-x)\cdot h)^{2}}\hskip 58.6126pt+~H^{2}\cdot\frac{-2\cdot(1-x)\cdot h^{\prime}}{(H+(1-x)\cdot h)^{3}}\phantom{\bigg.}

\displaystyle~=~\frac{2\cdot H(x)\cdot H^{\prime}(x)}{(H(x)+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime}(x))^{2}}\hskip 22.1931pt-~(H(x))^{2}\cdot\frac{2\cdot(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime\prime}(x)}{(H(x)+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime}(x))^{3}}\phantom{\bigg.}

\displaystyle~=~{\rm{RHS}}\text{ of ODE~\eqref{eq:ODE}},\phantom{\bigg.}

where the third step substitutes back
H=H(x)
,
h=H^{\prime}(x)
and
h^{\prime}=H^{\prime\prime}(x)
.

Therefore, ODE (
1
) is just a reformulation of the Euler-Lagrange equation and we immediately conclude the lemma from
Theorem

12
.
∎

In the rest of this subsection, we would resolve ODE (
1
). For ease of notation, we often write
H=H(x)
,
H^{\prime}=H^{\prime}(x)
and
H^{\prime\prime}=H^{\prime\prime}(x)
. Following
Lemma

5.3
, we can deduce that

\displaystyle\text{ODE~\eqref{eq:ODE}}\quad\iff\quad\parbox{95.3169pt}{\hfill$\dfrac{(1-x)\cdot(H^{\prime})^{2}}{(H+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime})^{2}}$}

\displaystyle~=~\frac{2\cdot H\cdot H^{\prime}}{(H+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime})^{2}}~-~\frac{2\cdot(1-x)\cdot H^{2}\cdot H^{\prime\prime}}{(H+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime})^{3}}\phantom{\bigg.}

\displaystyle\iff\quad\parbox{95.3169pt}{\hfill$\dfrac{2\cdot(1-x)\cdot H^{2}\cdot H^{\prime\prime}}{(H+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime})^{3}}$}

\displaystyle~=~\frac{2H\cdot H^{\prime}}{(H+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime})^{2}}~-~\frac{(1-x)\cdot(H^{\prime})^{2}}{(H+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime})^{2}}\phantom{\bigg.}

\displaystyle\iff\quad\parbox{95.3169pt}{\hfill$2\cdot(1-x)\cdot\dfrac{H^{\prime\prime}}{H^{\prime}}$}

\displaystyle~=~\bigg(2H\cdot H^{\prime}-(1-x)\cdot(H^{\prime})^{2}\bigg)\cdot\frac{H+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime}}{H^{2}\cdot H^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\iff\quad\parbox{95.3169pt}{\hfill$2\cdot(1-x)\cdot\dfrac{H^{\prime\prime}}{H^{\prime}}$}

\displaystyle~=~\bigg(2-(1-x)\cdot\frac{H^{\prime}}{H}\bigg)\cdot\bigg(1+(1-x)\cdot\frac{H^{\prime}}{H}\bigg).

(4)

Here the first step restates ODE (
1
). The second step moves the first term to the RHS and moves the third term to the LHS. The third step first multiplies
29
29

29

Because
x\in(0,\,\lambda)
and
\lambda\in(0,\,1)
, this term
\geq\frac{1}{H^{2}\cdot H^{\prime}}\cdot\binom{3}{2}\cdot H^{2}\cdot(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime}=3\cdot(1-x)
is
strictly positive
.
the both hand sides by the same term
\frac{1}{H^{2}\cdot H^{\prime}}\cdot(H+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime})^{3}
and then simplifies the LHS. And the last step rearranges the RHS.

We define the function
z(x):=(1-x)\cdot\frac{H^{\prime}(x)}{H(x)}
and, for brevity, may write
z=z(x)
and
z^{\prime}=z^{\prime}(x)
. We have
z(x)\geq 0
for any
x\in(0,\,\lambda)
, since the supremum bid
\lambda\in(0,\,1)
and the
H
is a
nonnegative increasing
CDF. Therefore, there exists a
nonnegative
subset
\Omega\subseteq[0,\,+\infty]
such that, over the whole support
x\in[0,\,\lambda]
, the CDF
H
can be formulated in term of a
parametric equation

\Big\{\,(x,\,H)=\big(\mathcal{X}(z),\,\mathcal{H}(z)\big)\,\,\Big|\,\Big.\,z\in\Omega\,\Big\}.

Below we would capture the analytic formulas
x=\mathcal{X}(z)
and
H=\mathcal{H}(z)
and then (in the proof of
Lemma

5.4
) study the condition for which the above parametric equation does yield a CDF
H
that is well defined on the support
x\in[0,\,\lambda]
.

Proof of the
x=\mathcal{X}(z)
formula.

We can deduce that

\displaystyle z\cdot H

\displaystyle~=~(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Longleftarrow

definition of
z(x)

\displaystyle z^{\prime}\cdot H~+~z\cdot H^{\prime}

\displaystyle~=~-H^{\prime}~+~(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\Longleftarrow

take derivative

\displaystyle(1-x)\cdot\frac{H^{\prime\prime}}{H^{\prime}}

\displaystyle~=~1~+~z~+~z^{\prime}\cdot\frac{H}{H^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\Longleftarrow

multiply
1\big/H^{\prime}
and rearrange

\displaystyle(1-x)\cdot\frac{H^{\prime\prime}}{H^{\prime}}

\displaystyle~=~1~+~z~+~(1-x)\cdot\frac{z^{\prime}}{z}

\displaystyle\Longleftarrow

substitute
H\big/H^{\prime}=\frac{1-x}{z}
for the RHS

Apply this identity (resp. the definition
z=(1-x)\cdot\frac{H^{\prime}}{H}
) to the LHS (resp. RHS) of ODE (
4
):

\displaystyle\text{ODE~\eqref{eq:solve_ODE:1}}\quad\iff\quad\parbox{123.76965pt}{\hfill$2\cdot\bigg(1+z+(1-x)\cdot\dfrac{z^{\prime}}{z}\bigg)$}

\displaystyle~=~(2-z)\cdot(1+z)

\displaystyle\iff\quad\parbox{123.76965pt}{\hfill$2\cdot(1-x)\cdot\dfrac{z^{\prime}}{z}$}

\displaystyle~=~-z\cdot(1+z)\phantom{\bigg.}

\displaystyle\iff\quad\parbox{123.76965pt}{\hfill$\dfrac{2}{z^{2}\cdot(1+z)}\cdot z^{\prime}$}

\displaystyle~=~\frac{-1}{1-x}.

(5)

Here the second step cancels the common term
(2+2z)
on the both hand sides. And the last step multiplies the both hand sides by the same positive term
\frac{1}{(1-x)\cdot z\cdot(1+z)}
.

We denote
\mu=z(\lambda)
for some parameter
\mu>0
, namely the boundary condition for (
5
) as a
partial differential equation
(PDE). For this PDE, the integration of the LHS on any interval between
z=z(x)
and
\mu=z(\lambda)
, is equal to that of the RHS on the corresponding interval between
x
and
\lambda
. Namely, we have

\displaystyle\text{PDE~\eqref{eq:solve_ODE:2}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\parbox{135.15059pt}{\hfill$\displaystyle{\int_{\mu}^{z}\frac{2}{y^{2}\cdot(1+y)}\cdot\mathrm{d}y}$}

\displaystyle~=~\int_{\lambda}^{x}\frac{-1}{1-y}\cdot\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad\parbox{135.15059pt}{\hfill$\Big(2\ln(1+1/y)-2/y\Big)\,\Big|\,\Big._{y\,=\,\mu}^{z}$}

\displaystyle~=~\ln(1-y)\,\Big|\,\Big._{y\,=\,\lambda}^{x}\phantom{\bigg.}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad\parbox{135.15059pt}{\hfill$\displaystyle{2\ln\bigg(\frac{1+1/z}{1+1/\mu}\bigg)-2/z+2/\mu}$}

\displaystyle~=~\ln(1-x)-\ln(1-\lambda)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad\parbox{135.15059pt}{\phantom{}}

\displaystyle\hskip-135.15059ptx~=~\mathcal{X}(z)~=~1-\frac{1-\lambda}{(1+1/\mu)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/\mu}}\cdot(1+1/z)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/z},\phantom{\bigg.}

(6)

where the last step rearranges the equation. Notably, at
z=\mu
we achieve the supremum bid
\mathcal{X}(\mu)=\lambda
.

Formula (
6
) is precisely the first part
x=\mathcal{X}(z)
of our parametric equation.
∎

Proof of the
H=\mathcal{H}(z)
formula.

To further obtain this formula, we observe that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{H}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}H}{\mathrm{d}x}~=~\frac{H^{\prime}}{H}~=~\frac{z}{1-x}~=~\frac{(1+1/\mu)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/\mu}}{1-\lambda}\cdot\frac{z}{(1+1/z)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/z}},

(7)

where the second step applies the definition
z=(1-x)\cdot\frac{H^{\prime}}{H}
and the last step applies Formula (
6
). Moreover, the derivative of function
\mathcal{X}(z)
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(z)~=~\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{d}z}

\displaystyle~=~-\frac{1-\lambda}{(1+1/\mu)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/\mu}}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}\bigg((1+1/z)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/z}\bigg)

\displaystyle~=~-\frac{1-\lambda}{(1+1/\mu)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/\mu}}\cdot\bigg(2\cdot(1+1/z)\cdot\frac{-1}{z^{2}}\cdot e^{-2/z}+(1+1/z)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/z}\cdot\frac{2}{z^{2}}\bigg)

\displaystyle~=~-\frac{1-\lambda}{(1+1/\mu)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/\mu}}\cdot\frac{2\cdot(1+1/z)\cdot e^{-2/z}}{z^{3}},

(8)

where the first step applies Formula (
6
) and the last step combines the like terms. Notice that this derivative
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(z)
is
strictly negative
, so
\mathcal{X}(z)
is a
strictly decreasing
function.

Combining
Equations

7
and
8
together gives

\displaystyle\big(\ln\mathcal{H}(z)\big)^{\prime}

\displaystyle~=~\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}\big(\ln H\big)~=~\frac{1}{H}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}H}{\mathrm{d}z}~=~\bigg(\frac{1}{H}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}H}{\mathrm{d}x}\bigg)\cdot\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{d}z}\bigg)

\displaystyle~=~\bigg(\frac{z}{(1+1/z)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/z}}\bigg)\cdot\bigg(-\frac{2\cdot(1+1/z)\cdot e^{-2/z}}{z^{3}}\bigg)

\displaystyle~=~\frac{-2}{z\cdot(z+1)}.

(9)

This ODE (
9
) must admit the boundary condition
\mathcal{H}(\mu)=1
, given that (as mentioned) at
z=\mu
we achieve the supremum bid
\mathcal{X}(\mu)=\lambda
. For this reason, taking the integration of the both hand sides on any interval
[\mu,\,z]
gives

\displaystyle\text{ODE~\eqref{eq:solve_ODE:6}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\parbox{86.78099pt}{\hfill$\displaystyle{\int_{\mu}^{z}\big(\ln\mathcal{H}(y)\big)^{\prime}\cdot\mathrm{d}y}$}

\displaystyle~=~\int_{\mu}^{z}\frac{-2}{y\cdot(y+1)}\cdot\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad\parbox{86.78099pt}{\hfill$\ln\mathcal{H}(z)-\ln\mathcal{H}(\mu)$}

\displaystyle~=~\Big(2\ln(1+1/y)\Big)\,\Big|\,\Big._{y\,=\,\mu}^{z}\phantom{\bigg.}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad\parbox{86.78099pt}{\hfill$\ln\mathcal{H}(z)$}

\displaystyle~=~2\ln\bigg(\frac{1+1/z}{1+1/\mu}\bigg)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad\parbox{86.78099pt}{\hfill$H~=~\mathcal{H}(z)$}

\displaystyle~=~\frac{(1+1/z)^{2}}{(1+1/\mu)^{2}}.

(10)

where the third step applies the boundary condition
\mathcal{H}(\mu)=1
to the LHS. Notice that
\mathcal{H}(z)
is a
strictly decreasing
function.

Formula (
10
) is precisely the second part
H=\mathcal{H}(z)
of our parametric equation.
∎

Based on the above discussions, we are able to prove
Lemma

5.4
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.4
.

We first check Condition (
2
) and then capture Implicit Equation (
3
).

Condition (
2
).

As mentioned, both of
\mathcal{X}(z)
and
\mathcal{H}(z)
are
strictly decreasing
functions (
Equations

8
and
10
). Moreover, at
z=\mu
we achieve the supremum bid
\mathcal{X}(\mu)=\lambda
and the boundary condition
\mathcal{H}(\mu)=1
. By considering all possible parameters
z\in[\mu,\,+\infty]
, our parametric equation
\big(\mathcal{X}(z),\,\mathcal{H}(z)\big)
yields a
strictly increasing
function
\overline{H}
with the domain
x\in[\mathcal{X}(+\infty),\,\lambda]
. In this domain, function
\overline{H}
is bounded between
\mathcal{H}(+\infty)=(1+1/\mu)^{-2}\geq 0
and
\mathcal{H}(\mu)=1
.

This function
\overline{H}
must be
consistent
with the considered CDF
H
. In this way, we require that: (i) the support
x\in[0,\,\lambda]
of CDF
H
is a
subset
of the domain
x\in[\mathcal{X}(+\infty),\,\lambda]
of function
\overline{H}
; and, if so, (ii) function
\overline{H}
can be a
well-defined
CDF on the support
x\in[0,\,\lambda]
.

Clearly, the first requirement is equivalent to

0~\geq~\mathcal{X}(+\infty)~=~1-(1-\lambda)\cdot(1+1/\mu)^{-2}\cdot e^{2/\mu},

which after being rearranged gives Condition (
2
). Indeed, the first requirement ensures the second one – function
\overline{H}
is
strictly increasing
and is bounded between
\mathcal{H}(+\infty)=(1+1/\mu)^{-2}\geq 0
and
\mathcal{H}(\mu)=1
in the whole domain
x\in[\mathcal{X}(+\infty),\,\lambda]
, thus (once the first requirement is satisfied) being a well-defined CDF on the support
x\in[0,\,\lambda]\subseteq[\mathcal{X}(+\infty),\,\lambda]
.

Implicit Equation (
3
).

We reformulate our
parametric equation

(x,\,H)=\big(\mathcal{X}(z),\,\mathcal{H}(z)\big)
below, which precisely gives the claimed implicit equation.

\displaystyle\text{Formula~\eqref{eq:solve_ODE:3}}~~\iff~~x

\displaystyle~=~1-\frac{1-\lambda}{(1+1/\mu)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/\mu}}\cdot(1+1/z)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/z}\phantom{\bigg.}

\displaystyle~=~1-\frac{1-\lambda}{(1+1/\mu)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/\mu}}\cdot\Big((1+1/\mu)^{2}\cdot H\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(2-(1+1/\mu)\cdot 2\sqrt{H}\Big)\phantom{\bigg.}

\displaystyle~=~1-(1-\lambda)\cdot H\cdot\exp\Big((1+1/\mu)\cdot\big(2-2\sqrt{H}\big)\Big).\phantom{\bigg.}

Here the first step restates Formula (
6
). The second step (Formula (
10
)) substitutes
(1+1/z)^{2}=(1+1/\mu)^{2}\cdot H
and
-1/z=1-(1+1/\mu)\cdot\sqrt{H}
.
30
30

30

Since the variable
z\in[\mu,\,+\infty]
is positive, we safely ignore the other (impossible) case
-1/z=1+(1+1/\mu)\cdot\sqrt{H}
.

And the last step rearranges the equation.

Combining Condition (
2
) and Implicit Equation (
3
) together finishes the proof.
∎

As an implication of
Lemma

5.4
, by setting
x=0
in Implicit Equation (
3
) we can characterize the pointmass
h_{\mu}:=H_{\mu}(0)
of a candidate worst-case CDF. This is formalized into
Corollary

5.5
. Also, because
Lemma

5.4
holds for any given supremum bid
\lambda\in(0,\,1)
, combining it with
Lemma

5.2
gives
Corollary

5.6
. These two corollaries are more convenient for our later use.

Corollary 5.5

(The ODE solutions)
.

Given a supremum bid
\lambda\in(0,\,1)
and a parameter
\mu>0
that meets Condition (
2
), the CDF
H_{\mu}
defined by Implicit Equation (
3
) has a pointmass
h_{\mu}:=H_{\mu}(0)
at
x=0
, which is the unique solution to the following equation:

\displaystyle\left\{\,~\begin{aligned} &1~=~(1-\lambda)\cdot h_{\mu}\cdot\exp\Big((1+1/\mu)\cdot\big(2-2\sqrt{h_{\mu}}\big)\Big)\\
&\text{such that $(1+1/\mu)^{-2}\leq h_{\mu}<1$}\phantom{\bigg.}\end{aligned}~\,\right\}.

Corollary 5.6

(
Price of Anarchy
)
.

Regarding
First Price Auctions
, the
Price of Anarchy
satisfies that

\displaystyle{\sf PoA}~\geq~1-\sup\left\{\,(1-\lambda)\cdot\int_{0}^{\lambda}\frac{H_{\mu}(x)\cdot H^{\prime}_{\mu}(x)\cdot\mathrm{d}x}{H_{\mu}(x)+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime}_{\mu}(x)}\,\,\middle|\,\,\begin{aligned} &0<\mu<+\infty\\
&0<\lambda\leq 1-(1+1/\mu)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/\mu}\end{aligned}\,\right\}.

Proof.

Applying
Lemma

5.4
to Optimization (
III
) gives an interim optimization that takes the supremum operation
twice
: (i) the inner operation, according to Condition (
2
), works on feasible space
\big\{\,\mu>0\,\,\big|\,\big.\,1-(1+1/\mu)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/\mu}\geq\lambda\,\big\}
given by the
\lambda\in(0,\,1)
; and (ii) the outer operation works on feasible space
\lambda\in(0,\,1)
. Switching the order of both operations gives the corollary.
∎

5.3
Optimizing the parameters
(\lambda,\,\mu)

We solve the optimization in
Corollary

5.6
in two steps: (i)
Lemma

5.7
transforms the considered
integral formula
into an equivalent
analytic formula
, Optimization (
V
); and (ii)
Lemma

5.8
further derives the analytic solution, i.e., finding the (unique) worst case
(\lambda^{*},\,\mu^{*})
of Optimization (
V
).

Lemma 5.7

(
Price of Anarchy
)
.

Regarding
First Price Auctions
, the
Price of Anarchy
satisfies that

\displaystyle{\sf PoA}~\geq~1-\sup\left\{\,(1-\lambda)\cdot\bigg((1-h_{\mu})-\frac{2-2\sqrt{h_{\mu}}}{1+1/\mu}\bigg)\,\,\middle|\,\,\begin{aligned} &0<\mu<+\infty\\
&0<\lambda\leq 1-(1+1/\mu)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/\mu}\\
&\text{$h_{\mu}$ defined by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{cor:pointmass}}\end{aligned}\,\right\}.

(V)

Lemma 5.8

(Worst case)
.

The solution to Optimization (
V
) is
1-1/e^{2}\approx 0.8647
, which can be achieved by the worst case
(\lambda^{*},\,\mu^{*})=(1-4/e^{2},\,1)
and the resulting pointmass
h_{\mu^{*}}=1/4
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.7
.

Following
Corollary

5.6
, given any feasible pair
(\lambda,\,\mu)
, we study the integral

\displaystyle(1-\lambda)\cdot\int_{0}^{\lambda}\frac{H_{\mu}(x)\cdot H^{\prime}_{\mu}(x)\cdot\mathrm{d}x}{H_{\mu}(x)+(1-x)\cdot H^{\prime}_{\mu}(x)}

\displaystyle~=~(1-\lambda)\cdot\int_{x\,\in\,[0,\,\lambda]}\frac{H_{\mu}\cdot\big(\mathrm{d}H_{\mu}\big/\mathrm{d}x\big)\cdot\mathrm{d}x}{H_{\mu}+(1-x)\cdot\big(\mathrm{d}H_{\mu}\big/\mathrm{d}x\big)}

\displaystyle~=~(1-\lambda)\cdot\int_{H_{\mu}\,\in\,[h_{\mu},\,1]}\frac{H_{\mu}\cdot\mathrm{d}H_{\mu}}{H_{\mu}+(1-x)\big/(\mathrm{d}x/\mathrm{d}H_{\mu})},

(11)

where the last step changes the variables. We aim to give an analytic formula for the term
\frac{1-x}{\mathrm{d}x/\mathrm{d}H_{\mu}}
. To this end, let us regard Implicit Equation (
3
) as a function
x(H_{\mu})
of variable
H_{\mu}\in[h_{\mu},\,1]
(i.e., the inverse function of the considered CDF), then the derivative is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{d}H_{\mu}}

\displaystyle~=~-(1-\lambda)\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}H_{\mu}}\bigg(H_{\mu}\cdot\exp\Big((1+1/\mu)\cdot\big(2-2\sqrt{H_{\mu}}\big)\Big)\bigg)

\displaystyle~=~-(1-\lambda)\cdot\bigg(\exp\Big((1+1/\mu)\cdot\big(2-2\sqrt{H_{\mu}}\big)\Big)\Bigg.

\displaystyle\phantom{~=~}\hskip 71.13188pt~+~\bigg.H_{\mu}\cdot\exp\Big((1+1/\mu)\cdot\big(2-2\sqrt{H_{\mu}}\big)\Big)\cdot(1+1/\mu)\cdot\frac{-1}{\sqrt{H_{\mu}}}\bigg)

\displaystyle~=~-(1-\lambda)\cdot\Big(1-(1+1/\mu)\cdot\sqrt{H_{\mu}}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big((1+1/\mu)\cdot\big(2-2\sqrt{H_{\mu}}\big)\Big)\phantom{\bigg.}

Combining this formula with Implicit Equation (
3
) gives

\displaystyle\frac{1-x}{\mathrm{d}x\big/\mathrm{d}H_{\mu}}

\displaystyle~=~\frac{\phantom{-}(1-\lambda)\cdot\parbox{110.39682pt}{\centering$H_{\mu}$\@add@centering}\cdot\exp\Big((1+1/\mu)\cdot\big(2-2\sqrt{H_{\mu}}\big)\Big)}{-(1-\lambda)\cdot\Big(1-(1+1/\mu)\cdot\sqrt{H_{\mu}}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big((1+1/\mu)\cdot\big(2-2\sqrt{H_{\mu}}\big)\Big)}

\displaystyle~=~\frac{H_{\mu}}{(1+1/\mu)\cdot\sqrt{H_{\mu}}-1}.

Plugging this analytic formula for the term
\frac{1-x}{\mathrm{d}x/\mathrm{d}H_{\mu}}
back to Integral (
11
) gives

Integral (
11
)

\displaystyle~=~(1-\lambda)\cdot\int_{H_{\mu}\,\in\,[h_{\mu},\,1]}\frac{H_{\mu}\cdot\mathrm{d}H_{\mu}}{H_{\mu}+\frac{H_{\mu}}{(1+1/\mu)\cdot\sqrt{H_{\mu}}-1}}

\displaystyle~=~(1-\lambda)\cdot\int_{H_{\mu}\in[h_{\mu},\,1]}\bigg(1-\frac{1}{(1+1/\mu)\cdot\sqrt{H_{\mu}}}\bigg)\cdot\mathrm{d}H_{\mu}

\displaystyle~=~(1-\lambda)\cdot\bigg(H_{\mu}-\frac{2\sqrt{H_{\mu}}}{1+1/\mu}\bigg)\,\bigg|\,\bigg._{H_{\mu}\,=\,h_{\mu}}^{1}

\displaystyle~=~(1-\lambda)\cdot\bigg((1-h_{\mu})-\frac{2-2\sqrt{h_{\mu}}}{1+1/\mu}\bigg),

which is precisely the formula in Optimization (
V
). This finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Optimization (
V
) involves just three variables and, indeed, one of them
h_{\mu}
is even determined by the other two
(\lambda,\,\mu)
.
Lemma

5.8
gives the analytic solution to Optimization (
V
).

Proof of
Lemma

5.8
.

For the moment, we regard the
\mu>0
as a given parameter. It follows from
Corollary

5.5
that
\lambda=G(h_{\mu})
, where the function
G
is defined in the domain
h_{\mu}\in\big[(1+1/\mu)^{-2},\,1\big)
:

\displaystyle G(h_{\mu})~:=~1-(1/h_{\mu})\cdot\exp\Big(-\big(2-2\sqrt{h_{\mu}}\big)\cdot(1+1/\mu)\Big).

This
G(h_{\mu})
is a continuous function. And for any
h_{\mu}>(1+1/\mu)^{-2}
, its derivative

\displaystyle G^{\prime}(h_{\mu})

\displaystyle~=~(1/h_{\mu}^{2})\cdot\exp\Big(-\big(2-2\sqrt{h_{\mu}}\big)\cdot(1+1/\mu)\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt~-~(1/h_{\mu})\cdot\exp\Big(-\big(2-2\sqrt{h_{\mu}}\big)\cdot(1+1/\mu)\Big)\cdot\Big((1+1/\mu)\big/\sqrt{h_{\mu}}\Big)

\displaystyle~=~(1/h_{\mu}^{2})\cdot\exp\Big(-\big(2-2\sqrt{h_{\mu}}\big)\cdot(1+1/\mu)\Big)\cdot\Big(1-(1+1/\mu)\cdot\sqrt{h_{\mu}}\Big)

is
strictly positive
. Thus, this function
G(h_{\mu})
is
strictly increasing
in the whole
left-closed right-open
domain
h_{\mu}\in\big[(1+1/\mu)^{-2},\,1\big)
. Moreover, the image of function
G(h_{\mu})
,

\Big\{\,G(h_{\mu})\,\,\Big|\,\Big.\,(1+1/\mu)^{-2}\leq h_{\mu}<1\,\Big\}~=~\big(0,\,1-(1+1/\mu)^{2}\cdot e^{-2/\mu}\big]

is precisely the feasible space of
\lambda
in Optimization (
V
), which means the mapping between all feasible
\lambda
and all
h_{\mu}\in\big[(1+1/\mu)^{-2},\,1\big)
is
one-to-one
. For these reasons, we can deduce that

Optimization (
V
)

\displaystyle~=~1-\sup\left\{\,\big(1-G(h_{\mu})\big)\cdot\bigg((1-h_{\mu})-\frac{2-2\sqrt{h_{\mu}}}{1+1/\mu}\bigg)\phantom{\frac{}{\Big.}}\,\middle|\,\,\begin{aligned} &0<\mu<+\infty\\
&(1+1/\mu)^{-2}\leq h_{\mu}<1\end{aligned}\,\right\}

\displaystyle~=~1-\sup\left\{\,\frac{(1-h_{\mu})-\big(2-2\sqrt{h_{\mu}}\big)\cdot(1+1/\mu)^{-1}}{h_{\mu}\cdot\exp\Big(\big(2-2\sqrt{h_{\mu}}\big)\cdot(1+1/\mu)\Big)}\,\,\middle|\,\,\begin{aligned} &0<\mu<+\infty\\
&(1+1/\mu)^{-2}\leq h_{\mu}<1\end{aligned}\,\right\}

\displaystyle~=~1-\sup\left\{\,\mathcal{G}(\beta,\,\gamma):=\frac{(1-\beta^{2})-(2-2\beta)\cdot\gamma}{\beta^{2}\cdot\exp\big((2-2\beta)\cdot\gamma^{-1}\big)}\phantom{\frac{}{\Big.}}\,\middle|\,\,\text{$0<\gamma\leq\beta<1$}\,\right\},

where the first step switches the optimization variable from
\lambda
to
h_{\mu}\in\big[(1+1/\mu)^{-2},\,1\big)
, the second step plugs the
G(h_{\mu})
formula, and the last step substitutes
\beta:=\sqrt{h_{\mu}}
and
\gamma:=(1+1/\mu)^{-1}
.

Below we would reason about the function
\mathcal{G}(\beta,\,\gamma)
defined above. The partial derivative of this function with respect to variable
\gamma
is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}}{\partial\gamma}

\displaystyle~=~\frac{-(2-2\beta)}{\beta^{2}\cdot\exp\big((2-2\beta)\cdot\gamma^{-1}\big)}~+~\frac{(1-\beta^{2})-(2-2\beta)\cdot\gamma}{\beta^{2}\cdot\exp\big((2-2\beta)\cdot\gamma^{-1}\big)}\cdot(2-2\beta)\cdot\gamma^{-2}

\displaystyle~=~\frac{(1-\beta^{2})-(2-2\beta)\cdot\gamma-\gamma^{2}}{\beta^{2}\cdot\exp\big((2-2\beta)\cdot\gamma^{-1}\big)}\cdot(2-2\beta)\cdot\gamma^{-2},

where the last step rearranges the formula. Clearly, in the feasible space
\{0<\gamma\leq\beta<1\}
, the sign of this partial derivative
\partial\mathcal{G}\big/\partial\gamma
depends on the numerator
(1-\beta^{2})-2\gamma\cdot(1-\beta)-\gamma^{2}
.

Figure 14:
Diagram of (the red curve) the
\mathcal{G}(\beta,\,\beta)
as an increasing function of
\beta\in(0,\,1/2]
and (the blue curve) the
\mathcal{G}(\beta,\,\gamma_{\beta})
as a decreasing function of
\beta\in(1/2,\,1)
.

For the moment, we regard
\beta\in(0,\,1)
as a given parameter and consider the parabola

P_{\beta}(\gamma)~:=~(1-\beta^{2})-2\gamma\cdot(1-\beta)-\gamma^{2}

in the domain
\gamma\in[0,\,\beta]
. This parabola opens
downwards
and has a
nonpositive
axis of symmetry
\gamma=-(1-\beta)
, thus being a
decreasing
function in the whole domain
\gamma\in[0,\,\beta]
. Also, the left/right ends are respectively
P_{\beta}(0)=1-\beta^{2}>0
and
P_{\beta}(\beta)=1-2\beta
. Let us do case analysis.

Case I that
0<\beta\leq 1/2
.

Now the both ends
P_{\beta}(0)
and
P_{\beta}(\beta)
, as well as the whole parabola, are
nonnegative
, which implies that
\partial\mathcal{G}\big/\partial\gamma\geq 0
for any
\gamma\in(0,\,\beta]
. Accordingly, we have

\displaystyle\sup\Big\{\,\mathcal{G}(\beta,\,\gamma)\,\,\Big|\,\Big.\,\text{\bf Case~I}\,\Big\}

\displaystyle~=~\sup\Big\{\,\mathcal{G}(\beta,\,\beta)\,\,\Big|\,\Big.\,\text{$0<\beta\leq 1/2$}\,\Big\}

\displaystyle~=~\sup\Big\{\,(1/\beta-1)^{2}\cdot e^{-(2/\beta-2)}\,\,\Big|\,\Big.\,\text{$0<\beta\leq 1/2$}\,\Big\}

\displaystyle~=~1/e^{2},\phantom{\Big.}

where the last two steps can be checked through elementary algebra. In particular, the
\mathcal{G}(\beta,\,\beta)
as a function of
\beta\in(0,\,1/2]
is increasing (see
Figure

14
for a visual aid), and the supremum
1/e^{2}\approx 0.1353
is achieved when
\beta=\gamma=1/2
.

Case II that
1/2<\beta<1
.

Now the left end
P_{\beta}(0)=1-\beta^{2}
is
positive
, the right end
P_{\beta}(\beta)=1-2\beta
is
negative
, and (elementary algebra) the parabola has exactly one root
\gamma_{\beta}:=\sqrt{(2-2\beta)}-(1-\beta)
in its domain. This implies that
\partial\mathcal{G}\big/\partial\gamma\geq 0
for any
\gamma\in[0,\,\gamma_{\beta}]
and
\partial\mathcal{G}\big/\partial\gamma\leq 0
for any
\gamma\in[\gamma_{\beta},\,\beta]
. As a consequence, we have

\displaystyle\sup\Big\{\,\mathcal{G}(\beta,\,\gamma)\,\,\Big|\,\Big.\,\text{\bf Case~II}\,\Big\}

\displaystyle~=~\sup\Big\{\,\mathcal{G}(\beta,\,\gamma_{\beta})\,\,\Big|\,\Big.\,\text{$1/2<\beta<1$}\,\Big\}

\displaystyle~=~\sup\Big\{\,\Big(\tfrac{\sqrt{2}-\sqrt{1-\beta}}{\beta/\sqrt{1-\beta}}\Big)^{2}\cdot\exp\Big(\tfrac{-2\sqrt{1-\beta}}{\sqrt{2}-\sqrt{1-\beta}}\Big)\,\,\Big|\,\Big.\,\text{$1/2<\beta<1$}\,\Big\}

\displaystyle~=~1/e^{2},\phantom{\Big.}

where the last two steps can be checked through elementary algebra. In particular, the
\mathcal{G}(\beta,\,\gamma_{\beta})
as a function of
\beta\in(1/2,\,1)
is decreasing (see
Figure

14
for a visual aid), and the supremum
1/e^{2}\approx 0.1353
is approached (but cannot be achieved) when
\gamma=\gamma_{\beta}
and
\beta\searrow 1/2
.

Combining both cases together gives
\sup\{\mathcal{G}(\beta,\,\gamma)\,|\,0<\gamma\leq\beta<1\}=1/e^{2}
and thus

\displaystyle\text{Optimization~\eqref{eq:UB_lambda_mu}}~=~1-\sup\Big\{\,\mathcal{G}(\beta,\,\gamma)\,\,\Big|\,\Big.\,0<\gamma\leq\beta<1\,\Big\}~=~1-1/e^{2}.

In particular, the worst case can be achieved when
\beta=\gamma=1/2
, or equivalently, when the supremum bid
\lambda=1-4/e^{2}\approx 0.4587
, the parameter
\mu=1
and the pointmass
h_{\mu}=1/4
.

This finishes the proof of
Lemma

5.8
.
∎

Lemmas

5.7
and
5.8
together imply the lower-bound part of
Theorem

1
, which is restated below.

Theorem

1
(Restated).

The
Price of Anarchy
in
First Price Auctions
is
\geq 1-1/e^{2}\approx 0.8647
.

6
Upper Bound Analysis

In this section, we first (
Example

4
) present our worst case instances in terms of
bid distributions
; then (
Lemma

6.1
) check the validity, i.e., the corresponding
value distributions
are well defined; and then (
Lemma

6.2
) evaluate the auction/optimal
Social Welfares
from this instance. The upper-bound part of
Theorem

1
will be a direct consequence of
Lemmas

6.1
and
6.2
.

The idea is to simulate the worst-case pseudo instance
H^{*}\otimes L^{*}
from
Section

5
,
using a number of i.i.d. low-impact bidders
\{L\}^{\otimes n}
to approximate the pseudo bidder
L^{*}
(
Remark

8
): Each individual low-impact bidder
L
is likely to have a small bid
\approx 0
, but the highest bid from
\{L\}^{\otimes n}
is (almost) identically distributed as the worst-case pseudo bidder
L^{*}
.

Example 4

(Worst-case instances)
.

Let
\lambda^{*}=1-4/e^{2}
and
L^{*}(b)=\frac{1-\lambda^{*}}{1-b}
for any
b\in[0,\,\lambda^{*}]
be a CDF function. Another CDF function
H^{*}(b)
is given by the implicit equation

\displaystyle\left\{\,(b,\,H^{*})\,\,\middle|\,\,\begin{aligned} &b~=~1-4H^{*}\cdot\exp\Big(2-4\sqrt{H^{*}}\Big)\\
&\text{such that $0\leq b\leq\lambda^{*}$ and $1/4\leq H^{*}\leq 1$}\phantom{\bigg.}\end{aligned}\,\right\}.

(12)

For an arbitrarily small constant
\varepsilon\in(0,\,1)
, consider the following
(n+1)
-bidder instance
H\otimes\{L\}^{\otimes n}
for
n:=\lceil 3/\varepsilon\rceil\geq 4
:

•

H(b):=H^{*}(b)
for
b\in[0,\,\lambda^{*}]
denotes the monopolist.

•

L(b):=\big(L^{*}(b)\big)^{\frac{1}{n-1}}
for
b\in[0,\,\lambda^{*}]
denotes the
common
bid distribution of
n\geq 4
many i.i.d.
low-impact
bidders.

Here we choose
L(b):=\big(L^{*}(b)\big)^{\frac{1}{n-1}}
rather than
\big(L^{*}(b)\big)^{\frac{1}{n}}
such that each lower-impact bidder
L
has the competing bid distribution
H(b)\cdot\big(L(b)\big)^{n-1}=H^{*}(b)\cdot L^{*}(b)
, exactly the same as the pseudo bidder
L^{*}
. But in this way, the monopolist
H
’s competing bid
\big(L(b)\big)^{n}=\big(L^{*}(b)\big)^{\frac{n}{n-1}}
slightly differs from the
L^{*}(b)
,
31
31

31

The other choice
L(b):=\big(L^{*}(b)\big)^{\frac{1}{n}}
is also fine, but the analysis will be more complicated.

so his/her value is not a constant
\equiv 1
but a random variable
\approx 1
spanning in a tiny range.
These are all formalized in
Lemma

6.1
, which checks the validity of this
(n+1)
-bidder instance; see
Figure

15
for a visual aid.

Lemma 6.1

(Validity)
.

Given an arbitrarily small constant
\varepsilon\in(0,\,1)
, the following hold for the
(n+1)
-bidder instance
H\otimes\{L\}^{\otimes n}
in
Example

4
:

1.

The monopolist
H
has an increasing bid-to-value mapping
\varphi_{H}(b):=1-\frac{1-b}{n}
for
b\in[0,\,\lambda^{*}]
and a well-defined value distribution
V_{H}(v)
given by the implicit equation

\displaystyle\left\{\,(v,\,V_{H})\,\,\middle|\,\,\begin{aligned} &v~=~1-\tfrac{1}{n}\cdot 4V_{H}\cdot\exp\Big(2-4\sqrt{V_{H}}\Big)\\
&\text{such that $1-\tfrac{1}{n}\leq v\leq 1-\tfrac{4/e^{2}}{n}$ and $1/4\leq V_{H}\leq 1$}\phantom{\bigg.}\end{aligned}\,\right\}.

The strategy
s_{H}
as the inverse of
\varphi_{H}(b)
is given by
s_{H}(v)=1-n\cdot(1-v)
.

2.

Each low-impact bidder
L
has an increasing bid-to-value mapping
\varphi_{L}(b)
given by the parametric equation

\displaystyle\Big\{\,(b,\,\varphi_{L})=\big(1-t^{2}\cdot e^{2-2t},\quad 1-t\cdot e^{2-2t}\big)\,\,\Big|\,\Big.\,1\leq t\leq 2\,\Big\}

and a well-defined value distribution
V_{L}(v)
given by the parametric equation

\displaystyle\Big\{\,(v,\,V_{L})=\big(1-t\cdot e^{2-2t},\quad\big(4/t^{2}\cdot e^{2t-4}\big)^{\frac{1}{n-1}}\big)\,\,\Big|\,\Big.\,1\leq t\leq 2\,\Big\}.

The strategy
s_{L}
as the inverse of
\varphi_{L}(b)
is given by the parametric equation
s_{L}(1-t\cdot e^{2-2t})=1-t^{2}\cdot e^{2-2t}
for
1\leq t\leq 2
.

3.

The strategy profile
s_{H}\otimes\{s_{L}\}^{\otimes n}
forms an equilibrium
s_{H}\otimes\{s_{L}\}^{\otimes n}\in\mathbb{BNE}(V_{H}\otimes\{V_{L}\}^{\otimes n})
.

Figure 15:
Diagram of the
(n+1)
-bidder instance
H\otimes\{L\}^{\otimes n}
in
Example

4
.

(i) The monopolist
H
has a bid CDF
H
(orange) given by Implicit Equation (
12
) and a value CDF
V_{H}
(red) given by
Item

1
of
Lemma

6.1
.

(ii) Each of the i.i.d. low-impact bidders
\{L\}^{\otimes n}
has a bid CDF
L
(green) given by
L(b)=(\frac{1-\lambda^{*}}{1-b})^{\frac{1}{n-1}}
for any
b\in[0,\,\lambda^{*}]=[0,\,1-4/e^{2}]
and a value CDF
V_{L}
(blue) given by
Item

2
of
Lemma

6.1
.

Proof.

Let us prove
Items

1
and
2
one by one. Then
Item

3
will be a direct consequence.

Item

1
.

The monopolist
H
has a competing bid distribution
L(b)^{n}=L^{*}(b)^{\frac{n}{n-1}}=(\frac{1-\lambda^{*}}{1-b})^{\frac{n}{n-1}}
and thus (by elementary algebra) a bid-to-value mapping

\displaystyle\varphi_{H}(b)~=~b+L(b)^{n}\big/\big(L(b)^{n}\big)^{\prime}~=~b+\tfrac{n-1}{n}\cdot(1-b)~=~1-\tfrac{1-b}{n}.

This mapping
\varphi_{H}(b)
is increasing on
b\in[0,\,\lambda^{*}]=[0,\,1-4/e^{2}]
, with the minimum
\varphi_{H}(0)=1-\frac{1}{n}
and the maximum
\varphi_{H}(\lambda^{*})=1-\frac{1-\lambda^{*}}{n}=1-\frac{4/e^{2}}{n}
.

According to
Lemma

2.18
, the value distribution
V_{H}(v)
follows the parametric equation

\displaystyle\Big\{\,(v,\,V_{H})=\big(\varphi_{H}(b),\,H(b)\big)\,\,\Big|\,\Big.\,b\in[0,\,\lambda^{*}]\,\Big\},

where the formula
V_{H}=H(b)=H^{*}(b)
is defined by Implicit Equation (
12
). Based on elementary algebra (i.e., using the substitution
b=1-n\cdot(1-v)
due to the formula
v=\varphi_{H}(b)=1-\frac{1-b}{n}
), we obtain the claimed implicit equation for
V_{H}(v)
.
Item

1
follows then.

Item

2
.

For notational brevity, below we may simply write
H^{*}=H^{*}(b)
and
L^{*}=L^{*}(b)
etc.
We let
t:=2\sqrt{H^{*}}\in[1,\,2]
as a function of
b
. Then

b=1-t^{2}\cdot\exp(2-2t)
by
Equation

12
and

\tfrac{\mathrm{d}b}{\mathrm{d}t}~=~(2t^{2}-2t)\cdot\exp(2-2t).

(13)

The low-impact bidders
L^{\otimes n}
have the same competing bid distribution
B(b)=H(b)\cdot\big(L(b)\big)^{n-1}=H^{*}(b)\cdot L^{*}(b)
. This distribution can be written as

B~=~H^{*}\cdot L^{*}~=~H^{*}\cdot\tfrac{4/e^{2}}{1-b}~=~\exp\big(-4+2t\big),

where
the last step applies
H^{*}=t^{2}/4
and
b=1-t^{2}\cdot\exp(2-2t)
. The above formula switches the range
t\in[1,\,2]
into the range
B\in[1/e^{2},\,1]
.
Thus

\displaystyle\varphi_{L}

\displaystyle~=~b+B\big/(\tfrac{\mathrm{d}B}{\mathrm{d}b})

\displaystyle~=~b+\exp\big(-4+2t\big)\cdot(\tfrac{\mathrm{d}b}{\mathrm{d}t})\big/(\tfrac{\mathrm{d}B}{\mathrm{d}t})

\displaystyle~=~1-t^{2}\cdot\exp(2-2t)+(t^{2}-t)\cdot\exp(2-2t)

\displaystyle~=~1-t\cdot e^{2-2t}.

We conclude with the parametric equation for
\varphi_{L}(b)
claimed in the statement of
Item

2
:

\displaystyle\Big\{\,(b,\,\varphi_{L})=\big(1-t^{2}\cdot e^{2-2t},\quad 1-t\cdot e^{2-2t}\big)\,\,\Big|\,\Big.\,1\leq t\leq 2\,\Big\}

With respect to
t\in[1,\,2]
, the both formulas
b=1-t^{2}\cdot e^{2-2t}
and
\varphi_{L}=1-t\cdot e^{2-2t}
are increasing functions. Thus, the bid-to-value mapping
\varphi_{L}(b)
is increasing for
b\in[0,\,1-4/e^{2}]=[0,\,\lambda^{*}]
, with the minimum
\varphi_{L}(0)=0
and the maximum
\varphi_{L}(\lambda^{*})=1-2/e^{2}
.

According to
Lemma

2.18
, the value distribution
V_{L}(v)
follows the parametric equation

\displaystyle\Big\{\,(v,\,V_{L})=\big(\varphi_{L}(b),\,L(b)\big)\,\,\Big|\,\Big.\,b\in[0,\,\lambda^{*}]\,\Big\},

where the formula
V_{L}=L(b)=(\frac{1-\lambda^{*}}{1-b})^{\frac{1}{n-1}}=(\frac{4/e^{2}}{1-b})^{\frac{1}{n-1}}
. By elementary algebra (i.e., employing the formula
b=1-t^{2}\cdot e^{2-2t}
in the defining parametric equation for
\varphi_{L}(b)
), we derive the claimed parametric equation for
V_{H}(v)
.
Item

2
follows then. This finishes the proof.
∎

Now we study the auction/optimal
Social Welfares
from our
(n+1)
-bidder instance
H\otimes\{L\}^{\otimes n}
.

Lemma 6.2

(
Price of Anarchy
)
.

Given an arbitrarily small constant
\varepsilon\in(0,\,1)
, the following hold for the
(n+1)
-bidder instance
H\otimes\{L\}^{\otimes n}
in
Example

4
:

1.

The expected optimal
Social Welfare

{\sf OPT}(H\otimes\{L\}^{\otimes n})\geq 1-\varepsilon
; and

2.

The expected auction
Social Welfare

{\sf FPA}(H\otimes\{L\}^{\otimes n})\leq 1-1/e^{2}
.

Proof.

Let us prove
Items

1
and
2
one by one.

Item

1
.

The value
v_{H}\sim V_{H}
of the monopolist
H
, supported on
\mathrm{supp}(V_{H})=\big[1-\frac{1}{n},\,1-\frac{4/e^{2}}{n}\big]
, is higher than the values of the lower-impact bidders
\{L\}^{\otimes n}
, which are supported on

[0,1-2/e^{2}]
. Thus the optimal
Social Welfare

{\sf OPT}(H\otimes\{L\}^{\otimes n})
always stems from the monopolist
H
:

{\sf OPT}(H\otimes\{L\}^{\otimes n})~=~\operatorname{{\bf E}}_{v_{H}}\big[v_{H}\big]~\geq~1-1\big/n~\geq~1-\varepsilon,\hskip 169.29381pt

where the last step uses
n=\lceil 3/\varepsilon\rceil\geq 1/\varepsilon
(
Example

4
).
Item

1
follows then.

Item

2
.

Recall that the low-impact bidders
L^{\otimes n}
have the common bid-to-value mapping
\varphi_{L}(b)
.
Following
Lemma

2.20
(with
\mathcal{B}(b)=H(b)\cdot L(b)^{n}
),
the auction
Social Welfare

{\sf FPA}(H\otimes\{L\}^{\otimes n})
from our
(n+1)
-bidder instance is given by

\displaystyle{\sf FPA}(H\otimes\{L\}^{\otimes n})

\displaystyle~=~\varphi_{H}(0)\cdot\mathcal{B}(0)+\int_{0}^{\lambda^{*}}\Big(\varphi_{H}(b)\cdot\tfrac{H^{\prime}(b)}{H(b)}\cdot\mathcal{B}(0)+n\cdot\varphi_{L}(b)\cdot\tfrac{L^{\prime}(b)}{L(b)}\cdot\mathcal{B}(b)\Big)\cdot\mathrm{d}b

\displaystyle~\leq~\mathcal{B}(0)+\int_{0}^{\lambda^{*}}\Big(\tfrac{H^{\prime}(b)}{H(b)}\cdot\mathcal{B}(0)+n\cdot\varphi_{L}(b)\cdot\tfrac{L^{\prime}(b)}{L(b)}\cdot\mathcal{B}(b)\Big)\cdot\mathrm{d}b

\displaystyle~=~\mathcal{B}(0)+\int_{0}^{\lambda^{*}}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b-\int_{0}^{\lambda^{*}}n\cdot(1-\varphi_{L}(b))\cdot\tfrac{L^{\prime}(b)}{L(b)}\cdot\mathcal{B}(b)\cdot\mathrm{d}b

\displaystyle~=~1-\int_{0}^{\lambda^{*}}n\cdot(1-\varphi_{L}(b))\cdot\tfrac{1}{(n-1)\cdot(1-b)}\cdot H(b)\cdot\big(\tfrac{1-\lambda^{*}}{1-b}\big)^{\frac{n}{n-1}}\cdot\mathrm{d}b

\displaystyle~\leq~1-\tfrac{n}{n-1}\cdot(1-\lambda^{*})^{\frac{n}{n-1}}\cdot\int_{0}^{\lambda^{*}}\frac{(1-\varphi_{L}(b))\cdot H(b)}{(1-b)^{2}}\cdot\mathrm{d}b

\displaystyle~=~1-\tfrac{n}{n-1}\cdot(1-\lambda^{*})^{\frac{n}{n-1}}\cdot\int_{1}^{2}\frac{(t\cdot e^{2-2t})\cdot(t^{2}/4)}{(t^{2}\cdot e^{2-2t})^{2}}\cdot\Big(\frac{\mathrm{d}b}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big)\cdot\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle~=~1-\tfrac{n}{n-1}\cdot(1-\lambda^{*})^{\frac{n}{n-1}}\cdot\int_{1}^{2}\frac{t-1}{2}\cdot\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle~=~1-\tfrac{n}{n-1}\cdot(4/e^{2})^{\frac{1}{n-1}}\cdot e^{-2}\phantom{\bigg.}

\displaystyle~\leq~1-e^{-2},

where the last inequality uses the fact
\frac{n}{n-1}\cdot(4/e^{2})^{\frac{1}{n-1}}=\big((1+\frac{1}{n-1})^{n-1}\cdot 4/e^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{n-1}}\geq(8/e^{2})^{\frac{1}{n-1}}>1
. The definition of
t\in[1,\,2]
and the expressions of
b
,
H
,
\varphi_{L}
and
\frac{\mathrm{d}b}{\mathrm{d}t}
in terms of
t
are given in the proof of
Lemma

6.2
.

Item

2
follows then.
This finishes the proof.
∎

We directly infer the upper-bound part of
Theorem

1
(restated below) from
Lemma

6.2
, since the constant
\varepsilon\in(0,\,1)
chosen in
Example

4
can be arbitrarily small.

Theorem

1
(Restated).

The
Price of Anarchy
in
First Price Auctions
is
\leq 1-1/e^{2}\approx 0.8647
.
